**Stem.** A 29-year-old woman admitted for a manic episode is started on risperidone. Two days later she is restless, pacing the hallway, unable to sit through meals, and says she 'can't stop moving.' She denies worsening mood symptoms. Which of the following best explains her presentation?

- Worsening mixed mania with motor agitation — increase the risperidone
- New-onset generalized anxiety disorder unmasked by hospitalization stress
- Restless legs syndrome — the urge to move is confined to the lower extremities at rest
- Akathisia — medication-induced inner restlessness from dopamine blockade that worsens with more antipsychotic **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic is the dangerous error: escalating the dopamine blocker intensifies akathisia. Missing the iatrogenic cause leads to a wrong new diagnosis. RLS vs akathisia distinguishes them by distribution and provocation — RLS is limb-specific and rest-provoked; akathisia is global, medication-timed, and worsens with increased dosing.

**Pearl.** Akathisia is the classic inpatient miss — inner restlessness from antipsychotics that worsens with more dopamine blockade, not less.

**Stem.** A 28-year-old woman admitted for a severe depressive episode is also found to have untreated PTSD — she endorses daily nightmares, hypervigilance, and avoidance behaviors that have persisted for two years since an assault. She has no history of substance use disorder. After addressing the acute safety concern, which is the most appropriate first-line pharmacotherapy for her PTSD?

- Start scheduled lorazepam — benzodiazepines reduce hyperarousal and improve sleep, targeting PTSD's core neurobiological substrate
- Start an SSRI or SNRI, counsel her about delayed onset, and plan trauma-focused therapy (prolonged exposure or CPT) as a discharge goal **← keyed correct**
- Start propranolol — beta blockade prevents autonomic consolidation of traumatic memories and is first-line for established PTSD
- Start an antipsychotic for the intrusive re-experiencing — flashback-like episodes indicate psychotic features requiring dopamine blockade

**Rationale.** The reflexive benzodiazepine is the most common trap — standing benzos for PTSD create dependence without treating the disorder and are explicitly avoided on the inpatient unit. SSRIs and SNRIs are first-line across all anxiety disorders and PTSD; definitive treatment pairs medication with trauma-focused therapy (prolonged exposure or CPT) planned at discharge. Propranolol is studied in acute peritraumatic settings, not as first-line pharmacotherapy for established PTSD. Antipsychotics conflate re-experiencing with psychosis.

**Pearl.** SSRIs/SNRIs are first-line for PTSD — benzodiazepines are a liability on the unit; pair medication with trauma-focused therapy (PE or CPT) at discharge.

**Stem.** A 29-year-old man with first-episode psychosis was started on haloperidol 3 days ago. He has been pacing the unit continuously, reports an inner urge to move, and the team has correctly identified this as akathisia. The attending decides that haloperidol should be continued for now at the same dose. Which pharmacological intervention has the strongest evidence as first-line treatment for akathisia in this setting?

- Benztropine — anticholinergic agents are first-line for all antipsychotic-induced movement disorders, including akathisia.
- Propranolol 20–40 mg BID — a non-selective beta-blocker with the strongest evidence base for akathisia from multiple small RCTs and Cochrane review. **← keyed correct**
- Immediate switch to clozapine — clozapine's low D2 affinity eliminates akathisia risk and is the definitive treatment when any antipsychotic causes akathisia.
- Standing diazepam — benzodiazepines are the first-line pharmacological agent for dopamine-blocker-induced restlessness.

**Rationale.** Anticholinergic first-line, Clozapine immediately, and Benzodiazepine first-line all misorient the management hierarchy. Propranolol (B) has the strongest evidence for akathisia: it is effective in multiple small RCTs reviewed in the Cochrane systematic analysis (Lima et al. 2004). The management sequence is: (1) reduce antipsychotic dose or switch if possible — the mechanism fix; (2) propranolol when dose reduction isn't immediately available; (3) benztropine if parkinsonism co-exists; (4) benzodiazepine adjunct if symptoms remain severe. Avoid escalating the antipsychotic — the cardinal error.

**Pearl.** Akathisia pharmacotherapy: propranolol 20–40 mg BID is first-line (strongest evidence); add benztropine if parkinsonism co-exists; benzodiazepine is third-line adjunct.

**Stem.** A 52-year-old man is admitted to the inpatient psychiatry unit. He is anxious, diaphoretic, and tremulous. His heart rate is 102. He says he has always been 'an anxious person.' The team prepares to increase his home sertraline. Which step is most important before attributing this presentation to his anxiety disorder?

- Obtain a psychiatric consultation — his elevated heart rate may indicate a panic attack requiring specialist evaluation.
- Screen for medical and withdrawal causes first — diaphoresis, tachycardia, and tremor require workup for hyperthyroidism, withdrawal states, arrhythmia, and hypoglycemia before attributing symptoms to primary anxiety. **← keyed correct**
- Increase the sertraline — the anxiety disorder history provides the most parsimonious explanation.
- Obtain an ECG and troponin immediately — the anxiety symptoms likely represent cardiac ischemia.

**Rationale.** Specialist before differential, Anchoring to prior diagnosis, and Single diagnosis before differential all bypass the foundational step. The page's first pearl: 'Anxiety is a symptom before it is a diagnosis — clear the medical and withdrawal mimics first.' In a newly admitted patient with diaphoresis, tremor, and tachycardia, alcohol or sedative withdrawal must top the differential.

**Pearl.** Anxiety is a symptom before it is a diagnosis — clear medical and withdrawal mimics (thyroid, withdrawal, arrhythmia, hypoglycemia) before treating the disorder.

**Stem.** A 44-year-old woman with panic disorder and alcohol use disorder in early recovery is admitted for assessment. She is anxious and requesting 'something to help right away.' The student considers adding a standing lorazepam dose for anxiety management. Which response reflects the inpatient standard?

- Start lorazepam 0.5 mg twice daily and titrate to response — benzodiazepines are first-line for inpatient anxiety.
- Give a single dose of lorazepam now and plan to stop it in 24 hours — brief PRN use avoids dependence.
- Avoid standing benzodiazepines on the inpatient unit — they drive dependence, falls, and delirium, and are especially hazardous with comorbid SUD; use skills-based approaches and SSRI/SNRI planning instead. **← keyed correct**
- Benzodiazepines are acceptable for a few days in any patient — dependence only develops with chronic outpatient use.

**Rationale.** Benzos for anxiety disorder, One dose is safe, and Inpatient benzos safe short-term all contradict the page's explicit guidance. The page states: 'Avoid standing benzodiazepines on the unit — they drive dependence, falls, and delirium, and are especially hazardous with comorbid SUD or in older adults.' This patient has both risk factors.

**Pearl.** Standing benzodiazepines are a liability on the inpatient unit — dependence, falls, delirium; especially avoid with SUD or in older adults; use SSRIs + behavioral skills instead.

**Stem.** A 32-year-old man with OCD spends 4–6 hours daily performing handwashing and checking rituals that he recognizes as excessive. He asks what the most effective treatment for his OCD is. Which response is most accurate?

- Cognitive restructuring — identifying and challenging the distorted beliefs underlying the OCD cognitions is the first-line behavioral approach.
- Prolonged exposure — reliving the feared OCD content in an imaginal narrative is the evidence-based treatment for OCD.
- Mindfulness-based stress reduction — cultivating non-judgmental awareness of obsessional thoughts reduces their power over the patient's behavior.
- Exposure and response prevention (ERP) — gradually exposing the patient to feared triggers while preventing the compulsive ritual, breaking the anxiety-reduction cycle that maintains OCD. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Cognitive restructuring over ERP, ERP confused with PE, and Mindfulness over ERP all substitute adjacent techniques for the specific evidence-based intervention. The page states: 'Definitive treatment is behavioral — ERP for OCD.' The mechanism of ERP (exposure + ritual prevention breaks the anxiety-reduction cycle) is what distinguishes it from general cognitive or mindfulness work.

**Pearl.** First-line behavioral treatment for OCD is ERP (exposure and response prevention) — not PE (which is PTSD), not generic CBT, not mindfulness alone.

- Linked pages: `t_anxiety.md`
- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `a`, `n`, `x`, `i`, `e`, `t`, `y`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `O`, `C`, `D`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `n`, ` `, `m`, `a`, `s`, `q`, `u`, `e`, `r`, `a`, `d`, `e`, ` `, `a`, `s`, ` `, `p`, `s`, `y`, `c`, `h`, `o`, `s`, `i`, `s`, ` `, `w`, `h`, `e`, `n`, ` `, `r`, `i`, `t`, `u`, `a`, `l`, `s`, ` `, `a`, `r`, `e`, ` `, `e`, `l`, `a`, `b`, `o`, `r`, `a`, `t`, `e`, `,`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `a`, `s`, ` `, `t`, `r`, `e`, `a`, `t`, `m`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `f`, `u`, `s`, `a`, `l`, ` `, `w`, `h`, `e`, `n`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `n`, `t`, `a`, `m`, `i`, `n`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `f`, `e`, `a`, `r`, `s`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `l`, `l`, `i`, `d`, `e`, ` `, `w`, `i`, `t`, `h`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `m`, `i`, `l`, `i`, `e`, `u`, `.`

**Stem.** A 29-year-old man is admitted from the emergency department with a possible psychotic disorder. He is noted to perform elaborate ritualized behaviors before eating — arranging his tray precisely, touching items in a fixed sequence, and repeating phrases — and he becomes extremely distressed if interrupted. He describes intrusive thoughts about contamination. He acknowledges the behaviors are excessive and senseless but cannot stop performing them. Which is the most accurate interpretation of this presentation?

- OCD can masquerade as psychosis when rituals are elaborate — the structured, ego-dystonic nature of the rituals and the contamination preoccupation are consistent with OCD, not a primary psychotic disorder. **← keyed correct**
- This confirms a psychotic disorder with magical thinking — ritualized behavior during a psychotic episode indicates command hallucinations driving the rituals.
- This is catatonia — the stereotyped movements and distress on interruption indicate a catatonic episode requiring lorazepam challenge.
- This represents an autism spectrum disorder with restricted/repetitive behaviors — pursue developmental history and ASD workup.

**Rationale.** Rituals = psychosis, Stereotypy = catatonia, and Repetitive behavior = ASD all replace OCD with other diagnoses based on surface features. The page notes: 'OCD can masquerade as psychosis when rituals are elaborate.' The discriminating features are ego-dystonicity (patient recognizes the ritual as excessive) and the contamination obsession → relief-seeking ritual chain.

**Pearl.** Elaborate ego-dystonic rituals + contamination obsessions on a psychiatric unit = OCD masquerading as psychosis — clarify ego-dystonicity before committing to a psychosis diagnosis.

**Stem.** A student is reviewing a patient with OCD and notes serial Y-BOCS scores in the chart: 32 on admission, 24 at one week, and 20 at two weeks. The attending asks the student to interpret what the Y-BOCS measures and what these scores indicate. Which response is most accurate?

- The Y-BOCS is a patient self-report symptom checklist for OCD — scores above 20 indicate clinical disorder; a drop from 32 to 20 reflects the patient's subjective sense of improvement.
- The Y-BOCS (Yale-Brown Obsessive-Compulsive Scale) is the gold-standard clinician-rated measure of OCD severity and treatment response — it includes obsession and compulsion domains scored separately, and the drop from 32 to 20 indicates a meaningful response to treatment. **← keyed correct**
- The Y-BOCS is a broadband anxiety screen — it measures generalized anxiety severity across all anxiety disorders and is used to track treatment response in any anxiety condition.
- The Y-BOCS is a behavioral observation tool completed by nursing staff during the hospital stay, documenting observable compulsive behaviors rather than the patient's internal experience.

**Rationale.** Y-BOCS = self-report, Broadband anxiety screen, and Behavioral observation only all mischaracterize the instrument. The page specifically names the Y-BOCS as 'the gold-standard clinician-rated measure of OCD severity and treatment response.' Serial tracking — obsessions and compulsions scored separately — is the measure's primary clinical value.

**Pearl.** Y-BOCS = gold-standard clinician-rated OCD scale; obsessions and compulsions scored separately; use it to track treatment response, not just screen at baseline.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "What is the structure of the Y-BOCS that makes it sensitive to treatment response over time?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "A single total score calculated from patient self-report items rated on a 0–4 scale — simple total tracking over serial administrations."}, {"key": "B", "t": "A symptom checklist phase (cataloguing which obsessions and compulsions are present) followed by 10 severity items (5 for obsessions, 5 for compulsions) rated by the clinician — the two subscores can detect differential improvement in obsessions vs compulsions.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "A frequency-only scale that counts the hours per day spent on rituals — higher ritual time equals higher severity regardless of patient distress."}, {"key": "D", "t": "A functional impairment scale that rates the impact of OCD on work, relationships, and ADLs — not symptom severity per se."}], "why": "The Y-BOCS structure is what makes it the gold standard. The symptom checklist phase establishes which obsessions and compulsions are present (a dimensional catalogue of content types), but the severity rating is driven by the 10 severity items — 5 for obsessions and 5 for compulsions — rated by the clinician across dimensions like time occupied, distress, resistance, and control. Because obsessions and compulsions are scored separately (0–20 each, 0–40 total), the Y-BOCS can detect when medication reduces compulsions while obsessions persist, or vice versa, which informs the decision to intensify ERP vs pharmacotherapy."}

**Stem.** A 34-year-old woman with PTSD is admitted following a psychiatric emergency. During nursing assessment, she becomes acutely distressed, raises her voice, and states, 'Don't touch me — get away from me.' Two staff members consider initiating a hold. The attending pauses and asks the team to consider the clinical frame. Which interpretation best reflects trauma-informed care?

- The patient's behavior meets criteria for involuntary hold — refusing assessment endangers the evaluation process.
- This is treatment-seeking behavior — the distress indicates she wants more intense intervention; respond by offering medication.
- This likely represents trauma activation — PTSD may surface as hyperarousal and reactions to perceived threats; the trauma-informed response is to pause, explain procedures, offer choices, and avoid restraint. **← keyed correct**
- The verbal aggression may be a prodrome of violence — activate the behavioral emergency protocol for potential assault.

**Rationale.** Refusal = hold criteria, Distress = medication need, and Verbal escalation = imminent assault all trigger coercive responses that retraumatize. The page explicitly states: 'PTSD may surface as hyperarousal, nightmares, or a behavioral reaction to restraint, seclusion, or a same-gender vs. opposite-gender examiner.' Trauma-informed care — 'predictability, explained procedures, offered choices, and minimized restraint/seclusion — reduces retraumatization and lowers behavioral escalation.'

**Pearl.** PTSD may surface as behavioral distress on the unit; trauma-informed care (predictability, choice, explain procedures, minimize restraint) reduces both retraumatization and escalation.

**Stem.** A 26-year-old combat veteran is admitted for a depressive episode. He is being interviewed by a female medical student when he abruptly terminates the interview and states he cannot continue. He has nightmares, is hypervigilant, and startle-reactive. The student asks the attending how to understand this behavior clinically. Which is most accurate?

- The patient is likely psychotic — the refusal to engage with an examiner is a negative symptom or paranoia.
- The patient is non-adherent and should be counseled on the importance of clinical cooperation.
- This may represent a personality disorder — emotional dysregulation and interview termination suggest borderline features.
- PTSD may surface as behavioral reactions to specific examiners or triggers; this combat veteran's avoidance of a female examiner is a recognized PTSD presentation — accommodate the trigger rather than pathologizing the refusal. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Avoidance = psychosis, Avoidance = non-compliance, and Avoidance = personality disorder all pathologize what is a PTSD-specific behavioral response. The page is explicit: 'PTSD may surface as hyperarousal, nightmares, or a behavioral reaction to restraint, seclusion, or a same-gender vs. opposite-gender examiner.' Accommodation (e.g., offering a male examiner) is the clinical response.

**Pearl.** PTSD may present on the unit as interview refusal or agitation related to specific triggers (examiner gender, restraint, procedures) — accommodate the trigger rather than pathologizing the refusal.

**Stem.** A 39-year-old woman with PTSD is ready for discharge after a brief inpatient stay. She was started on sertraline. The resident says, 'She's on an SSRI — she's set.' The attending asks the student to identify what is missing from this discharge plan.

- She should also have a benzodiazepine prescription for acute anxiety management in the first few weeks after discharge.
- The discharge plan is complete — SSRIs are the evidence-based treatment for PTSD, and outpatient titration will complete the course.
- She needs a referral to outpatient therapy that can deliver prolonged exposure (PE) or cognitive processing therapy (CPT) — medication alone does not finish the job; the behavioral component is the definitive treatment for PTSD. **← keyed correct**
- The sertraline dose should be maximized before discharge — achieving therapeutic dosing during inpatient stay makes outpatient follow-up less urgent.

**Rationale.** Discharge benzo for anxiety, Medication alone is sufficient, and Maximize dose before discharge all miss the key gap. The page states: 'Confirm the SSRI/SNRI is at a tolerated dose with a clear titration plan, and verify outpatient follow-up that can deliver ERP, PE, or CPT — the medication rarely finishes the job alone.' PE or CPT referral is the explicit discharge requirement.

**Pearl.** Medication alone rarely finishes the PTSD treatment job — discharge requires a referral to outpatient PE or CPT; confirming the behavioral therapy access is a discharge criterion.

**Stem.** A student is preparing a PTSD psychoeducation handout and wants to explain the difference between prolonged exposure (PE) and cognitive processing therapy (CPT). Both are listed as evidence-based treatments in the patient's discharge plan. Which statement about the distinction is most accurate?

- PE and CPT are the same treatment with different names used by different institutions.
- PE focuses on identifying and challenging maladaptive beliefs ('stuck points') about the trauma and its meaning; CPT focuses on repeated imaginal and in-vivo exposure to trauma-related memories and avoided stimuli.
- PE focuses on gradual, repeated imaginal and in-vivo exposure to the trauma memory and avoided triggers to reduce conditioned fear; CPT targets cognitive stuck points — maladaptive beliefs about safety, trust, power, esteem, and intimacy altered by the trauma. **← keyed correct**
- PE is behavioral (no cognitive component) and requires revisiting the trauma narrative; CPT is purely cognitive (no behavioral component) and avoids discussing the trauma entirely.

**Rationale.** PE = CPT and Behavioral vs cognitive = entirely separate flatten the distinction. The page names both PE and CPT as first-line behavioral treatments for PTSD. Knowing that PE works through fear extinction via exposure and CPT works through cognitive restructuring of stuck points is the key clinical distinction for recommending or describing the referral.

**Pearl.** PE = repeated exposure to trauma memory and avoided triggers; CPT = cognitive stuck points about safety/trust/esteem altered by trauma — both are first-line for PTSD, mechanisms differ.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "A patient with PTSD has significant disability from maladaptive beliefs — she believes she is permanently damaged, that the world is entirely unsafe, and that she was responsible for the assault. Which therapy would most directly target these specific cognitions?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Prolonged exposure (PE) — revisiting the trauma memory repeatedly reduces the activation tied to these beliefs through habituation and corrective experience."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Cognitive processing therapy (CPT) — directly targeting 'stuck points' (maladaptive appraisals about self, others, and the world) is the mechanism CPT was designed around, making it the more direct match for this cognitive profile.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "DBT skills training — distress tolerance skills reduce the emotional activation that sustains these beliefs."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Exposure and response prevention (ERP) — gradual exposure to feared beliefs while preventing safety behaviors will extinguish the associated fear."}], "why": "CPT is specifically designed around the 'stuck point' model: the idea that trauma disrupts assimilated beliefs about safety, trust, power, esteem, and intimacy, and that recovery requires directly challenging and revising these disrupted cognitions through structured written and verbal exercises. PE works primarily through fear extinction via repeated exposure to the trauma memory — it addresses the cognitions indirectly, through the corrective emotional experience of confronting the memory without harm. For a patient whose primary disability is cognitive (who I am now, is the world safe, was I responsible), CPT's direct targeting of these appraisals is the more mechanistically matched choice."}

**Stem.** A patient with panic disorder and frequent panic attacks wants long-term treatment. Which is the most appropriate first-line pharmacologic option?

- SSRI plus CBT maintenance **← keyed correct**
- Long-term benzodiazepine monotherapy
- An antipsychotic for maintenance
- Reassurance without structured treatment

**Rationale.** SSRIs, paired with CBT such as interoceptive exposure, are first-line for panic disorder. Benzodiazepines are short-term bridges at most given dependence risk.

**Pearl.** Panic disorder: SSRI plus CBT first-line; benzodiazepines are not maintenance monotherapy.

**Stem.** A patient with OCD has distressing obsessions and time-consuming compulsions. Which is the evidence-based first-line treatment?

- A benzodiazepine as monotherapy
- An SSRI (often at higher doses) plus exposure and response prevention **← keyed correct**
- Supportive therapy alone without exposure work
- An antipsychotic as first-line monotherapy

**Rationale.** OCD is treated first-line with an SSRI (frequently at higher doses than for depression) plus exposure and response prevention (ERP). Clomipramine is an effective second-line agent; antipsychotics augment refractory cases.

**Pearl.** OCD: SSRI (higher dose) + ERP first-line; clomipramine second-line; antipsychotic augmentation if refractory.

**Stem.** A veteran with PTSD has nightmares, hypervigilance, and avoidance. Which approach is first-line, and what should generally be avoided?

- Scheduled benzodiazepines as the mainstay
- Antipsychotic monotherapy for core symptoms
- Trauma-focused psychotherapy and/or SSRI/SNRI; avoid routine benzodiazepines **← keyed correct**
- Watchful waiting without treatment

**Rationale.** First-line PTSD care prioritizes trauma-focused psychotherapy such as CPT or prolonged exposure, with sertraline, paroxetine, or venlafaxine as medication options when medication is chosen. Routine benzodiazepines are generally avoided.

**Pearl.** PTSD: trauma-focused therapy first; SSRI/SNRI when medication is chosen; avoid routine benzodiazepines.

- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `a`, `n`, `x`, `i`, `e`, `t`, `y`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `E`, `x`, `a`, `m`, ` `, `t`, `r`, `e`, `a`, `t`, `m`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `c`, `h`, `o`, `r`, `s`, `:`, ` `, `p`, `e`, `r`, `f`, `o`, `r`, `m`, `a`, `n`, `c`, `e`, `-`, `o`, `n`, `l`, `y`, ` `, `s`, `o`, `c`, `i`, `a`, `l`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `x`, `i`, `e`, `t`, `y`, `.`

**Stem.** A student develops intense anxiety only when performing music in public but is comfortable in other social situations. Which best describes the condition and a reasonable option?

- Social anxiety disorder, Performance type requiring PRN medication **← keyed correct**
- Generalized anxiety disorder requiring daily long-term medication
- Panic disorder requiring PRN medication
- A normal reaction requiring no discussion

**Rationale.** Anxiety confined to discrete public performances is performance-only social anxiety. When medically appropriate, a PRN beta-blocker can reduce tremor, tachycardia, and sweating for those events; generalized social anxiety is treated with CBT/exposure and SSRI/SNRI when medication is needed.

**Pearl.** Performance-only social anxiety: PRN beta-blocker for physical performance symptoms; generalized social anxiety needs CBT/exposure ± SSRI/SNRI.

**Stem.** A 19-year-old college student is brought to the inpatient unit by his parents after three weeks of dramatically decreased need for sleep (sleeping two to three hours and feeling rested), expansive and grandiose mood, pressured speech, and impulsive spending. His parents note he has always been 'a little hyper' in school but never like this. He takes no medications and denies substance use. Most likely diagnosis?

- Bipolar disorder, manic episode — the episodic 3-week course with decreased need for sleep and grandiosity distinguishes this from ADHD **← keyed correct**
- ADHD, first recognized in adulthood — the impulsivity and hyperactivity confirm childhood-onset ADHD now decompensated
- Brief psychotic disorder — the grandiosity indicates loss of reality testing
- Adjustment disorder — the academic stress of college explains the sleep changes and mood elevation

**Rationale.** ADHD vs mania is the key discrimination: ADHD is chronic and cross-situational from childhood, not episodic with decreased need for sleep and grandiosity. Psychosis = schizophrenia misreads a mood symptom as a primary psychotic feature. Adjustment by default collapses a manic episode into a stress reaction.

**Pearl.** ADHD is chronic and cross-situational from childhood; mania is episodic — episodic ADHD-like symptoms are a mood or substance flag.

**Stem.** An autistic adult admitted after aggression at his group home is increasingly distressed on the inpatient unit — covering his ears, rocking, and repeatedly asking when it will be quiet. He is not in imminent danger. No PRN medication has been given. The most appropriate first step is which of the following?

- Administer a PRN antipsychotic — irritability and agitation in autism respond to targeted medication
- Engage him in a group activity to normalize the milieu and reduce his isolation
- Move him to a quieter space and establish a predictable routine to reduce sensory load **← keyed correct**
- Assign a staff member to redirect him verbally and prompt use of calming strategies

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic skips the evidence-based first step. Group = stimulation adds sensory load when reduction is the goal. Verbal prompting first increases auditory demand on an already overloaded sensory system. The environment-first approach — quiet space, predictable routine, reduced sensory input — is the foundational intervention in ASD on the inpatient unit.

**Pearl.** In ASD, lead with the environment — predictability, reduced sensory load, and concrete communication — before reaching for medication.

**Stem.** A 22-year-old man with autism spectrum disorder and intellectual disability who lives in a group home is admitted for acute behavioral escalation — he became agitated and hit a staff member after a change in his daily routine. A nurse says, 'He has ASD — this is probably just how he behaves.' Which response reflects the correct clinical approach?

- Agree — behavioral escalation is expected in ASD and does not require further workup.
- New behavioral escalation in a patient with ASD or ID earns a medical workup — diagnostic overshadowing (assuming new behavior is just the developmental disorder) is the cardinal error; screen for pain, constipation, infection, dental problems, and medication effects before attributing to baseline. **← keyed correct**
- Start risperidone immediately — it is FDA-approved for autism and will treat the behavioral dyscontrol.
- Contact the group home about the routine change — environmental causes are the only plausible explanation for behavioral escalation in ASD.

**Rationale.** Baseline dismissal, Medication before assessment, and Environmental cause only all skip the foundational step. The page's first pearl: 'Diagnostic overshadowing is the cardinal error — new symptoms in ASD/ID earn a workup, not a shrug.' Behavior is communication; medical drivers must be excluded before attributing to baseline or starting a psychotropic.

**Pearl.** Diagnostic overshadowing in ASD/ID: new behavioral escalation always earns a workup for pain, constipation, infection, dental issues, and medications before attributing to baseline.

**Stem.** An 18-year-old with autism spectrum disorder is admitted for aggressive behavior. His parents ask if medication can 'help with his autism.' The team considers starting risperidone. Which statement about the pharmacological target of risperidone in ASD is most accurate?

- Risperidone is FDA-approved to improve the core social-communication deficits of autism, including eye contact, reciprocal conversation, and restricted interests.
- Risperidone is effective for all ASD-related behavioral symptoms when used at sufficient doses — including social withdrawal, repetitive behaviors, and communication deficits.
- Risperidone and aripiprazole are FDA-approved for irritability and aggression associated with autism — they do not treat the core social-communication features; target symptoms must be explicitly defined, and metabolic monitoring is required. **← keyed correct**
- Risperidone is a first-line agent for all behaviors in any patient with autism regardless of severity — it should be started prophylactically to prevent aggression.

**Rationale.** Antipsychotic treats core ASD features, Sufficient dose treats all features, and Prophylactic antipsychotic all misstate the indication. The page is specific: 'Risperidone and aripiprazole are FDA-approved for irritability/aggression associated with autism (they do NOT treat the core social-communication features), so target symptoms explicitly, monitor metabolically, and avoid reflexive standing antipsychotics.'

**Pearl.** Risperidone/aripiprazole treat autism-associated irritability/aggression (FDA-approved) — they do NOT treat core social-communication features; always document the target symptom.

**Stem.** A 16-year-old boy with a childhood diagnosis of ADHD is admitted following his first episode of several days of markedly decreased sleep, grandiosity, pressured speech, and impulsive behavior. His parents report that his ADHD has 'always been there' but this episode is 'completely different.' His Vanderbilt score from 3 years ago documented inattention and hyperactivity across home and school settings. Which interpretation is most accurate?

- This is undertreated ADHD — intensifying stimulant therapy will address the behavioral escalation.
- The childhood ADHD diagnosis rules out bipolar disorder — the two cannot co-occur.
- The decreased sleep, grandiosity, and pressured speech are likely stimulant side effects from his ADHD medications — reduce the dose and reassess before considering a mood diagnosis.
- ADHD is chronic and cross-situational from childhood; episodic presentations with mood elevation, decreased need for sleep, and grandiosity are mood or substance flags, not ADHD exacerbations — this clinical picture warrants a first manic episode evaluation, not a stimulant adjustment. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** ADHD intensification for episodic change and ADHD excludes bipolar both apply the wrong clinical framework. Option C also describes the correct reasoning but D is more complete in naming the key discriminators. The page explicitly states: 'ADHD is chronic, pervasive, and cross-situational from childhood; mania is episodic with mood elevation, decreased need for sleep, and grandiosity — episodic ADHD-like symptoms are a mood or substance flag.'

**Pearl.** ADHD is chronic and cross-situational from childhood; a new episodic presentation with decreased sleep, grandiosity, and pressured speech is a first manic episode flag — not an ADHD exacerbation.

### qb_cdev_004

- Linked pages: `t_neurodev.md`
- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `n`, `e`, `u`, `r`, `o`, `d`, `e`, `v`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `C`, `a`, `t`, `a`, `t`, `o`, `n`, `i`, `a`, ` `, `i`, `n`, ` `, `a`, `u`, `t`, `i`, `s`, `m`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `u`, `n`, `d`, `e`, `r`, `-`, `r`, `e`, `c`, `o`, `g`, `n`, `i`, `z`, `e`, `d`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `i`, `m`, `p`, `o`, `r`, `t`, `a`, `n`, `t`, `;`, ` `, `n`, `e`, `w`, ` `, `m`, `u`, `t`, `i`, `s`, `m`, `,`, ` `, `i`, `m`, `m`, `o`, `b`, `i`, `l`, `i`, `t`, `y`, `,`, ` `, `p`, `o`, `s`, `t`, `u`, `r`, `i`, `n`, `g`, `,`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `s`, `t`, `u`, `p`, `o`, `r`, ` `, `i`, `n`, ` `, `a`, `n`, ` `, `a`, `u`, `t`, `i`, `s`, `t`, `i`, `c`, ` `, `p`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `s`, `h`, `o`, `u`, `l`, `d`, ` `, `p`, `r`, `o`, `m`, `p`, `t`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `t`, `a`, `t`, `o`, `n`, `i`, `a`, ` `, `e`, `v`, `a`, `l`, `u`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `.`

**Stem.** A 26-year-old man with autism spectrum disorder is admitted by his group home for a change in behavior. Over the past 10 days he has become mute, stopped eating, and is found in fixed postures for hours at a time. He previously spoke in simple sentences and was active in daily activities. Which diagnosis must be actively evaluated?

- Worsening autism — the increase in stereotyped behaviors and withdrawal represents a regression to a lower developmental level.
- Treatment-resistant depression — the mutism and akinesia reflect severe depression refractory to prior treatment.
- Neuroleptic malignant syndrome — posturing and immobility indicate a drug reaction if the patient is on an antipsychotic.
- Catatonia — new mutism, fixed posturing, and immobility in an autistic patient represent catatonia until proven otherwise; catatonia is under-recognized in ASD and requires specific evaluation and treatment. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Regression = autism worsening, Mutism = severe depression, and NMS by default all bypass the specific catatonia recognition task. The page explicitly calls out: 'Catatonia is under-recognized in autistic patients — look for it.' New mutism, immobility, and posturing in a previously communicative autistic adult is the classic presentation — it requires a catatonia evaluation (Bush-Francis scale), not dismissal as regression.

**Pearl.** Catatonia is under-recognized in ASD — new mutism, fixed posturing, and immobility in an autistic patient = catatonia evaluation, not regression or depression attribution.

**Stem.** A 9-year-old is referred for inattention and hyperactivity. Which is required to diagnose ADHD?

- Symptoms limited to the classroom
- New onset after age 12 with normal early childhood
- A confirmatory brain MRI or EEG
- Symptoms before age 12 with impairment in at least two settings **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** ADHD requires several symptoms before age 12, ≥6 months, with impairment in ≥2 settings; it is a clinical diagnosis.

**Pearl.** ADHD: symptoms before age 12, ≥2 settings, clinical diagnosis.

**Stem.** A 10-year-old has ADHD with significant impairment despite behavioral supports. First-line pharmacologic treatment?

- A stimulant **← keyed correct**
- An antipsychotic
- A benzodiazepine
- An SSRI

**Rationale.** Stimulants are first-line and most effective for core ADHD symptoms when not contraindicated; non-stimulants such as atomoxetine, viloxazine, and alpha-2 agonists are alternatives when risk-benefit concerns point away from stimulants.

**Pearl.** ADHD: stimulants first-line; non-stimulants are alternatives when risk or tolerability changes the plan.

**Stem.** An 8-year-old is argumentative, loses his temper, and defies adults, but has never been physically aggressive, destroyed property, stolen, or seriously violated others' rights. Best diagnosis?

- Conduct disorder
- Oppositional defiant disorder **← keyed correct**
- Antisocial personality disorder
- Intermittent explosive disorder

**Rationale.** ODD is angry/irritable mood, argumentative/defiant behavior, and vindictiveness WITHOUT the aggression, destruction, deceit, or serious rule-violations of conduct disorder.

**Pearl.** ODD = defiant/argumentative without violating others' rights; conduct disorder adds aggression/destruction/deceit.

**Stem.** A 4-year-old has marked deficits in social communication and reciprocity plus restricted, repetitive behaviors and insistence on sameness, present since early development. Best diagnosis?

- Social (pragmatic) communication disorder
- Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder
- Autism spectrum disorder **← keyed correct**
- Childhood-onset schizophrenia

**Rationale.** ASD requires BOTH social-communication deficits AND restricted, repetitive behaviors with early onset; the restricted/repetitive domain separates it from social communication disorder.

**Pearl.** Autism = social-communication deficits PLUS restricted/repetitive behaviors, early onset.

**Stem.** A 12-year-old has had multiple motor tics and at least one vocal tic, waxing and waning for 18 months, onset at age 9. Best diagnosis?

- Persistent (chronic) motor OR vocal tic disorder
- Provisional tic disorder
- Functional movement disorder
- Tourette disorder with motor and vocal tics **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Tourette requires BOTH multiple motor tics AND ≥1 vocal tic, >1 year, onset before age 18.

**Pearl.** Tourette: multiple motor tics AND ≥1 vocal tic, >1 year, onset before 18.

**Stem.** A 7-year-old has primary nocturnal enuresis with normal daytime continence and a normal exam. Most appropriate first-line management?

- Behavioral measures plus an enuresis alarm **← keyed correct**
- Open-ended desmopressin as first-line therapy
- Imipramine as first-line therapy
- Fluid restriction and punishment for wet nights

**Rationale.** For primary nocturnal enuresis with normal daytime continence and a normal exam, reassurance and behavioral measures are first-line; the enuresis alarm has the best long-term cure. Desmopressin is useful for short-term control, and TCAs are later-line because of toxicity.

**Pearl.** Enuresis: alarm for long-term cure; desmopressin for short-term control; never punish wet nights.

**Stem.** A child is evaluated for intellectual disability. What determines the severity level (mild, moderate, severe, profound)?

- Full-scale IQ score alone
- Adaptive-functioning impairment **← keyed correct**
- Chronological age at testing
- Presence of a known genetic syndrome

**Rationale.** Intellectual disability requires deficits in intellectual and adaptive functioning with developmental-period onset; severity is graded by adaptive functioning, not IQ alone.

**Pearl.** Intellectual disability severity = adaptive functioning, not IQ alone.

**Stem.** A 7-year-old refuses school, complains of stomachaches on school mornings that resolve on weekends, and is terrified that something will happen to his mother while apart. Best diagnosis?

- Generalized anxiety disorder
- Oppositional defiant disorder
- Malingering for external gain
- Separation anxiety disorder **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Developmentally inappropriate, excessive fear of separation from attachment figures — with somatic complaints and school refusal, lasting ≥4 weeks in a child — is separation anxiety disorder.

**Pearl.** Separation anxiety disorder: excessive separation fear, somatic complaints, school refusal (≥4 weeks in children).

**Stem.** A 6-year-old repeatedly soils his underwear; exam reveals a large stool mass and history of painful, infrequent bowel movements. Best diagnosis and initial management?

- Retentive encopresis from constipation **← keyed correct**
- Conduct disorder with deliberate defiance
- Primary psychotic process
- Normal age-appropriate behavior

**Rationale.** Encopresis at age 4 or older is often retentive with overflow incontinence. Treat the constipation with disimpaction and maintenance, add toileting routine and behavioral support, and avoid punishment.

**Pearl.** Encopresis: usually retentive overflow — treat the constipation first, add behavioral measures.

**Stem.** A 6-year-old speaks normally at home but consistently fails to speak at school, where she is otherwise engaged; this has lasted the school year and impairs learning. Best diagnosis?

- Autism spectrum disorder
- Selective mutism **← keyed correct**
- A language (communication) disorder
- Oppositional defiant disorder

**Rationale.** Selective mutism is a consistent failure to speak in specific social situations (e.g., school) despite speaking normally elsewhere, interfering with functioning — an anxiety-spectrum disorder, not a language or defiance problem.

**Pearl.** Selective mutism: won't speak in specific settings but speaks normally elsewhere — an anxiety disorder.

**Stem.** A 45-year-old man with schizophrenia is voluntarily admitted and refuses his prescribed antipsychotic. When asked, he explains what the medication is for, articulates specific concerns about its side effects, understands the risks of not taking it, and asks whether alternatives exist. Best next step?

- Conduct a formal capacity assessment; if he has capacity, his refusal must be respected, documented, and an alternative offered **← keyed correct**
- Override the refusal — voluntarily admitted patients accept the prescribed treatment plan as a condition of admission
- Seek an emergency court order — a schizophrenia diagnosis with medication refusal requires judicial review
- Discharge him against medical advice — he is voluntary and refusing treatment

**Rationale.** Refusal = no capacity in two forms — assumed from admission type and assumed from diagnosis — is the central error. Capacity is decision-specific and clinical: the four abilities are assessed, not inferred from the admission voluntary/involuntary status or from a psychiatric diagnosis. Discharge for refusal conflates refusal with grounds for discharge. This patient's demonstrated abilities (communicating, understanding, appreciating, reasoning) point toward capacity — in which case his refusal must be honored.

**Pearl.** Capacity is decision-specific and clinical — a psychiatric diagnosis and even a refusal do not equal incapacity; assess the four abilities.

**Stem.** An outpatient therapist is meeting with a 34-year-old patient who describes, in specific detail, a plan to assault a named family member. He states his intent to act before the end of the week and describes the location and means. The therapist's obligation is which of the following?

- Maintain confidentiality — disclosing the patient's statements would violate the therapeutic relationship and deter future help-seeking
- Document the threat and continue outpatient treatment with increased session frequency
- Take reasonable protective steps — which may include warning the potential victim, notifying law enforcement, hospitalizing the patient, or intensifying treatment **← keyed correct**
- Wait for corroborating evidence before acting — a single session disclosure does not establish genuine intent

**Rationale.** Confidentiality always wins, Document and continue, and Wait for action are three forms of inaction in the face of the duty to protect. A specific, credible threat to a named, identifiable person — with described means and timeline — triggers a duty to take reasonable protective steps. The options are broad: warning the victim, notifying police, hospitalizing, or intensifying treatment. The duty does not require certainty that harm will occur.

**Pearl.** A specific, credible threat to a reasonably identifiable victim overrides confidentiality — the duty to protect obligates action, not only documentation.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does this duty override therapeutic confidentiality?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Any mention of violence mandates disclosure under mental health law — the therapist has no discretion"}, {"key": "B", "t": "The therapist-patient privilege does not apply in outpatient settings, only in inpatient contexts"}, {"key": "C", "t": "A specific, credible threat to a named, identifiable person is the recognized exception — the duty to protect an endangered third party overrides confidentiality in that circumstance", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "Mentioning intent to harm automatically ends the therapeutic relationship and terminates all confidentiality obligations"}], "why": "The exception is specific and limited: it requires a serious threat to a reasonably identifiable person — not any mention of violence, not a setting restriction, and not automatic termination. Outside this exception, confidentiality stands. Disclosing broadly in response to general hostility or vague threats would itself be an ethical violation. The therapist-patient relationship continues; the duty is to protect, not to terminate."}

- Evidence: `e`, `t`, `h`, `i`, `c`, `s`, `_`, `l`, `e`, `g`, `a`, `l`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `A`, ` `, `s`, `e`, `r`, `i`, `o`, `u`, `s`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `r`, `e`, `a`, `t`, ` `, `t`, `o`, ` `, `a`, `n`, ` `, `i`, `d`, `e`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `f`, `i`, `a`, `b`, `l`, `e`, ` `, `v`, `i`, `c`, `t`, `i`, `m`, ` `, `t`, `r`, `i`, `g`, `g`, `e`, `r`, `s`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `d`, `u`, `t`, `y`, ` `, `t`, `o`, ` `, `p`, `r`, `o`, `t`, `e`, `c`, `t`, ` `, `(`, `T`, `a`, `r`, `a`, `s`, `o`, `f`, `f`, `)`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `n`, `f`, `i`, `d`, `e`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `a`, `l`, `i`, `t`, `y`, ` `, `y`, `i`, `e`, `l`, `d`, `s`, `.`

**Stem.** During an outpatient psychiatry session, a 34-year-old man with paranoid schizophrenia states, 'I have a gun at home. When John Smith gets out of prison next month, I'm going to kill him.' John Smith is his estranged brother. Which action is most appropriate?

- Escalate by warning the potential victim, notifying law enforcement, or hospitalizing the patient — a specific threat to an identifiable person triggers a duty to protect, and confidentiality yields to this obligation. **← keyed correct**
- Maintain confidentiality — the threat is against a family member, and family threats are not covered by the Tarasoff duty.
- Document the threat and reassess at the next session — the duty to warn only applies to imminent threats.
- Consult with a supervisor and take no other action until legal counsel is contacted — clinicians must confirm the legal requirement before taking any action.

**Rationale.** Family exception, Imminence required, and Legal clearance first all create barriers to a clinically required action. The page states: 'A specific threat to a specific person overrides confidentiality.' The duty to protect may be fulfilled by warning the victim, notifying police, hospitalizing the patient, or intensifying treatment — and specifics vary by state, but the tested principle is clear.

**Pearl.** Tarasoff: a specific threat to an identifiable victim overrides confidentiality — warn, notify police, or hospitalize; inaction in a credible threat is not a safe option.

**Stem.** A 6-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician with multiple bruises in various stages of healing. His mother explains that he 'bruises easily.' The student suspects possible physical abuse but is uncertain. There are no obvious fractures, and the child denies being hurt. Which is the correct action?

- Do not report yet — a definitive diagnosis of abuse must be established before filing a report, and reporting uncertainty could harm an innocent family.
- File a mandatory report with child protective services immediately — mandatory reporters must report on reasonable suspicion, not proof, and good-faith reporters are legally protected. **← keyed correct**
- Refer to a social worker and wait for a social assessment before deciding whether to report — the social worker's professional judgment supersedes the clinician's obligation.
- Conduct a formal forensic assessment before reporting — premature reports reduce the accuracy of subsequent forensic interviews.

**Rationale.** Proof required to report, Social work replaces report, and Forensic assessment before report all create delays in a child protection obligation. The page states: 'Reporting requires reasonable suspicion, not proof, and mandated reporters are generally protected when reporting in good faith.' Multiple healing bruises is reasonable suspicion; that is the threshold.

**Pearl.** Mandatory reporting for child abuse requires reasonable suspicion, not proof — good-faith reporters are protected; report now and let CPS investigate.

**Stem.** A 38-year-old man with schizophrenia is involuntarily hospitalized following an acute psychotic episode with danger to others. On day 2, he states that he refuses to take haloperidol. The team wants to medicate him over his objection because 'he's committed.' Which statement is most accurate?

- Involuntary hospitalization authorizes involuntary medication — the commitment order supersedes the patient's medication refusal.
- The team should use a shorter-acting intramuscular formulation — minimizing the degree of involuntary intervention makes the override ethically permissible.
- Involuntary hospitalization does NOT authorize involuntary medication; overriding the refusal requires either a true emergency (imminent danger) or a separate legal or adjudicated process independent of the commitment. **← keyed correct**
- The patient's schizophrenia diagnosis implies impaired judgment — a psychiatric diagnosis automatically confers authority to override medication refusal.

**Rationale.** Commitment = consent to medicate, Route reduces ethical constraint, and Diagnosis = impaired capacity are all versions of the same error. The page is clear: 'Involuntary hospitalization does NOT by itself authorize involuntary medication.' Overriding refusal requires an imminent-danger emergency or a separate legal/adjudicated process. Commitment status and medication consent are independent legal states.

**Pearl.** Commitment ≠ consent to medicate — involuntary hospitalization does not override medication refusal; a separate emergency or legal process is required.

**Stem.** A patient with delirium cannot provide coherent consent for an emergent procedure. The family asks if the patient 'has been declared incompetent.' The resident responds that the patient 'lacks capacity.' The family seems confused by the distinction. Which explanation is most accurate?

- Capacity and competence are synonymous — they differ only in which professional makes the determination.
- Competence is a clinical determination made by a physician or psychiatrist; capacity is a legal determination that requires a court order when the clinical team is uncertain about the patient's decision-making status.
- Incompetence is determined by a psychiatrist through formal neuropsychological testing; capacity is determined by a court based on a jury's judgment.
- Capacity is clinical and decision-specific — assessed by any physician for a particular decision at a point in time; competence is a legal determination made by a court, applicable more globally. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Capacity = competence, Description with keys swapped (B), and Roles reversed all miss the key discriminators. The page states: 'Capacity is decision-specific and clinical (a physician assesses it); competence is a legal determination (a court decides).' Option D captures both discriminators — the clinical/legal distinction AND the decision-specific nature of capacity.

**Pearl.** Capacity = clinical, decision-specific (physician decides); competence = legal determination (court decides) — they are not interchangeable.

**Stem.** A 62-year-old man is informed of the risks, benefits, and alternatives of a recommended ECT course; his cognition is grossly intact on interview, though no formal capacity assessment has been documented. He is under no coercion and says, 'I understand what you've told me, and I want to have ECT.' Which component of informed consent has NOT yet been addressed?

- He has expressed a choice — the consent is now complete because all required elements are present.
- The emergency exception permits ECT without formal consent in this case — ECT is a medical urgency.
- Voluntariness has not been assessed — the stem confirms disclosure and that the patient made a choice, but does not confirm he is free from undue influence, coercion, or therapeutic misconception.
- A formal clinical capacity assessment has not been explicitly performed — consent requires capacity, disclosure (risks/benefits/alternatives), and voluntariness; disclosure and voluntariness are confirmed in the stem, but capacity is asserted by the word 'intact cognition' without a documented four-ability assessment. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Choice = consent and Emergency exception for ECT overlook the structure of valid consent. Valid consent requires ALL three: capacity + disclosure + voluntariness. Disclosure is confirmed (risks/benefits/alternatives given), voluntariness is confirmed ('under no coercion'), but formal capacity documentation — the four abilities: communicate a choice, understand, appreciate, reason — is described as 'intact cognition' without a documented assessment. This is the gap.

**Pearl.** Informed consent = capacity + disclosure + voluntariness — all three must be documented; 'intact cognition' is not the same as a documented capacity assessment.

**Stem.** A 15-year-old presents to an outpatient clinic requesting evaluation and treatment for alcohol use disorder. He does not want his parents informed. His parents are alive, married, and have not relinquished guardianship. Which framework applies?

- Parental consent is always required for minors — the 15-year-old cannot consent to or receive evaluation without parental involvement.
- In many states, minors may consent to and receive substance use treatment without parental consent — this is one of the recognized statutory exceptions to the general parental consent rule; however, specific state law governs. **← keyed correct**
- Since he is 15, he is an emancipated minor in most states — emancipation applies to all minors above age 14.
- Adolescent confidentiality cannot be protected in substance use treatment — HIPAA requires parental access to minor treatment records in all clinical contexts.

**Rationale.** Minor always requires parental consent, Emancipation by age, and HIPAA overrides minor confidentiality all misstate the legal framework. The page states: 'In many states, care for STIs, contraception, substance use, and some mental-health services' are exceptions to the general parental consent requirement. The correct answer acknowledges the exception while noting state variation.

**Pearl.** In many states, minors can consent to substance use treatment without parental involvement — this is a recognized statutory exception; state law governs the specifics.

**Stem.** A patient with untreated mania insists on leaving; the team considers an involuntary hold. Which criterion must generally be met?

- The team simply believes admission would be beneficial
- The patient disagrees with the diagnosis
- Mental illness plus dangerousness or grave disability **← keyed correct**
- Family request for admission

**Rationale.** Involuntary hospitalization generally requires mental illness plus danger to self, danger to others, or grave disability; statutes vary by jurisdiction and local policy.

**Pearl.** Involuntary hold = mental illness + (danger to self / others / grave disability).

**Stem.** A resident asks whether a delirious patient is 'competent' to refuse a lumbar puncture. Most accurate framing?

- Capacity and competence are identical bedside labels
- Judicial review for every medical decision
- Lost capacity is permanent once documented
- Capacity is clinical and decision-specific; competence is legal **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Capacity is clinical, decision-specific, and can fluctuate; competence is a legal status decided by a court. The four abilities frame the bedside capacity assessment.

**Pearl.** Capacity = clinical, decision-specific, fluctuates; competence = legal, court-determined.

**Stem.** An unconscious patient arrives after an overdose, no surrogate reachable, needing immediate life-saving treatment. Which principle applies?

- Emergency exception with implied consent **← keyed correct**
- Wait for a court-appointed guardian before treating
- Require written consent before any intervention
- Treat an unrelated prior refusal as binding

**Rationale.** The emergency exception allows necessary treatment to prevent death or serious harm when the patient lacks capacity and no surrogate or applicable advance directive is available.

**Pearl.** Emergency exception: implied consent covers necessary urgent care when consent cannot be obtained.

**Stem.** A patient with intact capacity and a terminal illness refuses a recommended life-prolonging treatment; the team disagrees. Ethically correct action?

- Override because the treatment would prolong life
- Respect the refusal — a patient with capacity may decline even life-sustaining treatment **← keyed correct**
- Obtain a court order to compel treatment
- Declare the patient incompetent for refusing

**Rationale.** Autonomy governs: a capacitated patient may refuse even life-sustaining treatment, and disagreement with the team does not establish incapacity.

**Pearl.** A capacitated patient may refuse even life-sustaining treatment; a 'wrong' choice isn't incapacity.

**Stem.** A patient permanently lacks capacity and has no advance directive, but the surrogate knows the patient's long-standing values and prior wishes about a proposed procedure. Which standard applies first?

- The surrogate's own preference
- Best interest despite known patient values
- Substituted judgment based on known values and prior wishes **← keyed correct**
- Whatever the physician prefers

**Rationale.** When the patient's values or prior wishes are known, the surrogate applies substituted judgment — what the patient would have chosen. Best interest is the fallback when those wishes and values are unknown.

**Pearl.** Surrogate hierarchy: known wishes or values → substituted judgment → best interest when values are unknown.

**Stem.** During an interview, a child discloses details strongly suggesting physical abuse at home. The parent asks you to keep it confidential. What must you do?

- Keep it confidential because the parent requested it
- Wait until you have definitive proof before reporting
- Make a mandated report to child protective services based on reasonable suspicion **← keyed correct**
- Wait for the child to request a report

**Rationale.** Suspected child abuse is a mandatory report based on reasonable suspicion — you do not need proof, and confidentiality (including a parent's request) does not override the duty. Elder/dependent-adult abuse carries similar duties.

**Pearl.** Suspected child abuse = mandatory report on reasonable suspicion; confidentiality does not override it.

**Stem.** A 15-year-old comes alone requesting evaluation. Which statement about consent for a minor is most accurate?

- Parental consent is required for every non-emergency service
- A minor has broad adult authority for routine care
- Emergency care waits while staff reach a parent
- Parental consent is usual, with defined exceptions **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Parental consent is the general rule for minors, but important exceptions exist: emancipated or mature minors, certain confidential services depending on jurisdiction, and the emergency exception. Local law matters.

**Pearl.** Minors usually need parental consent, with jurisdiction-dependent confidential-service, emancipated-minor, and emergency exceptions.

**Stem.** A hospitalized patient invites the student doctor to meet socially and offers a substantial personal gift. What is the appropriate response?

- Decline the invitation and substantial gift respectfully **← keyed correct**
- Accept both to preserve rapport
- Accept the gift but decline the social meeting
- Revisit the invitation after discharge if both agree

**Rationale.** Professional boundaries protect patients: decline social invitations and substantial personal gifts respectfully. Sexual or romantic involvement with a patient is never acceptable, and significant gifts or dual relationships are boundary crossings to avoid.

**Pearl.** Decline significant gifts and dual relationships respectfully; sexual or romantic involvement with a patient is never acceptable.

**Stem.** A 31-year-old woman is admitted for a severe depressive episode with passive suicidal ideation. The intern plans to start sertraline today. Before initiating an antidepressant, the most important step is which of the following?

- Start sertraline now — the diagnosis of major depressive disorder is established by her current presentation
- Obtain a collateral history and formally screen for any prior manic or hypomanic episode before prescribing an antidepressant **← keyed correct**
- Start sertraline and add lithium prophylactically to cover any occult bipolar risk
- Start sertraline and a PRN benzodiazepine, since anxiety commonly accompanies depression

**Rationale.** Antidepressant before bipolar screen is the key trap: an SSRI started in unrecognized bipolar disorder can trigger mania or rapid cycling. Obtaining collateral and screening for past mania or hypomania is the gatekeeping step before any antidepressant is written. Prophylactic lithium without a diagnosis is premature polypharmacy, and the reflexive benzodiazepine adds risk without resolving the diagnostic question.

**Pearl.** Screen every depressed patient for past mania before writing for an antidepressant.

**Stem.** A 38-year-old man with bipolar I disorder, stable on lithium maintenance for two years, presents with a 2-day course of worsening tremor, confusion, and vomiting. Five days ago he began ibuprofen for back pain. Which of the following best explains his presentation?

- A breakthrough manic episode — agitation and confusion signal mood destabilization
- Viral encephalitis — the confusion warrants urgent neuroimaging and lumbar puncture
- Serotonin syndrome — lithium combined with ibuprofen triggers excess serotonin activity
- Lithium toxicity precipitated by ibuprofen reducing renal lithium clearance **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Treat the number not the patient, Missing the interaction, and Wrong toxidrome are the traps. Lithium has a narrow therapeutic window; NSAIDs reduce prostaglandin-mediated renal clearance and push lithium levels into the toxic range — a textbook drug interaction. The 2-day picture of tremor, confusion, and vomiting in a lithium-maintained patient who just started an NSAID is lithium toxicity until proven otherwise.

**Pearl.** NSAIDs, ACE-inhibitors/ARBs, thiazides, and dehydration push lithium levels toward toxicity — check the list when lithium patients deteriorate.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does ibuprofen raise lithium levels?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Ibuprofen displaces lithium from plasma protein binding, increasing free drug levels"}, {"key": "B", "t": "Ibuprofen inhibits hepatic metabolism of lithium, slowing its clearance"}, {"key": "C", "t": "Ibuprofen blocks gut absorption of lithium, causing erratic bioavailability and rebound toxicity"}, {"key": "D", "t": "Lithium is cleared almost entirely by the kidney; NSAIDs impair prostaglandin-dependent renal handling, reducing lithium clearance and raising serum levels", "c": true}], "why": "Lithium is not protein-bound and not hepatically metabolized — it is excreted almost entirely by the kidney. NSAIDs impair prostaglandin-mediated renal perfusion and tubular handling, reducing lithium clearance. This is why the page flags them alongside dehydration and other drugs that reduce glomerular filtration or tubular function as conditions that push levels toward toxicity."}

**Stem.** A 31-year-old woman with two prior depressive episodes was started on escitalopram without a bipolar screen. Two weeks later she is brought to the emergency department with 4 days of markedly decreased sleep, racing thoughts, and grandiosity. What does this presentation most likely represent?

- A new unipolar depressive episode — mood cycling within MDD can produce early-morning awakening and agitation resembling mania.
- An SSRI discontinuation syndrome — the behavioral and sleep changes reflect escitalopram's stimulant effect in some patients.
- Antidepressant-induced mood switch — starting an antidepressant in an unscreened patient with occult bipolar disorder can precipitate mania or mixed features; this is why bipolar screening is required before prescribing. **← keyed correct**
- Stimulant-induced insomnia — escitalopram acts as a stimulant in some patients and disrupts sleep without indicating a mood disorder.

**Rationale.** Cycling = unipolar, Antidepressant discontinuation, and Stimulant side effect all miss the key clinical teaching. The mood disorders page is explicit: 'a bipolar depression misread as unipolar can be pushed into mania or rapid cycling by an antidepressant.' This item tests the consequence of skipping the bipolar screen — not the procedural gap, but the clinical outcome that follows.

**Pearl.** Missing the bipolar screen before prescribing an antidepressant can precipitate a manic or mixed episode — know the consequence, not just the procedure.

**Stem.** A 38-year-old man presents for admission with a current depressive episode. He reports no prior manic episodes. His wife, who accompanied him, states that 3 years ago he 'went weeks without sleeping, gave away his car, and was fired for erratic behavior.' He does not recall this period as abnormal. Which diagnostic move has the highest yield in clarifying the bipolar question?

- Patient self-report is the most reliable — the wife's account may be distorted by relationship conflict.
- Obtain collateral history from the wife and prior clinicians — collateral is the single highest-yield diagnostic move for distinguishing bipolar from unipolar disorder. **← keyed correct**
- Administer a structured screening tool (Mood Disorder Questionnaire) — it replaces the need for collateral history.
- Only documented hospitalizations for mania count as evidence — behavioral reports from family members do not meet the diagnostic threshold.

**Rationale.** Self-report over collateral and Protocol over clinical judgment both bypass the highest-yield step. The mood page explicitly calls collateral 'the single highest-yield diagnostic move for distinguishing bipolar from unipolar.' This patient's lack of insight into a probable manic episode is classic — the wife's account is the clinical key.

**Pearl.** Collateral history is the single highest-yield diagnostic move for bipolar vs unipolar — patients with past mania often lack insight into the episode.

**Stem.** A 29-year-old man is admitted with depressed mood, anhedonia, and feelings of worthlessness. On detailed MSE, he also has racing thoughts, psychomotor agitation, and is sleeping only 3 hours per night despite feeling exhausted. He endorses passive suicidal ideation. Which statement about his presentation is most clinically important?

- The racing thoughts indicate that mania is the predominant syndrome and the depression is secondary — treat for mania first.
- The activating symptoms suggest his antidepressant dose is too high and producing anxiety — reduce the dose.
- Mixed features — concurrent depressive and activating symptoms — carry elevated suicide risk and are easy to miss; this combination warrants heightened safety vigilance. **← keyed correct**
- Mixed features can only occur in bipolar disorder — this patient's mixed presentation requires a bipolar I or II diagnosis before initiating treatment.

**Rationale.** One syndrome at a time, Side effect attribution, and Bipolar required for mixed all delay recognition of the dangerous phenotype. Mixed features — concurrent depression and activation — are explicitly called out on the mood page as easy to miss and carrying elevated suicide risk. This patient's passive SI plus mixed features is the classic high-risk presentation.

**Pearl.** Mixed features (depression + racing thoughts/agitation/decreased sleep) carry elevated suicide risk and are easy to miss — heighten safety vigilance.

**Stem.** A 71-year-old woman with no prior psychiatric history presents with 2 weeks of elevated mood, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, and grandiosity. Her family calls it her 'first manic episode.' She is on prednisone 40 mg daily for a recent flare of rheumatoid arthritis. Which step is most important before proceeding with a bipolar diagnosis?

- Document the manic episode and start a mood stabilizer — late-onset bipolar is increasingly recognized in older adults.
- Start an antipsychotic for the florid manic symptoms — mood stabilizers are too slow for acute management.
- Order a head MRI — new psychiatric symptoms in older adults indicate intracranial pathology until proven otherwise.
- Rule out medical mimics before anchoring on a primary mood diagnosis — corticosteroids are a recognized cause of manic symptoms; evaluate the steroid exposure as a contributing or precipitating cause. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Late-onset bipolar by default, Treat before diagnose, and Imaging before differential all skip the foundational step. The mood page lists corticosteroids in the medical mimic differential and explicitly states: 'Rule out medical mimics before anchoring on a primary mood disorder.' A 71-year-old on prednisone with a first-lifetime episode is steroid-induced mania until proven otherwise.

**Pearl.** New mood episodes in older or medically complex patients require a medical mimic workup — corticosteroids, thyroid disease, intoxication, CNS pathology.

**Stem.** A 44-year-old man with bipolar I disorder and two prior suicide attempts is starting maintenance therapy. The team is choosing between lithium and lamotrigine. Which argument most strongly favors lithium?

- Lithium has both the strongest anti-suicidal evidence and the strongest maintenance evidence in the mood stabilizer class — in a patient with prior attempts, both properties directly apply. **← keyed correct**
- Lamotrigine has equivalent anti-suicidal evidence to lithium — the choice should be based on tolerability alone.
- Lithium's anti-suicidal effect is limited to acute suicidality and does not apply to maintenance treatment.
- The narrow therapeutic window makes lithium too risky for long-term maintenance — the monitoring burden outweighs its benefits.

**Rationale.** Anti-suicidal = interchangeable, Narrow = second-line, and Monitoring = disqualifying all undervalue lithium in the highest-risk patient. The page specifically names lithium's anti-suicidal effect (Cipriani 2013) and BALANCE maintenance evidence as reasons to 'use it, and monitor it' — the monitoring is the price of the unique therapeutic profile, not a drawback.

**Pearl.** Lithium: strongest anti-suicidal evidence (Cipriani 2013) + strongest maintenance evidence (BALANCE trial) — use it in high-risk patients, and monitor it.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "What was the primary finding of the BALANCE trial regarding lithium and valproate for bipolar maintenance?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Valproate was superior to lithium for preventing depressive relapses in bipolar I disorder."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Lithium and valproate had equivalent relapse prevention — the monitoring burden explains why guidelines favor lithium despite equivalent efficacy."}, {"key": "C", "t": "Lithium was superior to valproate for preventing relapses in bipolar I disorder, supporting lithium as the preferred long-term maintenance mood stabilizer.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "BALANCE found that combination lithium + valproate was the only regimen that reduced relapse to below 50% at 2 years."}], "why": "The BALANCE trial (Geddes 2010) randomized patients with bipolar I disorder to lithium, valproate, or combination. **Stem.** A 67-year-old woman with treatment-resistant MDD is hospitalized. She has failed two adequate antidepressant trials, has active suicidal ideation, refuses all oral intake, and expresses the nihilistic delusion that her intestines have rotted. The team debates a third antidepressant versus ECT. Which framing is most accurate?

- A third antidepressant combined with an antipsychotic should be tried first — pharmacotherapy is the standard of care before proceeding to procedural interventions.
- ECT is not a last resort — it is first-line for severe, psychotic, catatonic, or life-threatening mood states and in pregnancy. **← keyed correct**
- rTMS is preferred because it avoids anesthesia and has equivalent efficacy to ECT for psychotic depression.
- Palliative consultation is appropriate — treatment resistance and refusal of oral intake indicate a terminal trajectory.

**Rationale.** This patient's presentation meets multiple ECT indications simultaneously. Pharmacotherapy first at any severity, Newer = better, and Hopeless by default all delay a definitive, evidence-based treatment. The mood page states explicitly: 'ECT is not a last resort — it is first-line for psychotic, catatonic, or life-threatening mood states and in pregnancy.' Active suicidality, refusal of food/fluids, psychotic features, and treatment resistance are all named ECT indications.

**Pearl.** ECT is not a last resort — it is first-line for psychotic, catatonic, or life-threatening depression; do not defer it when these criteria are met.

**Stem.** A 45-year-old man with MDD was started on sertraline 50 mg outpatient 4 weeks ago. His PHQ-9 improved from 22 to 18. His outpatient clinician says, 'No response — time to add lithium augmentation.' A student asks what should be verified before changing the plan. Which element of measurement-based, sequential treatment (in the spirit of STAR*D) is most relevant here?

- Whether the patient had adequate sleep — insomnia alone can prevent antidepressant response.
- The patient's family history — poor family response to SSRIs predicts individual non-response.
- Whether the dose was adequate and the duration sufficient before declaring non-response — a PHQ-9 drop from 22 to 18 at 4 weeks may represent ongoing response, not treatment failure. **← keyed correct**
- Whether the patient prefers augmentation — patient preference drives the treatment decision in measurement-based care.

**Rationale.** A dose increase may be preferable to augmentation when partial response is still emerging. Sleep over dose/duration, Pharmacogenomics before dose, and Preference over protocol all bypass the foundational step. STAR*D's model: pick an agent, dose it adequately, and track response systematically before switching or augmenting. A partial response at 4 weeks on a 50 mg sertraline dose has not yet established non-response.

**Pearl.** Sequential treatment (STAR*D): verify adequate dose and adequate duration before switching or augmenting — a partial response is not non-response.

**Stem.** A patient with bipolar I disorder, admitted during a depressive episode, tells the student: 'I don't see why the staff keeps bothering me about sleep — it doesn't affect my moods.' Which response best reflects the clinical evidence?

- Sleep disruption affects only energy and concentration, not mood stability — the patient's assessment is essentially correct.
- Sleep problems are a symptom of bipolar disorder, not a cause — treating the mood disorder will resolve the sleep disruption.
- Sleep medications are the priority — behavioral sleep protection is ineffective without pharmacotherapy.
- Sleep loss both worsens depression and precipitates mania in bipolar disorder — protecting sleep and circadian rhythm is active treatment, not optional self-care. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Sleep = energy only, Symptom not driver, and Pharmacotherapy over milieu all diminish the therapeutic importance of sleep. The mood page is explicit: 'protect sleep and circadian rhythm actively, since sleep loss both worsens depression and precipitates mania.' Sleep protection is a treatment modality, not a lifestyle recommendation.

**Pearl.** Sleep is treatment in bipolar disorder — sleep loss worsens depression AND precipitates mania; protecting circadian rhythm is an active clinical intervention.
