- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `m`, `o`, `o`, `d`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `F`, `o`, `r`, ` `, `a`, `c`, `u`, `t`, `e`, ` `, `m`, `a`, `n`, `i`, `a`, `,`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `d`, `u`, `c`, `e`, ` `, `s`, `t`, `i`, `m`, `u`, `l`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `,`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `s`, `t`, `o`, `r`, `e`, ` `, `s`, `l`, `e`, `e`, `p`, `,`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `s`, `t`, `a`, `r`, `t`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `o`, `p`, `t`, `i`, `m`, `i`, `z`, `e`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `m`, `o`, `o`, `d`, ` `, `s`, `t`, `a`, `b`, `i`, `l`, `i`, `z`, `e`, `r`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `p`, `s`, `y`, `c`, `h`, `o`, `t`, `i`, `c`, `.`

**Stem.** A 28-year-old man with bipolar I disorder is admitted for his first acute manic episode. He has no prior mood stabilizer exposure. Which is the most appropriate pharmacological framework for acute mania management?

- First-line options for acute mania — lithium, valproate, or a second-generation antipsychotic — combined with milieu measures to reduce stimulation and restore sleep. **← keyed correct**
- Start an antidepressant first — addressing the depression component of mixed mania speeds recovery.
- Benzodiazepine monotherapy is appropriate for a first manic episode — avoid mood stabilizers until the diagnosis is confirmed over multiple episodes.
- Clozapine should be considered first because of its superior efficacy in refractory mania.

**Rationale.** Avoid valproate in anyone of childbearing potential regardless of current pregnancy status. Antidepressant for mania, Defer mood stabilizer, and Skip the ladder all misstate the first-line framework. Acute mania is managed with lithium, valproate, or an SGA, combined with milieu interventions — reduce stimulation and restore sleep, since sleep loss itself perpetuates mania.

**Pearl.** Acute mania: lithium, valproate, or SGA + reduce stimulation + restore sleep; antidepressants are contraindicated.

**Stem.** A 58-year-old man with bipolar I disorder is stable on lithium 900 mg daily, level 0.8 mEq/L. He recently started ibuprofen for knee pain and reports new-onset hand tremor and mild mental fogginess for the past week. Most appropriate next step?

- Reassure the patient — tremor is an expected mild lithium side effect that does not indicate toxicity.
- Check a lithium level immediately — NSAIDs reduce renal prostaglandin activity, decreasing lithium clearance; new neurological symptoms in this context require urgent assessment for toxicity. **← keyed correct**
- Stop lithium immediately and restart once the pain resolves — the ibuprofen interaction makes continued lithium unsafe.
- Ibuprofen has no interaction with lithium — evaluate for a new neurological diagnosis.

**Rationale.** Treat the number not the patient, Overreact to interaction, and No drug interaction represent a spectrum of errors around a common drug interaction. The correct action is checking the level urgently — not dismissing the symptoms, not stopping lithium abruptly. The clinical situation maps directly to the page's warning about NSAIDs pushing lithium toward toxicity.

**Pearl.** New neurological symptoms (tremor, fogginess) in a lithium-treated patient after starting an NSAID → check level urgently; NSAIDs reduce lithium clearance.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Which drug classes are known to reduce lithium clearance and push levels toward toxicity?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Opioids, SSRIs, and calcium channel blockers — they alter renal tubular function via serotonergic and adrenergic mechanisms."}, {"key": "B", "t": "NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, and thiazide diuretics — all reduce renal lithium clearance through distinct mechanisms (prostaglandin inhibition, RAAS suppression, sodium depletion with compensatory lithium reabsorption); dehydration has the same effect.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Antipsychotics and anticonvulsants — they compete with lithium for renal tubular transporters."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Beta-blockers — they reduce cardiac output and thereby reduce renal perfusion and lithium clearance."}], "why": "The three drug classes and dehydration each reduce lithium clearance through different mechanisms but the clinical result is the same — higher serum lithium levels. NSAIDs inhibit prostaglandin synthesis, reducing renal blood flow and lithium excretion. ACE inhibitors and ARBs reduce angiotensin-driven sodium retention, causing compensatory lithium reabsorption. Thiazides cause sodium depletion, triggering the same compensation. Dehydration for any reason concentrates lithium by reducing urinary output. Any of these alone can push a therapeutic level into toxicity."}

- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `m`, `o`, `o`, `d`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `S`, `e`, `v`, `e`, `r`, `e`, ` `, `u`, `n`, `i`, `p`, `o`, `l`, `a`, `r`, ` `, `d`, `e`, `p`, `r`, `e`, `s`, `s`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `n`, ` `, `i`, `n`, `c`, `l`, `u`, `d`, `e`, ` `, `m`, `o`, `o`, `d`, `-`, `c`, `o`, `n`, `g`, `r`, `u`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `p`, `s`, `y`, `c`, `h`, `o`, `s`, `i`, `s`, ` `, `(`, `n`, `i`, `h`, `i`, `l`, `i`, `s`, `t`, `i`, `c`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `g`, `u`, `i`, `l`, `t`, `-`, `l`, `a`, `d`, `e`, `n`, ` `, `d`, `e`, `l`, `u`, `s`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `s`, `)`, `.`

**Stem.** A 70-year-old woman is admitted with severe depression. She is convinced her children have died in a car accident — despite video calls with her healthy children — and that her body is 'empty inside.' She is oriented, alert, and attentive. No confusion on exam. Which diagnosis is most consistent with this presentation?

- Schizophrenia — fixed, persistent false beliefs constitute a primary psychotic disorder.
- Delirium with nihilistic ideation — new psychosis in an elderly patient is delirium until proven otherwise.
- Severe major depression with psychotic features — the nihilistic and guilt-laden delusions are mood-congruent and consistent with the depression; orientation and attention are intact. **← keyed correct**
- Schizoaffective disorder — concurrent mood episodes and psychotic features define this diagnosis.

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia, All psychosis in elderly = delirium, and Concurrent = schizoaffective all misdiagnose the presentation. Psychotic depression produces mood-congruent delusions (nihilistic, guilt-laden) within the context of a severe depressive episode — the alert, attentive patient without fluctuating consciousness rules out delirium.

**Pearl.** Mood-congruent psychotic features (nihilistic/guilt-laden delusions) in severe depression = psychotic depression, not schizophrenia — psychosis confined to the mood episode is the discriminator.

**Stem.** A 36-year-old man with 'recurrent unipolar depression' has been on sertraline 100 mg for 8 months. His current episode resolved well, but over the past 2 weeks he has developed markedly decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, and impulsive spending. He is not using substances. Which is the most accurate interpretation?

- The patient is experiencing sertraline discontinuation syndrome — the mania-like symptoms are medication side effects that will resolve spontaneously.
- He has developed a superimposed stimulant use disorder — the pattern is classic methamphetamine intoxication.
- This represents sertraline's therapeutic effect — the energy and motivation improvement of depression recovery can resemble hypomania.
- Antidepressant monotherapy has precipitated a manic switch in a patient likely misdiagnosed as unipolar — the diagnosis should be reconsidered as bipolar disorder and the antidepressant should not be continued as monotherapy. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Side effect attribution, Substance first, and Recovery looks like hypomania all avoid the correct diagnosis. The mood page explicitly states: 'a bipolar depression misread as unipolar can be pushed into mania or rapid cycling by an antidepressant.' This is the clinical manifestation of that process — treatment-emergent mania revealing unrecognized bipolar disorder.

**Pearl.** Manic symptoms emerging on antidepressant monotherapy = probable treatment-emergent switch revealing unrecognized bipolar — not side effects, not recovery.

**Stem.** A 49-year-old man admitted for severe bipolar depression requests discharge on day 6. His mood has partially improved (PHQ-9 from 24 to 17), but he is still sleeping only 4 hours per night, his safety plan is not yet completed, and he does not have an outpatient appointment scheduled. The attending asks the student to frame the clinical decision. Which is most accurate?

- Discharge readiness is functional and safety-based, not calendar-based — incomplete sleep restoration, an incomplete safety plan, and no follow-up appointment are clinical criteria not yet met; continued inpatient care is indicated. **← keyed correct**
- The partial PHQ-9 improvement indicates adequate response — at day 6 the acute risk has passed and discharge is appropriate.
- The patient has the right to leave against medical advice — sign him out and provide a safety net number.
- Six days is within the standard inpatient admission length — continue treatment by the calendar.

**Rationale.** Score = discharge permission, Autonomy over safety, and Calendar over function all substitute a proxy for clinical readiness. The mood page is explicit: 'Discharge readiness is functional and safety-based, not calendar-based: improving mood and resolving acute risk, adequate sleep and intake, an established and tolerated medication, and a concrete follow-up.' None of these criteria are met.

**Pearl.** Discharge readiness in mood disorders is functional and safety-based: sleep restored, safety plan complete, follow-up scheduled — not calendar-driven.

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman in her 14th week of pregnancy presents with severe MDD: active suicidal ideation with a plan, refusal to eat, psychomotor retardation, and a prior excellent response to ECT 4 years ago. The team debates continuing the current antidepressant versus offering ECT. Which statement best reflects the clinical standard?

- ECT is contraindicated in pregnancy due to the risk of uterine contractions during the induced seizure.
- Continue the antidepressant through pregnancy — the benefits of pharmacotherapy always outweigh teratogenic risks in severe depression.
- ECT is a preferred treatment option in this presentation — pregnancy is an explicit ECT indication when medications are undesirable, and this patient also has active suicidality, refusal of oral intake, and a prior good ECT response. **← keyed correct**
- Ketamine infusion should be chosen over ECT — it avoids anesthesia and is equally effective for suicidal ideation in pregnancy.

**Rationale.** Absolute contraindication invented, Pharmacotherapy always first, and Newer = better all distort the evidence. ECT has no absolute contraindications; pregnancy is an ECT indication. This patient has four concurrent indications: active suicidality, refusal of oral intake, pregnancy where medications are less desirable, and prior good ECT response.

**Pearl.** ECT in pregnancy: no absolute contraindication, explicitly indicated when medications are undesirable — especially when there is active suicidality, refusal to eat, or prior ECT response.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why is ECT specifically listed as a preferred option in pregnancy, compared to pharmacotherapy?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "ECT carries no teratogenic risk because the electrical current does not cross the placenta."}, {"key": "B", "t": "ECT avoids systemic fetal drug exposure — the induced seizure under anesthesia has a well-established safety record in pregnancy with obstetric monitoring, whereas fetal pharmacological exposure carries teratogenic and developmental risks that vary by agent.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "ECT is FDA-approved for use in pregnancy, whereas all antidepressants are FDA category D or X."}, {"key": "D", "t": "ECT is faster than pharmacotherapy — rapid treatment is the only advantage, not the avoidance of drug exposure."}], "why": "The clinical advantage of ECT in pregnancy is that the therapeutic mechanism (induced seizure) does not require systemic pharmacological exposure that crosses the placenta. Many psychotropics have established or potential teratogenic risks, particularly during organogenesis, and all agents have some potential for fetal or neonatal effects. ECT with appropriate obstetric monitoring avoids this entirely. The long safety record of ECT in pregnancy is one reason it appears on the indications list alongside the more familiar severity-based indications."}

**Stem.** A 76-year-old woman on hospital day 3 after hip-fracture repair is referred to psychiatry for 'depression.' She is quiet, slow to respond, and sleeps much of the day; her nurse notes she was conversational yesterday evening but is 'flat' this morning. She cannot recite the months of the year backward. Most likely explanation?

- Major depressive episode — recommend starting an antidepressant
- Early dementia unmasked by hospitalization
- Hypoactive delirium — screen for precipitating causes and deliriogenic medications **← keyed correct**
- Expected postoperative fatigue — reassure the team and sign off

**Rationale.** Acute onset, a course that fluctuates across one shift, and failed months-backward — inattention, the cardinal deficit — assemble the CAM picture; low arousal makes it hypoactive delirium, the form most often mislabeled as depression or overlooked because the patient is quiet. Dementia declines over months, not shifts, and 'postoperative fatigue' does not take attention with it. The work now is finding the drivers — infection, metabolic derangement, deliriogenic medications, retention, constipation, pain.

**Pearl.** Hypoactive delirium is the dangerous one: commonly missed, worse outcomes, and easily mislabeled as depression.

**Stem.** A 44-year-old woman with a history of bipolar disorder is found mute and immobile in her room, staring past the examiner. When the examiner positions her arm in an awkward posture, it remains in place for over a minute. A colleague suggests starting haloperidol for presumed psychosis. Best next step?

- Evaluate for catatonia using a structured rating instrument before initiating any antipsychotic **← keyed correct**
- Start haloperidol immediately — mutism and posturing indicate untreated psychosis
- Apply a physical hold for safety while awaiting psychiatric evaluation
- Order a urine drug screen and defer further evaluation to the morning team

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia drives the dangerous action: haloperidol in an unexcluded catatonia can precipitate NMS. Waxy flexibility (sustained posturing) is a catatonic sign — catatonia must be rated before any dopamine blocker is started. Restraint first violates least-restrictive principle; Quiet patient = stable patient defers an urgent evaluation.

**Pearl.** Avoid antipsychotics until catatonia is excluded — they can precipitate or worsen NMS and malignant catatonia; the lorazepam challenge is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

**Stem.** An 82-year-old man on postoperative day 2 after hip arthroplasty is confused and agitated. The Confusion Assessment Method is positive for delirium. He has no history of dementia. Which is the most appropriate initial management step?

- Start scheduled haloperidol — antipsychotics prevent delirium progression in post-surgical patients.
- Implement non-pharmacologic delirium measures — reorientation, sleep-wake protection, early mobilization, sensory restoration — investigate the underlying cause, and deprescribe deliriogenic medications. **← keyed correct**
- Start a benzodiazepine PRN — GABAergic agents calm delirium-related agitation while the cause is sought.
- Order a STAT non-contrast CT head — new confusion in a post-surgical patient indicates intracranial pathology until proven otherwise.

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic is the most dangerous trap — scheduled antipsychotics are not for delirium prevention and carry a mortality warning in older patients with dementia-related psychosis. The reflexive benzodiazepine deepens delirium unless the cause is alcohol or sedative withdrawal. Non-pharmacologic measures — reorientation, sleep-wake cycle protection, early mobilization, sensory aids, deprescribing — are first-line while the cause is found.

**Pearl.** Non-pharmacologic measures (ABCDEF bundle, sensory aids, mobility) are first-line for delirium — antipsychotics are for dangerous agitation only, never for prevention.

**Stem.** A 78-year-old woman with mild Alzheimer disease is admitted from her nursing home after 2 days of worsening confusion. A CAM is positive. She is agitated and picking at her IV. The team asks the student to help select an appropriate PRN medication for dangerous agitation. Which of the following is correct?

- A benzodiazepine is the safest first choice — GABAergic agents are better tolerated than antipsychotics in cognitively impaired older patients.
- Start a scheduled antipsychotic to prevent further deterioration — treating the agitation reduces downstream complications.
- Combine a benzodiazepine with a low-dose antipsychotic for the best coverage of mixed delirium-related agitation.
- If pharmacotherapy is needed after non-pharmacologic measures fail, use the lowest effective antipsychotic dose time-limited with close monitoring; avoid benzodiazepines unless the cause is alcohol or sedative withdrawal. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** The reflexive benzodiazepine and Agitation = antipsychotic are both active. Benzodiazepines are appropriate only for alcohol and sedative withdrawal — in other delirium they worsen confusion and increase aspiration and fall risk. If pharmacotherapy is needed for dangerous agitation after non-pharmacologic measures, a low-dose antipsychotic time-limited is the choice. Polypharmacy before non-pharmacologic management is never the correct framework.

**Pearl.** Benzodiazepines worsen delirium — reserve them for alcohol and sedative withdrawal only; antipsychotics at the lowest effective dose, time-limited, for dangerous agitation.

**Stem.** A 74-year-old man presents with an 18-month history of vivid recurrent visual hallucinations (small animals in the room), episodes of staring with fluctuating cognition, mild rigidity and slowness, and his wife's report that he 'acts out his dreams' and has struck her while asleep. Which dementia subtype does this constellation most strongly suggest?

- Lewy body dementia — the combination of fluctuating cognition, recurrent visual hallucinations, spontaneous parkinsonism, and REM sleep behavior disorder is the characteristic core-feature tetrad. **← keyed correct**
- Behavioral-variant frontotemporal dementia — visual hallucinations and behavioral change point to frontotemporal degeneration.
- Alzheimer disease — amnestic dementia with behavioral symptoms in a 74-year-old is most commonly Alzheimer.
- Vascular dementia — stepwise decline with parkinsonism and motor slowing points to cerebrovascular disease.

**Rationale.** Most common = correct drives most errors here: Alzheimer is most prevalent, but the full LBD tetrad — fluctuating cognition, recurrent visual hallucinations, spontaneous parkinsonism, and REM sleep behavior disorder — is present. FTD shows disinhibition and personality change without this constellation. Vascular dementia follows a stepwise course with focal signs, not this tetrad. Recognizing LBD matters urgently because of its neuroleptic sensitivity.

**Pearl.** LBD tetrad: fluctuating cognition + recurrent visual hallucinations + parkinsonism + REM sleep behavior disorder — recognize it before anyone prescribes an antipsychotic.

**Stem.** A patient with known Lewy body dementia is admitted with agitation and paranoid delusions. A nurse asks whether the team should start haloperidol. The most appropriate response is which of the following?

- Start haloperidol — first-generation antipsychotics are better tolerated in elderly patients than second-generation agents.
- Start olanzapine — second-generation antipsychotics do not carry neuroleptic sensitivity risk in LBD.
- Hold the antipsychotic, flag the LBD diagnosis to the team, pursue non-pharmacologic management first, and reserve quetiapine or pimavanserin if pharmacotherapy is truly unavoidable. **← keyed correct**
- Start risperidone — its lower EPS profile compared to haloperidol makes it the safest antipsychotic for LBD.

**Rationale.** Newer = better in three forms. In LBD, neuroleptic sensitivity can be triggered by any dopamine-blocking agent — the risk is highest with typicals and substantial with high-D2-occupancy SGAs like risperidone and olanzapine. The correct first step is flagging the diagnosis, non-pharmacologic management, and choosing quetiapine or pimavanserin if medication is truly unavoidable.

**Pearl.** Avoid antipsychotics in LBD — especially typicals; if pharmacotherapy is unavoidable, quetiapine or pimavanserin are lower-risk choices.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why is neuroleptic sensitivity so severe in Lewy body dementia?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Alpha-synuclein deposits reduce plasma protein binding of antipsychotics, causing unpredictably high free drug levels."}, {"key": "B", "t": "In LBD, dopaminergic neurons are already severely depleted — antipsychotics block the remaining dopamine receptors, precipitating profound rigidity, autonomic instability, and potentially death.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "LBD patients cannot metabolize antipsychotics at standard adult doses due to age-related hepatic decline."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Visual hallucinations in LBD are cholinergic, not dopaminergic — blocking dopamine has no symptom effect and creates only adverse effects."}], "why": "It is a receptor-depletion problem, not a pharmacokinetic one. LBD selectively destroys dopaminergic neurons in the nigrostriatal pathway, leaving remaining receptors critical for motor function. Blocking them produces an extreme parkinson-like crisis — severe rigidity, autonomic dysregulation, worsening consciousness — that can be fatal. This is why the mechanism matters: it is pharmacodynamic sensitivity, not a dosing issue."}

**Stem.** A 69-year-old man is referred for evaluation of progressive cognitive decline, urinary incontinence that preceded the cognitive changes by several months, and a gait his wife describes as 'shuffling and magnetic — like his feet are stuck to the floor.' MRI is pending. Which diagnosis should be considered first because of its potential reversibility?

- Alzheimer disease — amnestic-predominant decline with gait changes in a 69-year-old is most likely AD.
- Normal-pressure hydrocephalus — the triad of magnetic gait apraxia, cognitive decline, and urinary incontinence is potentially reversible with ventriculoperitoneal shunting. **← keyed correct**
- Vascular dementia — gait dysfunction and cognitive decline indicate stepwise cerebrovascular disease.
- Parkinson disease dementia — gait dysfunction and cognitive decline indicate a Lewy body spectrum disorder.

**Rationale.** Most common = correct drives most errors. The specific triad — wet (urinary incontinence), wacky (cognitive decline), and wobbly (magnetic gait) — is classic NPH, and its potential reversibility with shunting makes it a must-diagnose. The magnetic gait is low-clearance, wide-based, and apraxic — different from Parkinsonian tremor-and-rigidity gait. Early incontinence preceding cognition is unusual for Alzheimer or vascular dementia at this stage.

**Pearl.** NPH triad = wet, wacky, wobbly (urinary incontinence, cognition, magnetic gait) — potentially reversible with ventriculoperitoneal shunting.

**Stem.** Psychiatry is consulted on an 82-year-old man in the surgical ICU on postoperative day 3. He is quiet, slow to respond, and sleeping between nursing checks — his nurse reports he was interactive at yesterday's evening assessment. His CAM is positive. The covering team wonders why this presentation is more concerning than a 'hyperactive' delirious patient. Which statement best explains the clinical risk?

- Hyperactive delirium carries worse outcomes — the agitation, pulling at lines, and fall risk create more immediate danger than a quiet, withdrawn presentation.
- The two subtypes have equivalent outcomes — motor presentation does not predict prognosis; treatment targets the underlying cause regardless of subtype.
- Hypoactive delirium responds better to antipsychotics — the reduced agitation creates a therapeutic window for pharmacologic intervention that hyperactive delirium does not allow.
- Hypoactive delirium carries worse outcomes precisely because it goes unrecognized — the quiet, non-interactive patient does not trigger intervention; it is commonly mislabeled as depression and associated with higher mortality. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Hyperactive = higher risk, Subtypes equivalent, and Hypoactive = better pharmacologic target all invert the clinical reality. The source states: 'The hypoactive form is the one that gets missed, and it carries worse outcomes precisely because no one is alarmed by a quiet patient.' Recognition requires deliberate screening — the CAM is the tool.

**Pearl.** Hypoactive delirium = the dangerous one: commonly missed, worse outcomes, easily mislabeled as depression.

**Stem.** A 68-year-old woman is admitted after 4 months of progressive cognitive decline and withdrawal. Neuropsychological testing suggests a moderate amnestic syndrome. She has no known prior psychiatric or neurological history. Which is the most appropriate first-line laboratory workup before assigning a neurodegenerative diagnosis?

- CBC, CMP, TSH, B12/folate, and consider RPR/HIV — reversible causes must be excluded before a neurodegenerative diagnosis is established. **← keyed correct**
- Anti-NMDA receptor antibody panel — autoimmune encephalitis mimics dementia and should be ruled out first in all new presentations.
- Brain MRI with gadolinium — structural imaging is the single most important diagnostic step for a new dementia presentation.
- APOE genotyping — an APOE ε4 allele would confirm Alzheimer disease and guide management.

**Rationale.** Jump to autoimmune and Imaging before labs both skip the foundational step. The reversible-cause panel — B12/folate, TSH, CMP, and when indicated RPR/HIV — must precede neurodegenerative labeling because these conditions are treatable and clinically indistinguishable at presentation. APOE genotyping is not a diagnostic standard. MRI is appropriate, especially for atypical or rapid presentations, but not before basic labs.

**Pearl.** Order the reversible-cause panel (B12/folate, TSH, CMP, ± RPR/HIV) on every new cognitive presentation before assigning a neurodegenerative diagnosis.

**Stem.** A 78-year-old woman with mild cognitive impairment is admitted for worsening confusion and agitation over the past week. She recently started diphenhydramine nightly for insomnia and oxybutynin for urge incontinence. The student reviews her medication list. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- Add risperidone for the agitation — it is the most evidence-based pharmacotherapy for behavioral symptoms in cognitive impairment.
- Order a 24-hour EEG — medication-induced encephalopathy can produce seizure patterns that explain cognitive worsening.
- Flag diphenhydramine and oxybutynin as high-risk anticholinergic agents for the team, escalate for deprescribing, and recommend non-pharmacologic sleep and bladder strategies. **← keyed correct**
- Reassure the family that mild cognitive impairment commonly worsens under medical stress and will recover once she is discharged.

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic and Face-value reassurance both bypass the diagnosis. Anticholinergic burden is a recognized, reversible driver of cognitive worsening in older adults — diphenhydramine and oxybutynin are among the Beers Criteria's most flagged agents. Jump to testing substitutes cost for the straightforward clinical action. The geriatric psychiatry principle: the drug list is often the diagnosis; deprescribing is active treatment.

**Pearl.** In older adults with cognitive change, the medication list is often the diagnosis — assess anticholinergic burden and deprescribe before adding anything.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why do anticholinergic medications disproportionately impair cognition in older adults?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Older adults have reduced hepatic metabolism, causing higher serum drug levels than predicted for a given dose."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Older adults have reduced cholinergic reserve due to aging and often concurrent dementia — blocking acetylcholine impairs attention and memory in a system already depleted.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Anticholinergic agents cause orthostatic hypotension, reducing cerebral perfusion and producing cognitive symptoms."}, {"key": "D", "t": "The mechanism is peripheral: urinary retention and constipation trigger systemic inflammation that secondarily impairs cognition."}], "why": "It is a pharmacodynamic-reserve problem, not primarily pharmacokinetic. Acetylcholine is essential for attention and memory; older adults — especially those with early Alzheimer or MCI — have fewer cholinergic neurons. Blocking the remaining ones with an anticholinergic produces an outsized, often rapid cognitive effect that clinically mimics dementia progression. This is why cholinesterase inhibitors (which increase acetylcholine) treat AD, and anticholinergics are its pharmacologic enemy."}

**Stem.** A 58-year-old man is brought to the emergency department by his wife, who reports an 18-month history of increasingly disinhibited behavior — making inappropriate sexual comments to strangers, hoarding food, and neglecting hygiene. He has no memory complaints, names and recognizes objects normally, and denies mood symptoms. Neuropsychological testing shows prominent executive dysfunction with intact episodic memory. Most likely diagnosis?

- Bipolar disorder — new-onset mania presents with disinhibition, impulsivity, and executive dysfunction in midlife.
- Behavioral-variant frontotemporal dementia — early disinhibition, socially inappropriate behavior, and executive dysfunction with relative sparing of episodic memory in a younger adult is the classic bvFTD presentation. **← keyed correct**
- Early-onset Alzheimer disease — insidious behavioral change in a 58-year-old is most commonly early-onset AD.
- Personality disorder — disinhibition, impulsivity, and boundary violations reflect a longstanding personality pattern rather than a new neurological process.

**Rationale.** Bipolar by default, Most common = correct, and Personality disorder by default are three forms of the same error: assigning a functional psychiatric diagnosis before considering neurodegeneration. FTD is the dementia of younger adults, presenting with behavior and personality change or progressive language, while sparing episodic memory early — the exact opposite of Alzheimer.

**Pearl.** Frontotemporal dementia: younger onset, behavioral disinhibition or progressive language first, episodic memory spared early — the signature that separates it from Alzheimer.

**Stem.** A 74-year-old woman with no prior psychiatric history is brought to the emergency department by her family. She has developed, over the past month, a fixed belief that her neighbors are poisoning her food and has stopped eating. She is alert, coherent, and oriented. There is no mood disturbance. Which is the most important next step?

- Diagnose late-onset schizophrenia — paranoid delusions in late life unexplained by a mood disorder represent a primary psychotic illness.
- Start an antipsychotic immediately — the refusal to eat constitutes an acute safety emergency requiring empirical treatment.
- Reassure the family that paranoid ideation is a normal variant of cognitive aging.
- Conduct a medical workup to exclude organic causes of new-onset psychosis — including delirium, medication effect, CNS lesion, metabolic derangement, and autoimmune encephalitis — before establishing a primary psychiatric diagnosis. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia, Treat before diagnosis, and Face-value reassurance all skip the foundational step. New-onset psychosis in an older adult without a prior psychiatric history is a medical emergency until proven otherwise. The differential includes delirium, CNS lesion, metabolic cause, autoimmune encephalitis, and medication-induced psychosis — all requiring workup before a primary psychotic diagnosis is made.

**Pearl.** New psychosis in an older adult is a medical emergency until proven otherwise — work up before labeling or treating.

**Stem.** A 35-year-old man with bipolar disorder screens positive for catatonia on the Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale — he is mute, maintains awkward arm postures for extended periods, and stares past the examiner. The team has appropriately held the antipsychotic. What is the indicated next step?

- Administer a lorazepam challenge (IV or IM) — meaningful improvement in catatonic signs after benzodiazepines supports the diagnosis and begins treatment; benzodiazepines are first-line for catatonia. **← keyed correct**
- Initiate a clozapine trial — persistent psychiatric symptoms after stopping the antipsychotic indicate a need for a clozapine evaluation.
- Restart the antipsychotic at a lower dose — catatonic symptoms worsen without dopamine blockade and the patient needs partial antipsychotic coverage.
- Apply soft restraints and obtain an urgent head CT to rule out structural causes before proceeding with any treatment.

**Rationale.** Clozapine for catatonia, Restart antipsychotic, and Restraint before treatment all defer or undermine first-line treatment. The catatonia source states: 'A test dose of lorazepam is both a diagnostic maneuver and the start of treatment — a meaningful, often rapid improvement supports the diagnosis and predicts response.' Benzodiazepines are first-line; ECT is for benzodiazepine-refractory or malignant catatonia.

**Pearl.** After positive catatonia screen + holding the antipsychotic → lorazepam challenge next: both diagnostic and therapeutic.

**Stem.** A 29-year-old woman with schizophrenia has been mute and posturing for 2 days. Today she is found febrile to 39.6°C, diaphoretic, hypotensive, with lead-pipe rigidity and a markedly elevated CK. Her antipsychotic was increased yesterday. Best next step?

- Administer additional lorazepam challenge doses and observe on the psychiatric unit — the rigidity suggests catatonic stupor responding to benzodiazepines.
- Increase the antipsychotic — this is a catatonic variant of treatment-resistant psychosis requiring more aggressive dopamine blockade.
- Stop all antipsychotics immediately, transfer to a medical unit for hemodynamic monitoring and supportive care, and obtain urgent psychiatric and neurology consultation — ECT should be considered for malignant or refractory catatonia. **← keyed correct**
- Treat empirically for sepsis — fever, hypotension, and elevated CK indicate bacterial infection requiring antibiotics.

**Rationale.** Underestimate the emergency and Agitation = antipsychotic are the two clinical catastrophes. This is NMS/malignant catatonia: fever, autonomic instability, lead-pipe rigidity, elevated CK after antipsychotic escalation. The correct sequence is stop all antipsychotics, transfer to a medical unit, and emergently consider ECT for malignant or refractory catatonia — not more benzodiazepines on the psychiatric unit and certainly not more antipsychotic.

**Pearl.** Malignant catatonia overlaps with NMS: fever + rigidity + elevated CK + autonomic instability after antipsychotic exposure — stop antipsychotics, transfer to medical, and consider ECT urgently.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does ECT have a role in malignant catatonia when benzodiazepines have not been effective?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "ECT provides dopamine agonism that directly counteracts antipsychotic receptor blockade."}, {"key": "B", "t": "ECT is used because it avoids all pharmacologic agents, preventing further receptor blockade."}, {"key": "C", "t": "ECT directly treats the catatonic syndrome regardless of etiology — it is the definitive treatment for malignant or benzodiazepine-refractory catatonia and its effectiveness does not depend on removing the antipsychotic.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "ECT is a last resort used only after a second antipsychotic trial has failed in treatment-resistant psychosis."}], "why": "ECT acts directly on the motor and autonomic systems driving catatonia — its mechanism bypasses the pharmacologic state that triggered the syndrome. The BAP 2023 catatonia guideline endorses ECT for severe, refractory, or malignant catatonia as a treatment that should not be delayed when the patient is deteriorating. The example here illustrates this: benzodiazepines are insufficient for malignant presentations; ECT can be lifesaving even when the antipsychotic cannot be fully cleared."}

**Stem.** An 82-year-old man with moderate-to-severe Alzheimer disease is currently on donepezil 10 mg daily. His neurologist asks the student to identify what pharmacotherapy optimization is supported by evidence for this stage of disease.

- Increase donepezil to the maximum tolerated dose — dose escalation of cholinesterase inhibitors alone is the evidence-based approach for moderate-to-severe disease.
- Add memantine — combination of a cholinesterase inhibitor and memantine (NMDA antagonist) is the evidence-based pharmacotherapy for moderate-to-severe Alzheimer disease. **← keyed correct**
- Switch to a rivastigmine patch — transdermal delivery is more effective than oral donepezil in advanced disease.
- Start a monoclonal anti-amyloid antibody — these agents are now standard of care for all stages of Alzheimer.

**Rationale.** Dose escalation alone and Newer = better in two forms. For mild-to-moderate AD, cholinesterase inhibitors alone are the standard. For moderate-to-severe AD, adding memantine is the evidence-based optimization. Anti-amyloid antibodies are specialist-initiated, amyloid-confirmed, and restricted to early disease.

**Pearl.** Cholinesterase inhibitors for mild-to-moderate AD; add memantine for moderate-to-severe disease.

**Stem.** A 73-year-old woman with recurrent major depression (last treated 5 years ago) is referred for cognitive evaluation after 3 months of 'memory problems.' On formal cognitive testing she scores in the impaired range on delayed recall but consistently answers 'I don't know' rather than confabulating; she is slow to initiate answers and appears effortfully disengaged. She remains alert and attentive, and scores 22 on the PHQ-9. Most appropriate next step?

- Diagnose major neurocognitive disorder — the cognitive test scores confirm a dementia syndrome independent of mood.
- Start a cholinesterase inhibitor — the episodic memory impairment indicates Alzheimer-spectrum disease requiring pharmacotherapy.
- Order an amyloid PET scan — direct amyloid visualization definitively distinguishes depression-associated cognitive impairment from Alzheimer disease.
- Prioritize treatment of the depressive episode and reassess cognitive function after remission — the pattern of 'I don't know' responses, effortful disengagement, and a PHQ-9 of 22 is consistent with pseudodementia that may resolve with antidepressant treatment. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Scores = diagnosis, Treat the number not the patient, and Jump to advanced imaging all bypass the pseudodementia maneuver. 'I don't know' responses with effortful disengagement and preserved alertness — rather than confabulation and unawareness — is the clinical signature of depression-associated cognitive impairment (Caine 1981). The correct next step is antidepressant treatment and follow-up cognitive assessment.

**Pearl.** Pseudodementia: 'I don't know' with effortful disengagement and preserved alertness — treat the depression first and reassess cognition; scores may fully normalize.

**Stem.** A 16-year-old with severe anorexia nervosa has been restrictive for 4 months and is medically admitted with critically low weight. Before nutritional rehabilitation begins, which approach most directly reduces the risk of refeeding syndrome?

- Start full nutritional rehabilitation immediately at a standard adult caloric target to restore weight as rapidly as possible.
- Give prophylactic anxiolytics and monitor for 24 hours before introducing nutrition — anxiety increases metabolic demand in malnourished patients.
- Correct all electrolyte abnormalities to normal before introducing any nutrition — electrolyte imbalances worsen if nutrition begins before they are stable.
- Start caloric refeeding at a conservative initial target and advance slowly; administer thiamine before or with carbohydrate and check phosphate, potassium, and magnesium at least daily early in refeeding. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Faster is safer, Sedation before nutrition, and Normalize first all create delay or metabolic risk. The source's 'start low, go slow' principle is the safety framework: conservative start, daily electrolyte checks, and thiamine before carbohydrate to prevent Wernicke encephalopathy.

**Pearl.** Refeeding syndrome prevention: start low, go slow, give thiamine before carbohydrate, check phosphate daily.

**Stem.** A 27-year-old mother, 9 days postpartum, arrives with acute confusion and delusions that her infant 'was sent to test her faith.' Her husband reports hours of apparent lucidity followed by rapid deterioration. The diagnosis of postpartum psychosis is made. Which management priority is most immediately critical?

- Refer for outpatient psychiatry follow-up and prescribe a low-dose antipsychotic — postpartum psychosis in first-time mothers without prior mental illness typically resolves with minimal intervention.
- Immediate psychiatric hospitalization — postpartum psychosis is a psychiatric emergency; explicitly assess and restrict unsupervised access to the infant, start acute pharmacotherapy (lithium or antipsychotic), and protect sleep. **← keyed correct**
- Prioritize medical workup to rule out thyroid dysfunction and eclampsia before any psychiatric treatment — pharmacotherapy is premature until medical causes are excluded.
- The lucid interval between episodes provides reassurance — waxing-and-waning symptoms are inconsistent with psychosis; this is most likely a severe postpartum adjustment reaction.

**Rationale.** Outpatient sufficient, Medical workup before treatment, and Lucid interval = reassurance all miss the emergency framing. The source states: 'POSTPARTUM PSYCHOSIS IS A PSYCHIATRIC EMERGENCY: rapid onset, waxing/waning presentation, and real risk to both mother and infant — admit and treat urgently.' The safety assessment must explicitly include the infant.

**Pearl.** Postpartum psychosis = immediate hospitalization, restrict unsupervised infant access, start lithium or antipsychotic urgently.

- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `e`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `n`, `g`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `R`, `e`, `f`, `e`, `e`, `d`, `i`, `n`, `g`, ` `, `s`, `y`, `n`, `d`, `r`, `o`, `m`, `e`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `m`, `p`, `l`, `i`, `c`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `a`, `t`, ` `, `k`, `i`, `l`, `l`, `s`, `:`, ` `, `h`, `y`, `p`, `o`, `p`, `h`, `o`, `s`, `p`, `h`, `a`, `t`, `e`, `m`, `i`, `a`, ` `, `i`, `s`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `h`, `a`, `l`, `l`, `m`, `a`, `r`, `k`, `;`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `d`, `a`, `n`, `g`, `e`, `r`, ` `, `i`, `s`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `r`, `d`, `i`, `a`, `c`, ` `, `f`, `a`, `i`, `l`, `u`, `r`, `e`, `,`, ` `, `a`, `r`, `r`, `h`, `y`, `t`, `h`, `m`, `i`, `a`, `,`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `s`, `p`, `i`, `r`, `a`, `t`, `o`, `r`, `y`, ` `, `f`, `a`, `i`, `l`, `u`, `r`, `e`, `,`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `d`, `e`, `l`, `i`, `r`, `i`, `u`, `m`, `.`, ` `, `S`, `u`, `p`, `p`, `l`, `e`, `m`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `p`, `h`, `o`, `s`, `p`, `h`, `a`, `t`, `e`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `g`, `i`, `v`, `e`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `i`, `a`, `m`, `i`, `n`, `e`, ` `, `b`, `e`, `f`, `o`, `r`, `e`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `w`, `i`, `t`, `h`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `r`, `b`, `o`, `h`, `y`, `d`, `r`, `a`, `t`, `e`, `.`

**Stem.** A 17-year-old with severe anorexia nervosa has been starved for 4 months and is starting nutritional rehabilitation. On day 3 of refeeding, her phosphate drops to 2.1 mg/dL. She is also mildly confused and has new-onset leg weakness. What is the most important diagnosis to recognize?

- Refeeding syndrome — the metabolic shift from catabolic to anabolic metabolism drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells; hypophosphatemia is the hallmark and can cause cardiac failure, arrhythmia, respiratory failure, and delirium. **← keyed correct**
- Electrolyte depletion from malnutrition alone — she was already malnourished on admission, so the low phosphate reflects pre-existing deficiency rather than a refeeding process.
- Conversion disorder — the confusion and leg weakness represent a functional neurological symptom disorder superimposed on the eating disorder.
- Thiamine deficiency — the neurological symptoms indicate Wernicke encephalopathy from B1 deficiency.

**Rationale.** Pre-existing deficiency only, Functional explanation for metabolic emergency, and Thiamine over phosphate all miss the refeeding-specific mechanism. The page calls refeeding syndrome 'the complication that kills' — hypophosphatemia is the hallmark. Note that D (thiamine) is also clinically relevant and the page specifically says 'give thiamine before or with carbohydrate' — but the presenting electrolyte abnormality and timing point to refeeding syndrome as the primary diagnosis.

**Pearl.** Refeeding syndrome: watch phosphate — the hallmark is hypophosphatemia after starting nutrition in the severely malnourished; start low, go slow; supplement phosphate; give thiamine before carbs.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does hypophosphatemia develop after initiating refeeding in a severely malnourished patient, rather than during the starvation period itself?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Carbohydrate refeeding increases renal phosphate excretion — the kidneys preferentially excrete phosphate when insulin levels rise."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Refeeding shifts metabolism from catabolic to anabolic — glucose and insulin drive phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells for new cellular synthesis; since total body stores are depleted from malnutrition, the extracellular pool drops precipitously.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Starvation preserves phosphate by reducing cellular metabolic demand — refeeding simply re-activates the pre-existing phosphate deficiency that was masked."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Thiamine deficiency prevents phosphate absorption from the gut — refeeding exposes this deficiency because oral phosphate intake increases."}], "why": "During starvation, catabolism releases phosphate from cells into the extracellular space, partially maintaining serum levels despite total body depletion. When refeeding begins, glucose triggers insulin release, which drives phosphate (along with potassium and magnesium) into cells for anabolic processes (glycolysis, ATP synthesis, protein synthesis). This intracellular shift drops extracellular phosphate rapidly — in an already-depleted patient, the serum level can fall to dangerous levels within 24–72 hours of starting nutrition. This is why refeeding syndrome occurs early in the refeeding course and why the most malnourished patients are at highest risk."}

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman is referred for 'weight-related anxiety.' She is at normal weight and denies dieting. However, she endorses recurrent binge episodes followed by self-induced vomiting several times weekly, and she reports that her self-worth is almost entirely based on her weight and shape. On exam, you note calluses on her right hand knuckles. Which diagnosis should be suspected?

- Anorexia nervosa, binge-purging subtype — the purging behavior and weight preoccupation are classic for anorexia.
- Binge-eating disorder — binge episodes with distress are the defining feature; no compensatory behavior is required.
- Bulimia nervosa — normal weight does not rule out bulimia; the binge-purge cycle, weight/shape self-evaluation, and Russell's sign (knuckle calluses) are the diagnostic markers. **← keyed correct**
- Purging disorder — recurrent purging in the absence of objectively large binge episodes, with weight/shape concern, is classified as purging disorder.

**Rationale.** Normal weight rules out AN, Binge = BED, and Normal weight = malingering all miss the defining features. The page: 'Bulimia nervosa — weight is often normal or high — a normal weight does not rule it out. Look for Russell's sign (knuckle calluses), parotid enlargement, and dental enamel erosion.' All three examination findings distinguish BN from BED: the presence of compensatory behaviors (purging) is the key.

**Pearl.** Normal weight DOES NOT rule out bulimia nervosa — look for binge-purge cycle + Russell's sign (knuckle calluses) + parotid swelling + dental erosion.

**Stem.** A 28-year-old woman who delivered 6 days ago is brought by her husband with confusion, talking to people who aren't there, and expressing beliefs that her baby 'was sent to test her faith.' She had been laughing and crying alternately. She was seen yesterday and appeared fine for two hours. What is the most critical diagnosis to consider?

- Baby blues — mild postpartum mood changes are expected in the first 2 weeks and are self-limited.
- Postpartum depression — the mood lability and behavioral change represent a perinatal major depressive episode.
- Postpartum psychosis — this is a psychiatric emergency; abrupt onset within days of delivery, waxing-and-waning course, psychotic features centered on the infant, and the lucid interval does not provide reassurance. **← keyed correct**
- Medical emergency — new psychosis postpartum indicates eclampsia or infection until proven otherwise.

**Rationale.** Baby blues by default, Postpartum depression default, and Medical cause only all fail to identify postpartum psychosis as the emergency. The page: 'Postpartum psychosis is a psychiatric emergency: rapid onset, waxing/waning presentation, and real risk to both mother and infant — admit and treat urgently.' The lucid interval yesterday is explicitly stated to not provide reassurance: 'A lucid interval does not rule out psychosis — the waxing/waning course is part of the illness.'

**Pearl.** Postpartum psychosis is a psychiatric emergency — abrupt onset, waxing/waning course, infant-focused delusions within days of delivery; a lucid interval does NOT rule it out.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "What psychiatric history most strongly predicts postpartum psychosis, and why does this influence the medication decision at discharge?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "A history of major depressive disorder — postpartum psychosis is an extension of unipolar depression and responds best to antidepressants."}, {"key": "B", "t": "A history of bipolar disorder — postpartum psychosis is strongly linked to bipolar spectrum illness; stopping maintenance mood stabilizer roughly doubles the risk of postpartum relapse.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "A history of schizophrenia — postpartum psychosis is simply a schizophrenic relapse triggered by the hormonal changes of delivery."}, {"key": "D", "t": "No prior psychiatric history predicts postpartum psychosis — it is idiopathic and equally likely in any postpartum patient."}], "why": "Postpartum psychosis is strongly linked to bipolar disorder — patients with bipolar disorder have a risk approximately 100-fold higher than the general population. This matters for discharge because 'stopping maintenance medication roughly doubles postpartum relapse (pooled ~35% overall; ~66% off medication vs ~23% on prophylaxis).' The medication-stop decision in a postpartum patient with bipolar disorder is itself a high-risk intervention — 'stop everything' is not a safe default."}

**Stem.** A 45-year-old woman is referred for psychiatric consultation for 'medically unexplained' chest pain and fatigue. Her symptoms are real and distressing, her workup is negative, and she spends hours daily researching health threats and 4–5 hours daily focused on her symptoms. Which DSM-5-TR principle is most relevant to the diagnosis?

- Somatic symptom disorder requires that the symptoms have no identifiable organic cause — the diagnosis is made only after medical explanation is definitively excluded.
- Somatic symptom disorder requires at least 3 distinct somatic complaints across different organ systems.
- These complaints are best diagnosed as illness anxiety disorder because the patient's main symptom is health preoccupation rather than multiple somatic symptoms.
- DSM-5-TR defines somatic symptom disorder by the excessive thoughts, feelings, and behaviors around symptoms — not by whether those symptoms are medically explained; a patient with a confirmed medical diagnosis can still meet SSD criteria if her response to symptoms is disproportionate. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** SSD = medically unexplained, Multiple organ systems required, and Preoccupation = IAD all misstate the DSM-5-TR diagnosis. The page's first pearl: 'SSD is defined by the excessive response to symptoms, not by whether they're medically explained (a key DSM-5-TR shift from medically unexplained).' This is a tested concept: the diagnosis no longer hinges on medical explanation.

**Pearl.** SSD (DSM-5-TR): defined by excessive thoughts/feelings/behaviors about symptoms — NOT by whether the symptoms are medically unexplained; a confirmed medical illness can coexist with SSD.

**Stem.** A 36-year-old woman is admitted with a 3-day history of right-sided paralysis and inability to walk. Neurological workup is negative. When the examiner tests voluntary right hip extension, extension is absent; however, strong right hip extension is felt involuntarily when the patient flexes the left hip against resistance (positive Hoover sign). Which statement about the diagnosis is most accurate?

- Functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion) is diagnosed here by a positive examination finding — the Hoover sign; this is a rule-in diagnosis, not solely a diagnosis of exclusion. **← keyed correct**
- The diagnosis requires a psychiatric interview confirming psychosocial stress as the precipitant before a neurological cause can be excluded.
- The diagnosis requires a complete neurological workup including MRI, EMG, and nerve conduction studies before conversion can be considered.
- The positive examination finding is unreliable — the Hoover sign has poor sensitivity and should not anchor a diagnosis.

**Rationale.** Psychiatric cause required, Full workup before conversion, and Hoover sign unreliable all delay diagnosis or require unnecessary conditions. The page is explicit: 'Conversion/functional neurological disorder is a rule-in diagnosis — Hoover sign, tremor entrainment, non-epileptic seizures. Name conversion by its positive signs — don't make it a diagnosis of exclusion alone.'

**Pearl.** Functional neurological disorder (conversion) is diagnosed by POSITIVE examination signs (Hoover sign, tremor entrainment, give-way weakness) — not solely by exclusion; DSM-5 removed the psychological precipitant requirement.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why did DSM-5 remove the requirement for an identified psychological stressor from the diagnosis of functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion)?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "To make the diagnosis easier to code — the removal was an administrative convenience rather than a clinical improvement."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Because a psychological precipitant is not reliably identifiable in all cases, and requiring it was clinician-attributed (confabulated) rather than patient-reported; the diagnosis now rests on the neurological examination finding, which is objective.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Because DSM-5 de-medicalized all psychiatric diagnoses — the psychological stressor requirement was removed from all diagnoses in the somatic section."}, {"key": "D", "t": "To increase the sensitivity of the diagnosis — more patients qualify without requiring documentation of a precipitant."}], "why": "The pre-DSM-5 requirement for an identified psychological stressor was criticized because (1) clinicians often attributed stress confabulatorily rather than patients actually reporting a clear precipitant, (2) it was unreliable across clinicians, and (3) it implied that the absence of an identified stressor could falsely reassure about the diagnosis. DSM-5 shifted the criteria to rest on the positive neurological examination finding — an objective, replicable observation — which better separates functional from structural neurological disease and removes the subjective psychological stressor requirement."}

**Stem.** A 42-year-old woman has been hospitalized multiple times for recurrent infections. Staff have noted that her central line infections always follow nursing shifts during which she is left alone with the IV port. A camera reveals that she is injecting herself with contaminated fluid. She appears distressed when confronted and says, 'I just need doctors to take care of me.' There is no evidence of secondary gain (no pending disability, no legal issue). Which diagnosis is most consistent?

- Malingering — the deliberate production of symptoms for secondary gain is the defining feature.
- Somatic symptom disorder — the recurrent hospitalizations and the distress of the patient indicate a pattern of disproportionate health concern.
- Conversion disorder — the pattern of self-limited infections with resolution suggests a functional mechanism.
- Factitious disorder imposed on self — conscious falsification or induction of illness motivated by assuming the sick role, without external incentive; 'I need doctors to take care of me' is the factitious driver. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Deliberate = malingering, Recurrent admissions = SSD, and Episodic = functional all apply the wrong framework. The page's diagnostic table: 'Factitious disorder — conscious falsification or induction of illness (self or another) motivated by assuming the sick role, without external reward.' The two-axis rule: 'Separate on two axes: conscious? and external incentive? Factitious = internal motive (sick role); malingering = external incentive (and is not a disorder).'

**Pearl.** Factitious = conscious, internal motive (sick role); malingering = conscious, external incentive (money/drugs/legal); neither has unconscious somatic symptoms.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "A caregiver is suspected of intentionally inducing illness in her 6-year-old child to maintain hospital access and medical attention. Which specific diagnosis and clinical obligation apply?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Malingering by proxy — the external incentive (social support, housing) makes this malingering rather than factitious disorder; no mandatory reporting obligation applies."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Factitious disorder imposed on another (medical child abuse) — this is a form of child abuse and a mandatory reporting obligation; protecting the child takes priority over confronting the caregiver.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Munchausen syndrome by proxy — this is a historical term; no specific mandatory reporting obligation exists because psychiatric diagnosis does not create reporting duties."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Somatic symptom disorder imposed by parent — the parent's excessive health preoccupation shapes the child's medical utilization; no reporting duty applies."}], "why": "The current DSM-5-TR term is 'factitious disorder imposed on another' (formerly Munchausen by proxy). The page explicitly states it is a form of abuse: 'Factitious disorder imposed on another (medical child abuse) is reportable.' The clinical obligation is to protect the dependent (the child) and file a mandatory report — this takes precedence over psychiatric consultation, confrontation, or treatment of the caregiver. The page cross-references the ethics and law teaching: 'Imposed on another (formerly Munchausen by proxy) is a form of abuse — report it.'"}

**Stem.** A 55-year-old Puerto Rican woman is brought to the emergency department by her family. She is hyperventilating, trembling, shouting uncontrollably, and has fallen to the floor. Her family says this has happened before after a stressful family argument. Her vitals are normal and she recovers fully within 30 minutes. Which culturally informed interpretation is most accurate?

- This is a panic disorder episode — the hyperventilation and trembling meet the physiological criteria for a panic attack.
- This is a functional neurological disorder — the shouting and falling indicate a non-epileptic seizure.
- This may be ataque de nervios — a Latino cultural concept of distress characterized by trembling, shouting, hyperventilation, and sometimes falling, typically precipitated by family stress; cultural inquiry is essential before applying a Western diagnostic label. **← keyed correct**
- This is malingering — the rapid recovery and interpersonal context suggest the episode served a social function.

**Rationale.** Symptoms match panic, Motor symptoms = functional seizure, and Socially embedded = malingering all bypass cultural inquiry. The page: 'Cultural concepts of distress (e.g., ataque de nervios) can mimic panic or psychosis — ask before you label.' Using Kleinman's explanatory-model questions would surface the patient's interpretation and cultural meaning before assigning a diagnosis.

**Pearl.** Ataque de nervios: culturally patterned distress in Latino communities — trembling, shouting, hyperventilation, falling, stress-triggered — can be misread as panic, seizure, or psychosis without cultural inquiry.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "What is the purpose of Kleinman's explanatory-model questions in cross-cultural clinical encounters?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "To confirm that the patient's illness understanding matches the biomedical diagnosis — explaining any discrepancy corrects cultural misunderstanding."}, {"key": "B", "t": "To elicit the patient's own framework for what the illness means, what caused it, what they fear, and what treatment they expect — surfacing beliefs and expectations that shape adherence and therapeutic relationship.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "To determine whether the patient requires a medical interpreter — the questions reveal whether language is the primary communication barrier."}, {"key": "D", "t": "To screen for psychosis — patients who attribute illness to supernatural causes are flagged for psychiatric assessment."}], "why": "Kleinman's explanatory-model questions ('What do you call this problem? What do you think caused it? How does it affect you? What are you most afraid of? What treatment do you expect?') are not about correcting the patient's beliefs or screening for pathology. They are about surfacing the patient's illness framework so the clinician understands what the illness means to the patient — beliefs about cause, significance, and expected treatment that directly influence adherence, trust, and therapeutic planning. Cultural concepts of distress may emerge through these questions that would otherwise be mislabeled as panic or psychosis."}

**Stem.** A 34-year-old Black man presents with paranoid ideation, disorganized thinking, and social withdrawal that began 3 months ago. A student suggests schizophrenia. The attending asks the student to consider a documented cultural bias. Which statement reflects the clinical evidence?

- Black patients present with more severe schizophrenia symptoms on average, which explains the higher diagnostic rate.
- The diagnostic difference reflects differential rates of schizophrenia across demographic groups — epidemiological data confirm higher prevalence in this population.
- Cultural mistrust in the clinical encounter leads Black patients to endorse more psychotic symptoms on screening, creating a false-positive signal.
- Black patients are disproportionately over-diagnosed with schizophrenia and under-diagnosed with mood disorders relative to comparable presentations — a documented clinician bias that is not explained by symptom profile or true prevalence differences. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Severity explains disparity, Epidemiology explains disparity, and Patient behavior explains disparity all misattribute the cause. The page is explicit: 'Black (and some other minority) patients are disproportionately over-diagnosed with schizophrenia and under-diagnosed with mood disorders relative to comparable presentations — a documented bias to guard against.' This is a patient-safety issue that requires active self-monitoring.

**Pearl.** Documented bias: Black patients are over-diagnosed with schizophrenia and under-diagnosed with mood disorders — not due to prevalence differences, but clinician diagnostic bias; guard against it.

**Stem.** A 30-year-old mother, 3 weeks postpartum, scores a 14 on the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS). She is distressed and anxious. The student says, 'She screened positive for postpartum depression — start sertraline.' The attending asks what the student may have missed. Which is most accurate?

- The EPDS is not validated for postpartum use — use the PHQ-9 instead.
- A score of 14 on the EPDS requires hospitalization — outpatient sertraline is below the level of care indicated.
- The EPDS screens for depression and anxiety but does NOT detect psychotic or manic symptoms — explicitly ask about hallucinations, delusions, and manic symptoms before assuming postpartum depression rather than psychosis or bipolar relapse. **← keyed correct**
- Sertraline is contraindicated in the first 4 weeks postpartum — the student should recommend a different SSRI.

**Rationale.** EPDS not validated, EPDS score mandates hospitalization, and Sertraline contraindicated all misstate the evidence. The page's explicit pearl: 'The EPDS screens depression, not psychosis — ask about delusions and perceptual disturbance directly.' The student's error is treating a positive depression screen as a complete psychiatric assessment — the EPDS misses psychosis and mania by design.

**Pearl.** EPDS screens for depression and anxiety — it does NOT detect psychosis or mania; always ask explicitly about delusions, hallucinations, and manic symptoms in every postpartum patient.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "A postpartum patient describes recurring intrusive thoughts of accidentally dropping her baby, which she finds horrifying. She does not act on these thoughts and goes out of her way to avoid holding the baby near stairs. What is the correct interpretation, and how does it differ from a concerning psychotic feature?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "This is a command hallucination instructing her to harm the baby — she requires emergency admission and a safety plan for infant protection."}, {"key": "B", "t": "This is postpartum OCD — the intrusive thoughts are ego-dystonic (horrifying to her), and she avoids situations that could lead to the feared outcome; this differs from psychotic phenomena — voices experienced as external or beliefs about the infant held with delusional conviction, which are an emergency regardless of how distressed the mother is by them — and the correct response is clinical support and reassurance, not emergency separation from the baby.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "This is a normal postpartum anxiety response — new mothers universally have intrusive thoughts about infant safety, which resolves without treatment."}, {"key": "D", "t": "This is a delusion of persecution — the belief that she might harm her child is a paranoid misinterpretation that requires antipsychotic treatment."}], "why": "Postpartum OCD intrusive thoughts are a critically important differential. The page states: 'Postpartum OCD intrusive thoughts are ego-dystonic and not command hallucinations — distinguish them to avoid both over- and under-reaction.' Key discrimination: (1) Ego-dystonic: the patient is horrified by the thoughts and does not want to act on them. (2) She avoids, not approaches, situations that could lead to the feared outcome. (3) Psychotic phenomena are experienced as external perceptions (voices) or held with delusional conviction; the mother's distress does not rule them out, and risk tracks conviction and appraisal of the experience, not ego-syntonicity. Mislabeling postpartum OCD as command hallucinations leads to emergency separation and antipsychotic treatment of a patient who needs reassurance and OCD-specific treatment."}

**Stem.** A patient repeatedly produces factitious illness — contaminating urine samples and injecting herself to cause infection — with no external reward, apparently to occupy the sick role. Best diagnosis?

- Malingering for external incentive
- Somatic symptom disorder
- Illness anxiety disorder
- Factitious disorder imposed on self **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Factitious disorder is intentional falsification/induction of illness to assume the sick role WITHOUT external incentive; malingering has an external incentive; SSD/illness anxiety are not intentionally produced.

**Pearl.** Factitious = deliberate, sick-role, no external gain; malingering = deliberate, external gain; SSD = not intentional.

**Stem.** Fourteen months after her spouse's death, a woman has persistent intense yearning and preoccupation with the deceased, difficulty accepting the death, avoidance of reminders, emotional numbness, and a feeling that life is meaningless. Symptoms impair function and exceed her cultural norms. Which DSM-5-TR diagnosis best fits?

- Prolonged grief disorder **← keyed correct**
- Normal bereavement
- Major depressive disorder
- Adjustment disorder

**Rationale.** Prolonged grief disorder is grief-specific: at least 12 months after the death of a close person in adults, intense yearning or preoccupation plus additional grief symptoms causes impairment and exceeds cultural norms. MDD can co-occur, but grief centers on yearning and preoccupation with the deceased.

**Pearl.** Prolonged grief disorder: persistent yearning/preoccupation plus grief-specific symptoms beyond 12 months in adults, with impairment beyond cultural norms.

### qb_otherdx_012

- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `s`, `l`, `e`, `e`, `p`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `N`, `a`, `r`, `c`, `o`, `l`, `e`, `p`, `s`, `y`, `.`

**Stem.** A 20-year-old has months of overwhelming daytime sleepiness with sudden, brief episodes of bilateral leg weakness triggered by laughter, plus vivid hallucinations while falling asleep and episodes of waking paralysis. Best diagnosis?

- Obstructive sleep apnea
- Narcolepsy (type 1, with cataplexy) **← keyed correct**
- A primary psychotic disorder
- Generalized anxiety disorder

**Rationale.** The narcolepsy tetrad — excessive daytime sleepiness, cataplexy (emotion-triggered muscle-tone loss), sleep paralysis, and hypnagogic hallucinations — with low CSF orexin/hypocretin defines narcolepsy type 1; MSLT shows short REM latency.

**Pearl.** Narcolepsy: sleepiness + cataplexy + sleep paralysis + hypnagogic hallucinations; low orexin.

**Stem.** A woman develops sudden left-arm paralysis after an argument; exam shows a positive Hoover sign and findings inconsistent with any neuroanatomic lesion, and workup is normal. Best diagnosis?

- Malingering for external incentive
- Factitious disorder imposed on self
- Functional neurological symptom disorder **← keyed correct**
- Acute ischemic stroke

**Rationale.** Neurologic symptoms with internal inconsistency incompatible with recognized disease, such as a positive Hoover sign, define functional neurological symptom disorder when not intentionally produced. It is a rule-in diagnosis based on positive signs, not merely normal tests.

**Pearl.** Functional neurological symptom disorder is ruled in by positive inconsistent signs, not by accusation or exclusion alone.

- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `d`, `i`, `s`, `s`, `o`, `c`, `i`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `v`, `e`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `D`, `i`, `s`, `s`, `o`, `c`, `i`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `v`, `e`, ` `, `i`, `d`, `e`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `t`, `y`, ` `, `d`, `i`, `s`, `o`, `r`, `d`, `e`, `r`, `.`

**Stem.** A woman with a severe childhood trauma history has recurrent gaps in memory for everyday events and describes two distinct identity states with different names and behaviors that others have witnessed. Best diagnosis?

- Borderline personality disorder
- Malingering for external gain
- Schizophrenia with primary psychosis
- Dissociative identity disorder **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Two or more distinct personality states plus recurrent amnesia beyond ordinary forgetting, strongly associated with severe early trauma, defines dissociative identity disorder — commonly confused with (but distinct from) schizophrenia.

**Pearl.** DID = ≥2 distinct identity states + amnesia, linked to severe early trauma; not schizophrenia.

**Stem.** A 26-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder tells her student doctor that she trusts only her and that the other staff 'just don't care.' Night nurses describe her as cooperative and warm; day nurses report she screams at them. How is this pattern best understood?

- The patient has genuine rapport with the student and a legitimate complaint about day staff behavior
- Splitting — a feature of BPD in which people are experienced as entirely good or bad, requiring team consistency as the antidote **← keyed correct**
- Deliberate manipulation — the patient is consciously exploiting the split to get preferential treatment
- A delusional belief about staff intentions that warrants diagnostic reassessment for a psychotic disorder

**Rationale.** Face-value reassurance takes the report at face value and misses the clinical process. Manipulation framing leads to punitive responses that worsen therapeutic alliance. Psychosis = schizophrenia misapplies a diagnostic frame to a personality-disorder phenomenon. Splitting — all-good or all-bad experiencing of staff — is a feature of BPD; the intervention is team communication and consistency, not confrontation.

**Pearl.** Splitting is a feature of BPD, not a character flaw — team consistency and shared notes are the antidote, not confrontation.

**Stem.** A 22-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder is admitted in crisis with severe anxiety and sleep disturbance. She has been engaged in DBT for six months with good skill use documented. She is asking for 'something for her nerves.' The pharmacologic approach most consistent with evidence-based practice for BPD is which of the following?

- Prescribe lorazepam PRN — addressing the anxiety will help her engage with skills
- Add a nightly benzodiazepine for sleep — insomnia is fueling the crisis cycle
- Add an SSRI — anxiety in a BPD patient with good DBT engagement suggests an undertreated mood component requiring pharmacological augmentation.
- Minimize pharmacotherapy — if needed, a single low-risk agent for ≤1 week, preferring a sedating antihistamine or low-dose quetiapine over a benzodiazepine. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** The reflexive benzodiazepine in two forms (PRN and scheduled) and Polypharmacy first are the traps. No medication treats BPD itself; the evidence base supports skills over pills, and benzodiazepines in BPD carry the highest risk of attempted or completed suicide of any psychotropic class studied (HR 1.61, Lieslehto 2023). The NICE-aligned approach is a single, low-risk agent at the minimum dose for ≤1 week — sedating antihistamine or low-dose quetiapine preferred over benzodiazepines.

**Pearl.** Benzodiazepines in BPD carry the highest risk of attempted or completed suicide of any class studied — reach for skills, not pills.

**Stem.** A 27-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder is admitted following a suicide attempt by overdose. The treating team is considering starting lorazepam to help manage her emotional lability and distress between therapy sessions. Which evidence-based statement most accurately characterizes this plan?

- Benzodiazepines should not be started or escalated for BPD symptoms or crisis distress — they are associated with the highest risk of attempted or completed suicide of any psychotropic class studied in this population and worsen disinhibition. **← keyed correct**
- A short 5-day lorazepam course is safe — the risk only applies to chronic outpatient benzodiazepine use, not brief inpatient administration.
- Benzodiazepines are acceptable in BPD when prescribed at the lowest effective dose — the risk-benefit ratio favors treatment for acute distress.
- Lorazepam is first-line for BPD crisis management — emotional lability is its primary pharmacological target.

**Rationale.** Inpatient benzos safe short-term, Low dose = safe, and Benzo for BPD emotional dysregulation all lead to starting a contraindicated drug class. The page is explicit: 'Never start or escalate benzodiazepines in BPD — highest risk of attempted or completed suicide of any class (Lieslehto 2023); reach for skills, not pills.'

**Pearl.** Never start or escalate benzodiazepines for BPD symptoms or crisis distress — they carry the highest suicide risk of any psychotropic class in this population; use skills, not pills.

**Stem.** A patient with borderline personality disorder has been admitted for one week. Day-shift nurses describe her as 'demanding and exhausting,' while night-shift nurses call her 'our most engaged patient.' The team recognizes this as splitting. Which response most directly addresses the splitting dynamic?

- Have the primary clinician confront the patient with both accounts of her behavior — addressing the pattern directly will reduce the manipulative splitting.
- Bring the divergent staff perceptions to the next team meeting; establish a unified care plan with consistent limits, shared documentation, and identical responses from all staff. **← keyed correct**
- Rotate staff assignments so different nurses interact with the patient each shift, preventing any single staff member from becoming the 'good' or 'bad' object.
- Document the staff disagreement in the chart as evidence of inconsistent care quality and file a milieu management concern.

**Rationale.** Confront the patient, Rotation reduces splitting, and Document the disagreement all fail the unified-team response. The source states: 'Apply consistent, transparent limit-setting and communicate as a unified team — shared notes and handoffs blunt splitting.' Bringing divergent perceptions to rounds is the clinical intervention.

**Pearl.** Splitting in BPD is treated by team unity — shared notes, consistent limits, and bringing divergent perceptions to rounds; confronting the patient or rotating staff backfires.
