**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder has had 6 psychiatric admissions in the past 2 years, each following an episode of suicidal ideation and non-suicidal self-injury after an interpersonal rupture. She describes ongoing passive suicidal ideation at baseline. Tonight she is calm, the relationship dispute that triggered this admission has somewhat resolved, and her self-harm behaviors have decreased. How should the team conceptualize the discharge planning?

- She should be admitted as long as suicidal ideation is present — admission ends only when suicidal ideation resolves completely.
- Discharge should be planned now that the acute crisis has resolved — her chronic suicidality is managed primarily through collaborative safety planning and outpatient therapy, not serial acute admissions. **← keyed correct**
- Transfer to a long-term residential program is the appropriate step — repeated admissions indicate that outpatient treatment has failed.
- Increase the frequency of psychiatric medications — the recurrent admissions indicate inadequate pharmacotherapy.

**Rationale.** Admission until SI resolves, Repeated admissions = step up, and Polypharmacy for BPD all fail to apply the chronic-vs-acute distinction. The page is explicit: 'For chronic suicidality, collaborative safety planning is often preferable to repeated or prolonged admission, which can reinforce the hospital as the primary coping strategy.' Discharge planning after acute-on-chronic resolution is the correct frame.

**Pearl.** Chronic BPD suicidality ≠ acute crisis — distinguish the baseline from escalation; safety planning + outpatient DBT, not serial admissions, manage the chronic pattern.

**Stem.** A 31-year-old woman with a documented BPD diagnosis is admitted with severe depressive symptoms, inability to get out of bed, anhedonia, and new passive suicidal ideation. A third-year student says, 'She has BPD — this is probably just her mood lability.' The attending asks the student to reflect on this reasoning. What is the specific error in this approach?

- BPD should not be diagnosed in women under 35 — the patient's age makes the diagnosis questionable.
- The student should have assessed self-harm first before considering mood symptoms.
- BPD mood lability is transient and identity-driven; sustained anhedonia and neurovegetative symptoms over days are fully explained by BPD dysregulation and do not warrant evaluation for superimposed major depression.
- The student is using diagnostic overshadowing — attributing the depressive symptoms to the BPD diagnosis and under-investigating a potentially treatable mood episode; a BPD diagnosis does not protect a patient from also having major depression. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Age-based BPD exclusion and Sequence error miss the core error. Option C has the correct clinical description but key D is the answer that specifically names the error and its mechanism. The page calls this 'the cardinal error: diagnostic overshadowing — letting a personality label cause you to under-treat a real, acute, treatable illness.'

**Pearl.** Diagnostic overshadowing in BPD: never let a personality diagnosis suppress investigation of a treatable comorbid mood, psychotic, or substance disorder.

**Stem.** A 19-year-old man is admitted after an assault. He has a documented history of truancy, fire-setting, and cruelty to animals beginning at age 12. Which finding is required to diagnose antisocial personality disorder?

- Evidence of conduct disorder with onset before age 15 **← keyed correct**
- A current age of at least 16 years
- The presence of psychotic symptoms during the aggressive episodes
- A single act of serious violence in adulthood

**Rationale.** ASPD requires the person be ≥18 AND show evidence of conduct disorder with onset before age 15 — the childhood antecedent is mandatory.

**Pearl.** Antisocial personality disorder: age ≥18 plus evidence of conduct disorder before age 15.

**Stem.** A 45-year-old man believes coworkers are subtly undermining him and reads hidden hostile meaning into ordinary remarks, present for decades. He has never had hallucinations, sustained delusions, or functional collapse. Most likely diagnosis?

- Delusional disorder, persecutory type
- Paranoid personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Schizophrenia, paranoid presentation
- Schizotypal personality disorder

**Rationale.** Pervasive, longstanding distrust and suspiciousness without frank delusions or psychosis is paranoid personality disorder (Cluster A).

**Pearl.** Pervasive lifelong distrust without frank psychosis = paranoid personality disorder.

**Stem.** A 30-year-old woman has few relationships, magical thinking, ideas of reference, and odd speech; her social anxiety does not lessen as she gets to know people and stems from paranoid discomfort rather than fear of embarrassment. Which best fits?

- Schizoid personality disorder
- Avoidant personality disorder
- Schizotypal personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Social anxiety disorder

**Rationale.** Odd beliefs, magical thinking, ideas of reference, and eccentric cognition with persistent paranoid social anxiety define schizotypal PD (Cluster A).

**Pearl.** Odd beliefs + magical thinking + persistent paranoid social discomfort = schizotypal.

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder and recurrent self-harm asks what treatment is most likely to help long-term. Most appropriate first-line recommendation?

- Long-term benzodiazepines for affective instability
- An antipsychotic as definitive monotherapy
- Brief supportive counseling as the whole treatment plan
- A structured evidence-based psychotherapy such as dialectical behavior therapy **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Structured psychotherapy (DBT and related) is first-line for BPD; medications are adjunctive and symptom-targeted, and benzodiazepines are avoided.

**Pearl.** BPD: psychotherapy (DBT) first-line; meds adjunctive; avoid benzodiazepines.

**Stem.** A 50-year-old accountant is preoccupied with rules, lists, and perfectionism so that tasks are never finished; he sees nothing wrong with this and is proud of his standards. He has no true obsessions or compulsions. Best diagnosis?

- Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Obsessive-compulsive disorder
- Generalized anxiety disorder
- Narcissistic personality disorder

**Rationale.** OCPD is an ego-syntonic Cluster C pattern of perfectionism, orderliness, and control without true obsessions or compulsions (which define OCD).

**Pearl.** OCPD = ego-syntonic perfectionism/control, no true obsessions/compulsions; OCD is ego-dystonic with rituals.

**Stem.** On rounds you are asked to group the personality disorders. Which pairing of cluster and descriptor is correct?

- Cluster A = anxious; Cluster B = odd; Cluster C = dramatic
- Cluster A = odd/eccentric; Cluster B = dramatic/erratic; Cluster C = anxious/fearful **← keyed correct**
- Cluster A = dramatic; Cluster B = anxious; Cluster C = odd
- There is no descriptive grouping of the personality disorders

**Rationale.** DSM clusters: A (paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal) odd/eccentric; B (antisocial, borderline, histrionic, narcissistic) dramatic/erratic; C (avoidant, dependent, OCPD) anxious/fearful.

**Pearl.** Cluster A = odd, B = dramatic, C = anxious.

**Stem.** A patient is irritable and demanding during an acute manic episode; the team wonders about a personality disorder. What best distinguishes a personality disorder from this presentation?

- Difficult behavior during one acute inpatient episode
- Reliable diagnosis during an acute mood or psychotic episode
- Enduring cross-context traits beginning by adolescence or early adulthood **← keyed correct**
- A single dramatic behavior that captures the patient's character

**Rationale.** Difficulty-equals-PD trap, Timing error, and One-behavior fallacy all diagnose too quickly. Personality disorder requires an enduring, pervasive, inflexible pattern beginning by adolescence or early adulthood and present across contexts — not behavior limited to an acute manic, psychotic, intoxicated, or withdrawal state.

**Pearl.** Diagnose personality from an enduring cross-context pattern, not from acute-episode behavior.

**Stem.** A 40-year-old executive expects constant admiration, believes he is uniquely special and entitled to the best of everything, and dismisses colleagues as beneath him; he becomes enraged by mild criticism. Best diagnosis?

- Narcissistic personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Antisocial personality disorder
- Histrionic personality disorder
- Bipolar disorder, manic episode

**Rationale.** Grandiosity, need for admiration, entitlement, and lack of empathy define narcissistic personality disorder — distinct from antisocial (rights violations) and histrionic (attention through emotionality).

**Pearl.** NPD = grandiosity + need for admiration + entitlement + lack of empathy.

**Stem.** A 33-year-old woman cannot make everyday decisions without excessive reassurance, feels helpless when alone, and urgently seeks a new relationship for care whenever one ends. Best diagnosis?

- Avoidant personality disorder
- Dependent personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Borderline personality disorder
- Histrionic personality disorder

**Rationale.** A pervasive, excessive need to be taken care of — submissiveness, difficulty making decisions without reassurance, and urgently seeking a replacement relationship for care — defines dependent personality disorder (Cluster C).

**Pearl.** Dependent PD = excessive need to be cared for, submissive/clinging, urgently replaces lost relationships.

**Stem.** A 29-year-old is uncomfortable when not the center of attention, is dramatic and seductive with staff, shows rapidly shifting shallow emotions, and considers acquaintances closer than they are. Best diagnosis?

- Borderline personality disorder
- Narcissistic personality disorder
- Histrionic personality disorder **← keyed correct**
- Dependent personality disorder

**Rationale.** Excessive attention-seeking, dramatic and seductive behavior, shallow shifting emotions, and overestimating intimacy define histrionic personality disorder (Cluster B).

**Pearl.** Histrionic PD = attention-seeking, dramatic/seductive, shallow shifting emotions.

**Stem.** A 23-year-old man with a history of depression on high-dose sertraline is brought to the emergency department 3 hours after a second serotonergic medication was added to his regimen. Examination shows agitation, diarrhea, diaphoresis, and clonus; reflexes are brisk and symmetrically hyperactive throughout. Temperature is 38.3°C. Which of the following best explains this presentation?

- Neuroleptic malignant syndrome — high-dose SSRIs can block dopamine receptors
- Anticholinergic toxidrome — sertraline has strong anticholinergic activity
- Alcohol withdrawal — autonomic instability and agitation are hallmarks
- Serotonin syndrome — rapid onset after a second serotonergic agent was added, with hyperreflexia, clonus, and autonomic instability **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Wrong toxidrome is the common trap in two directions: NMS (requires a dopamine blocker, slower, lead-pipe rigid) and anticholinergic (dry skin, urinary retention, no clonus). It's just withdrawal misreads autonomic instability without the serotonin-specific signs. Serotonin syndrome is the answer: rapid onset after serotonergic augmentation, clonus, hyperreflexia, and autonomic instability are its distinguishing features.

**Pearl.** Serotonin syndrome: rapid onset, clonus, hyperreflexia after a serotonergic agent was added. NMS: lead-pipe rigidity, elevated CK, on a dopamine blocker — slower and stiffer.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does this presentation fit serotonin syndrome rather than neuroleptic malignant syndrome?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Both syndromes are clinically indistinguishable — only CK level reliably separates them"}, {"key": "B", "t": "NMS occurs only with first-generation antipsychotics; sertraline is an antidepressant"}, {"key": "C", "t": "Serotonin syndrome requires two agents simultaneously; this patient is only on one medication"}, {"key": "D", "t": "The reflexes distinguish them: serotonin syndrome produces clonus and hyperreflexia; NMS produces lead-pipe rigidity — and this patient has no dopamine blocker on board", "c": true}], "why": "Clonus is the serotonin syndrome fingerprint at the spinal cord — it distinguishes it from NMS (lead-pipe rigidity) and from anticholinergic toxidrome (no clonus). NMS can occur with second-generation antipsychotics, so drug class alone does not settle it. The absence of a dopamine blocker on this patient's list and the presence of clonus together confirm serotonin syndrome."}

**Stem.** A 34-year-old man with treatment-resistant schizophrenia has just been started on clozapine. He reports feeling better than he has in years. The treatment team asks the student to identify the most critical ongoing monitoring parameter for this medication. Which of the following is correct?

- Monthly liver function tests — clozapine is heavily metabolized by the liver and hepatotoxicity is the primary dose-limiting toxicity
- Absolute neutrophil count monitoring per prescribing requirements — clozapine carries a risk of agranulocytosis **← keyed correct**
- Serum clozapine levels at every visit — toxicity is reliably predicted by drug concentration
- Monthly QTc interval on ECG — clozapine's primary dose-limiting toxicity is cardiac arrhythmia

**Rationale.** Wrong monitoring target in two forms — LFTs and QTc — and Treat the number not the patient (serum levels without ANC) are the traps. Clozapine's mandated monitoring is the absolute neutrophil count due to the risk of potentially fatal agranulocytosis. Serum levels and LFTs may be checked but do not constitute the primary safety obligation.

**Pearl.** Clozapine requires mandatory ANC monitoring for agranulocytosis; also watch for myocarditis, severe constipation/ileus, and seizures.

**Stem.** A student argues that quetiapine should be chosen over haloperidol for a patient with psychosis because 'it's a second-generation agent and therefore more effective.' An attending asks what the CATIE trial established about this reasoning. Which response is most accurate?

- The student is correct — second-generation antipsychotics have uniformly superior efficacy to first-generation agents in head-to-head trials.
- CATIE showed that antipsychotics differ primarily in side-effect profile — metabolic effects, EPS, QTc, and prolactin — not in raw efficacy; agents should be chosen based on the patient's specific risk profile. **← keyed correct**
- CATIE found that olanzapine is the most effective antipsychotic overall and should be the default first choice.
- First-generation antipsychotics are preferred in first-episode psychosis because their longer evidence base makes them more predictable.

**Rationale.** Newer = better is the target trap. CATIE's fundamental lesson: the FGA/SGA distinction is not a reliable efficacy hierarchy. Choose by side-effect profile — the patient's metabolic risk, EPS vulnerability, QTc baseline, and prolactin sensitivity are the discriminating factors.

**Pearl.** CATIE: choose antipsychotics by side-effect profile, not generation — no consistent efficacy advantage for SGAs over FGAs.

**Stem.** A 23-year-old man is starting risperidone for his first psychotic episode. The student is asked to set up his monitoring schedule. What does the standard require for ALL patients starting any antipsychotic?

- Baseline ECG — all antipsychotics require cardiac clearance due to QTc prolongation risk.
- Serum drug level at steady state — therapeutic monitoring ensures adequate receptor occupancy.
- Urine drug screen at baseline — substance use can confound both the psychotic presentation and antipsychotic response.
- Baseline weight, fasting glucose, and lipid profile — then ongoing tracking; metabolic monitoring is part of the antipsychotic prescription for every agent. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** QTc always, Drug levels = necessary, and Most relevant test first all substitute a different monitoring task for the universal one. Metabolic monitoring — baseline weight, glucose, and lipids, then tracking — is built into the antipsychotic prescription regardless of the agent chosen. The page explicitly states: 'metabolic monitoring is part of the prescription.'

**Pearl.** Every antipsychotic: baseline weight, fasting glucose, lipids — then ongoing tracking. Metabolic monitoring is part of the prescription.

**Stem.** A 49-year-old man with bipolar I disorder has had three prior hospitalizations and two suicide attempts. The team is weighing lithium versus valproate for maintenance. Which statement about lithium's unique clinical profile is most accurate?

- Lithium has the strongest anti-suicidal evidence in its class and a narrow therapeutic window requiring renal and thyroid monitoring — its unique therapeutic profile and its monitoring requirements are inseparable considerations. **← keyed correct**
- Lithium's anti-suicidal effect is secondary to mood stabilization — any effective mood stabilizer achieves equivalent reduction in completed suicide.
- Lithium is first-line only for acute mania; its anti-suicidal and maintenance evidence are not strong enough to justify its monitoring burden.
- Lithium can be started without baseline labs in a medically healthy patient — monitoring is required only once toxicity signs appear.

**Rationale.** Anti-suicidal = just mood stabilization, Narrow = second-line, and Monitoring = optional all undermine the case for lithium in the highest-risk patient. Lithium's anti-suicidal evidence is cited on the page as worth remembering specifically when the team is weighing risk. For a patient with two prior suicide attempts, this is the discriminating consideration.

**Pearl.** Lithium: strongest anti-suicidal evidence in the mood stabilizer class + narrow window demanding renal/thyroid monitoring — both sides of the profile matter.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does lithium's narrow therapeutic window require renal monitoring specifically?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Lithium is metabolized in the kidney, and renal impairment reduces the rate of lithium breakdown."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Lithium is not protein-bound and is excreted entirely by the kidney — any reduction in renal clearance (dehydration, NSAIDs, diuretics, declining GFR) directly raises the serum level into the toxic range.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "Renal monitoring detects lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, which is the primary toxicity of concern."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Renal monitoring is a regulatory requirement without a strong pharmacokinetic rationale."}], "why": "Lithium is renally cleared without protein binding and without hepatic transformation. This means the serum level is determined almost entirely by renal clearance. Any factor that reduces GFR — dehydration, NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, loop diuretics, or simply age-related decline — will raise the level. The therapeutic and toxic windows are close enough that a modest change in clearance can shift a stable patient into toxicity. This is why renal function is monitored longitudinally, not just at baseline."}

**Stem.** The team plans to start valproate for a 27-year-old woman with bipolar I disorder admitted for acute mania. The student reviews her chart and finds no documentation of pregnancy status or contraception. Which action must occur before the first dose is given?

- Order baseline LFTs — hepatotoxicity is valproate's primary safety concern and must be screened before initiation.
- Obtain neurology sign-off — valproate is primarily an anticonvulsant and requires specialist authorization before psychiatric use.
- Confirm she is not pregnant and discuss contraception — valproate is a known teratogen (neural tube defects and other major malformations); this must be addressed before initiating in any person who could become pregnant. **← keyed correct**
- Start valproate at a low dose and monitor for teratogenic risk — low doses are safe in pregnancy.

**Rationale.** Hepatotox before teratogenicity, Specialist equals prerequisite, and Low dose equals safe all miss or displace the must-act-first check. The page is explicit: valproate should not be started in people who may become pregnant given teratogenicity — this check must come before the first dose, not afterward.

**Pearl.** Before valproate in any person who could become pregnant: confirm not pregnant, discuss contraception — this is the pre-prescribing gate, not a post-hoc conversation.

**Stem.** A 29-year-old man presents with a 3-week first depressive episode. His maternal grandmother and aunt both have bipolar disorder. The team is ready to prescribe sertraline. The student asks whether a bipolar screen should be done first. The most appropriate response is:

- A family history of bipolar disorder has no bearing on SSRI prescribing — treat the current episode.
- Screen for prior hypomanic or manic episodes before starting sertraline — an antidepressant without a mood stabilizer can destabilize unrecognized bipolar disorder, and a strong family history elevates the suspicion. **← keyed correct**
- Start sertraline and monitor for mania — treatment-emergent mania is how bipolar disorder is identified in practice.
- Co-prescribe a mood stabilizer with the SSRI as a precaution — mood stabilizers prevent antidepressant-induced destabilization.

**Rationale.** Diagnosis by symptom alone, Treat before diagnose, and Precautionary polypharmacy all bypass the screening step. The page states: 'screen for bipolarity before starting' an antidepressant — an antidepressant alone can destabilize bipolar illness. A positive family history, especially multiple first-degree relatives, makes this screening step particularly important.

**Pearl.** Screen for prior hypomania or mania before every first antidepressant prescription — family history of bipolar disorder elevates the index of suspicion.

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman started sertraline 3 days ago for major depression. She calls the unit and says, 'I don't feel any better — maybe this medication isn't right for me.' What is the most accurate and helpful response?

- Consider switching agents — inadequate early response after 3 days in a young adult suggests pharmacogenomic mismatch.
- Increase the dose — subtherapeutic early levels explain the absence of response.
- Discontinue sertraline and pursue psychotherapy alone — the black box warning signals that SSRIs are more dangerous than beneficial.
- Continue sertraline and reassess in several weeks — antidepressant response emerges over weeks, not days; counsel her on expected delayed onset and monitor for early side effects. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Antidepressants require weeks before mood benefits emerge — early absence of response is not treatment failure. Impatient switching, Treat the number, and Black box = don't treat are three forms of the same error: responding to early non-response as if it were evidence of treatment failure. The page is clear: onset is delayed — symptom relief lags weeks behind the first dose. Managing expectations is a clinical skill.

**Pearl.** SSRI onset is delayed — weeks, not days; early side effects can appear before mood benefit; it is too early to assess response at day 3.

**Stem.** A 21-year-old man is starting escitalopram for his first major depressive episode. His parents ask: 'We've heard there's a black box warning on these medications — what does it mean?' Which response best reflects the FDA warning?

- All antidepressants carry an FDA black box warning for increased suicidal thinking and behavior through age 24 — close monitoring especially early in treatment is mandated; this is a monitoring requirement, not a contraindication. **← keyed correct**
- The warning applies only to tricyclic antidepressants — SSRIs are exempt from the black box warning.
- The black box applies only to patients under 18 — your son at 21 is no longer in the warning category.
- The warning concerns completed suicide — the medications increase the likelihood of a lethal attempt.

**Rationale.** Selective citation, Partial citation: Selective citation, Partial citation in two forms. The FDA black box warning covers all antidepressants through age 24 — not just TCAs, not just under-18 patients. The correct framing is that it mandates close monitoring early in treatment, not that it prohibits treatment. The absolute increase in suicidal ideation/behavior is roughly 1 per 110 treated, against an NNT near 10 for response.

**Pearl.** FDA black box: all antidepressants + all ages through 24 = monitor closely early; it is a monitoring mandate, not a contraindication.

**Stem.** A 71-year-old woman with generalized anxiety disorder has been prescribed lorazepam 0.5 mg TID continuously for 18 months by her primary care physician. She is now admitted for a fall and mild confusion. The student is asked to comment on her lorazepam use. Which is the most accurate framing?

- Lorazepam TID is appropriate for generalized anxiety — long-term benzodiazepine maintenance has a well-established evidence base for GAD.
- The dose has remained stable, so tolerance and dependence are unlikely — the clinical concern is solely whether the dose is sufficient for symptom control.
- Long-term scheduled benzodiazepines in an older adult accumulate risk: physical dependence, falls, and delirium; the admission events (fall, confusion) are consistent with adverse effects; deprescribing should be planned with appropriate tapering. **← keyed correct**
- Benzodiazepines produce less sedation in older adults due to reduced receptor sensitivity — standing use is safe in elderly patients.

**Rationale.** Autopilot prescribing, Stability equals safety, and Wrong direction of aging effect all normalize long-term scheduled benzodiazepines. The fall and confusion on admission are not incidental — they are the expected adverse effects the page names: falls, dependence, and delirium especially in older adults. The answer: targeted, short-term use is the standard; this patient needs a supervised taper.

**Pearl.** Standing benzodiazepines in older adults: dependence, falls, and delirium are expected adverse effects — short-term targeted use is the standard; deprescribe with a supervised taper.

**Stem.** A 39-year-old man with schizophrenia has failed two adequate antipsychotic trials (olanzapine, then risperidone) at adequate doses and durations. The team discusses clozapine. What is the non-negotiable safety requirement before clozapine can be initiated?

- Baseline EEG — clozapine lowers the seizure threshold and a baseline neurological status must be documented.
- Baseline absolute neutrophil count and ongoing ANC monitoring per the prescribing information — agranulocytosis is the central safety concern requiring structured hematologic surveillance. **← keyed correct**
- Baseline echocardiogram — clozapine-associated myocarditis requires cardiac clearance before initiation.
- A failed trial of ECT — clozapine is reserved for patients who have also failed somatic therapies.

**Rationale.** Seizure monitoring over agranulocytosis, Cardiac over ANC, and Hierarchical misread all displace the correct priority. Clozapine's unique safety architecture is organized around the ANC: agranulocytosis is the life-threatening risk that drove the original REMS requirement, and ongoing hematologic monitoring per the prescribing information remains mandatory.

**Pearl.** Clozapine requires baseline ANC + ongoing monitoring per prescribing information — agranulocytosis is the central safety concern; the FDA eliminated the clozapine REMS in 2025 (FDA, 2025); ANC monitoring continues per the prescribing information.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why is the absolute neutrophil count monitored for clozapine rather than total white blood cell count alone?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Clozapine suppresses all white blood cell lines equally — ANC and WBC change in parallel, but ANC is the regulatory standard."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Clozapine selectively suppresses neutrophil production — a normal total WBC can mask agranulocytosis if lymphocytes are preserved; ANC isolates the neutrophil compartment where the risk resides.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "The ANC requirement is purely regulatory and does not reflect a pharmacologic distinction from total WBC monitoring."}, {"key": "D", "t": "Clozapine causes neutrophilia, not neutropenia; monitoring the ANC detects the upper end of the distribution."}], "why": "Clozapine's bone marrow toxicity targets neutrophil production — specifically, it suppresses the neutrophil precursors in the myeloid lineage. A patient can have a normal or near-normal total WBC with preserved lymphocytes while their neutrophil count has dropped to a dangerous level. The ANC directly measures the neutrophils, which are the cells responsible for fighting bacterial infections. Agranulocytosis — ANC below 500 — renders the patient functionally unable to mount a bacterial defense, making infections rapidly life-threatening."}

**Stem.** Two patients on the unit develop acute syndromes. Patient A was started on venlafaxine 3 days ago and tramadol was added yesterday; today he is agitated with muscle twitching, hyperreflexia, and diaphoresis. Patient B was started on haloperidol 5 days ago; today he is febrile to 39.8°C with 'lead-pipe' rigidity and a CK of 4,100. Which response correctly characterizes both presentations?

- Both patients have NMS — elevated temperature and rigidity after antipsychotic exposure is the defining feature of NMS in both cases.
- Both patients have serotonin syndrome — venlafaxine and haloperidol both affect serotonin pathways.
- Both emergencies should be treated identically with cyproheptadine and cooling measures.
- Patient A has serotonin syndrome (clonus, hyperreflexia, rapid onset after adding a serotonergic agent); Patient B has NMS (lead-pipe rigidity, hyperthermia, elevated CK, slower onset after antipsychotic). These are distinct emergencies with different mechanisms and treatments. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** All rigidity equals NMS, All agitation equals serotonin syndrome, and One treatment for all are three forms of diagnostic conflation. The discriminating features: serotonin syndrome = rapid onset, clonus, hyperreflexia, after serotonergic agent combination; NMS = slower onset, lead-pipe rigidity, hyperthermia, elevated CK, after dopamine blocker. Treating them identically mismatches the pharmacologic antidote to the wrong mechanism.

**Pearl.** Serotonin syndrome: rapid onset, clonus, hyperreflexia (serotonergic agents); NMS: slower, lead-pipe rigidity, hyperthermia, elevated CK (dopamine blockade) — different mechanisms, different treatments.

**Stem.** A 65-year-old man with bipolar I disorder maintained on lithium 900 mg daily presents with intention tremor, unsteady gait, and mild confusion over 5 days. His lithium level was 0.75 mEq/L two months ago. He recently started lisinopril for newly diagnosed hypertension. Most likely explanation?

- Lithium toxicity — ACE inhibitors reduce renal lithium clearance, leading to lithium level elevation; tremor, ataxia, and confusion are early-to-moderate toxicity signs. **← keyed correct**
- Essential tremor triggered by a new antihypertensive — beta-blockers, not ACE inhibitors, cause tremor.
- Bipolar mixed state with neurological features — the combination of mood instability and motor symptoms represents bipolar cycling.
- Lisinopril-induced hypotension — orthostatic changes reduce cerebral perfusion and explain the ataxia and confusion.

**Rationale.** Neurological symptoms equal neurological diagnosis, Bipolar by default, and Cardiac first all bypass the pharmacokinetic explanation that is sitting in the chart. ACE inhibitors reduce renal lithium clearance — a well-established drug interaction. The triad of tremor, ataxia, and confusion after something changed in a lithium-treated patient is the textbook lithium toxicity presentation.

**Pearl.** Lithium toxicity: tremor + ataxia + confusion = check the level when something changed (dehydration, NSAIDs, ACE inhibitors, diuretics, declining renal function).

**Stem.** A 72-year-old woman with treatment-resistant MDD has active suicidal ideation, has stopped eating and drinking, and expresses a fixed belief that her body is rotting. She has failed two adequate antidepressant trials. The team is choosing between a third antidepressant plus antipsychotic versus ECT. Which response best reflects the evidence?

- Start a third antidepressant plus an atypical antipsychotic — combination pharmacotherapy is the standard for psychotic depression and avoids the risks of anesthesia.
- ECT — it is the most effective treatment for severe, psychotic, and treatment-resistant depression and provides the rapid response required for a patient with active suicidality who is not eating or drinking. **← keyed correct**
- rTMS — it avoids anesthesia, is FDA-cleared for treatment-resistant depression, and has equivalent efficacy to ECT in psychotic depression.
- Esketamine — rapid-acting antidepressants with same-day onset should be tried before ECT for any treatment-resistant presentation.

**Rationale.** Pharmacotherapy first at any severity, Newer = better, and Ordering by recency all delay the most effective and most appropriate intervention. ECT's indications include exactly this constellation: severe, psychotic, treatment-resistant depression with active suicidality and refusal to eat/drink. The page lists 'rapid, definitive response needed' as the specific framing that points to ECT.

**Pearl.** ECT is the most effective treatment for severe, psychotic, or catatonic depression — choose it when a rapid, definitive response is needed (active suicidality, not eating/drinking, prior good ECT response).

**Stem.** A patient with severe depression is scheduled for ECT tomorrow. Her current medications include clonazepam 1 mg BID, gabapentin 300 mg TID, and escitalopram 20 mg daily. Her ECT team asks the student to identify which medication adjustment is most critical before the procedure.

- Discontinue escitalopram — antidepressants inhibit the neuroplasticity mechanisms through which ECT works.
- Hold gabapentin only — anticonvulsants raise seizure threshold, but benzodiazepines are safe to continue through ECT.
- Hold clonazepam and gabapentin — benzodiazepines and anticonvulsants raise the seizure threshold and blunt the induced seizure that is the mechanism of ECT's therapeutic effect. **← keyed correct**
- Discontinue all medications for 72 hours before ECT — polypharmacy during ECT increases anesthesia risk.

**Rationale.** Wrong medication stopped, Partial hold, and All medications stopped all fail to identify the pharmacologically correct adjustment. The ECT page is explicit: benzodiazepines and anticonvulsants raise the seizure threshold and reduce ECT efficacy — they are the agents to hold. Escitalopram is not contraindicated.

**Pearl.** Before ECT: hold benzodiazepines and anticonvulsants — they raise the seizure threshold and reduce ECT efficacy.

**Stem.** A 48-year-old man with treatment-resistant depression (two adequate antidepressant trials, no psychotic features, no active suicidality) strongly prefers to avoid general anesthesia. He asks the team about neuromodulation options. Which correctly characterizes what is available?

- rTMS is equivalent to ECT for all indications including psychotic depression — the delivery route is the only difference.
- ECT is the only FDA-cleared neuromodulation option for treatment-resistant depression.
- Esketamine intranasal requires no monitoring and can be self-administered at home once initiated.
- rTMS is FDA-cleared for treatment-resistant depression, requires no anesthesia, carries a small seizure risk, and is less potent than ECT; for this patient who declines anesthesia, rTMS is a clinically appropriate next step. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Newer = better, Ignores rTMS, and Misread REMS monitoring all mischaracterize the options. rTMS is FDA-cleared for TRD and OCD, outpatient, anesthesia-free, with a small seizure risk — less potent than ECT but a reasonable option for patients who cannot or will not tolerate anesthesia. Esketamine requires in-office REMS monitoring.

**Pearl.** rTMS: FDA-cleared for TRD and OCD, no anesthesia, small seizure risk, less potent than ECT — appropriate for patients who decline anesthesia.

**Stem.** A 27-year-old woman with no prior psychiatric history is admitted for new-onset paranoid ideation, disorganized behavior, and auditory hallucinations. Her chart notes she was started on high-dose prednisone three weeks ago for an autoimmune flare. Before establishing a primary psychiatric diagnosis, the most important step is which of the following?

- Take a thorough medication and medical history and consider a substance- or medication-induced cause before labeling this a primary psychotic disorder **← keyed correct**
- Start a second-generation antipsychotic immediately — symptom control should not await diagnostic clarity
- Diagnose schizophreniform disorder based on symptom type and the absence of a prior psychiatric history
- Reassure the family that first-episode psychosis in young adults is almost always primary and will respond to antipsychotics

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia is the trap in two forms: treating immediately or diagnosing before excluding secondary causes. Psychosis is a syndrome with a broad differential — corticosteroids are a recognized cause of secondary psychosis, and the clinical picture (new-onset, no prior history, active steroid course) demands a medical workup first. Face-value reassurance skips that step and commits to a prognosis without a diagnosis.

**Pearl.** Psychosis is a syndrome — exclude delirium, substances, medications, and autoimmune causes before settling on a primary disorder.

**Stem.** A 37-year-old man with schizophrenia has been stable on haloperidol 5 mg/day and sertraline 100 mg/day for a comorbid depressive episode. His sertraline was increased to 150 mg two days ago. He now presents with fever (38.9°C), agitation, diaphoresis, and muscle rigidity. CK is mildly elevated at 680 U/L. The differential includes serotonin syndrome and neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Which finding, if present on examination, would most strongly support serotonin syndrome over neuroleptic malignant syndrome?

- Uniform lead-pipe resistance throughout the passive range of motion of the upper and lower extremities, with normal deep tendon reflexes.
- Creatine kinase (CK) above 5,000 U/L with rising creatinine suggesting early rhabdomyolysis.
- Gradual onset of rigidity and fever developing over 48–72 hours after the haloperidol was started three months ago, with no change in the serotonergic regimen.
- Ankle clonus with brisk, brisker-than-baseline deep tendon reflexes throughout the lower extremities. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Lead-pipe rigidity + hyporeflexia → NMS; clonus + hyperreflexia → SS. The three distractors all describe NMS features: lead-pipe rigidity (A), dramatically elevated CK (B), and subacute onset tied to the dopamine blocker rather than the serotonergic change (C). Ankle clonus with lower-extremity hyperreflexia (D) is the hallmark physical finding of serotonin syndrome — it reflects serotonin-mediated hyperactivation of spinal reflex arcs and is absent in NMS, where rigidity comes from central dopaminergic dysfunction. The added clinical clues — onset after the sertraline increase, mild rather than dramatic CK — also favor SS over NMS. In practice, both syndromes require stopping the offending agent; the physical exam reflex pattern is the fastest bedside discriminator.

**Pearl.** NMS vs. SS at the bedside: lead-pipe rigidity + hyporeflexia → NMS (dopamine blocker, subacute onset); clonus + hyperreflexia → SS (serotonergic agent, rapid onset hours).

**Stem.** A 22-year-old man presents with 3 weeks of auditory hallucinations, paranoid delusions, and disorganized behavior. The team is ready to diagnose schizophrenia and start an antipsychotic. The student asks whether anything should be done first. Which step is most critical?

- Obtain a collateral history from family — schizophrenia cannot be diagnosed without family confirmation.
- Start the antipsychotic immediately — early treatment of schizophrenia reduces the duration of untreated psychosis and improves long-term outcomes.
- Start a structured first-episode protocol (RAISE) — coordinated specialty care reduces DUP more effectively than routine treatment.
- Exclude secondary causes of psychosis — delirium, substance-induced psychosis, mood disorder with psychotic features, and autoimmune encephalitis — before labeling a primary psychotic disorder. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Collateral = diagnosis, Treat before diagnose, and Protocol before differential all skip the foundational step. The page states: 'Psychosis is a syndrome — exclude delirium, substances, and autoimmune/seizure causes before calling it primary.' In a young adult with new-onset psychosis, the organic yield is highest at the first episode.

**Pearl.** Psychosis is a syndrome — exclude delirium, substance-induced, mood disorder with psychotic features, and autoimmune encephalitis before diagnosing primary psychotic disorder.

**Stem.** A 19-year-old man presents with his first psychotic episode — 4 weeks of auditory hallucinations, paranoid delusions, and social withdrawal with no prior psychiatric history. Laboratory workup is sent. The team is discussing imaging. When is neuroimaging warranted in first-episode psychosis?

- First-episode psychosis warrants a thorough medical workup — the organic yield is highest when there is no prior psychiatric history; MRI is indicated with focal findings, atypical or abrupt onset, unusual age, seizures, or autoimmune encephalitis on the differential. **← keyed correct**
- Neuroimaging is not indicated in first-episode psychosis — it is expensive and does not alter the psychiatric management plan.
- CT is preferred over MRI in first-episode psychosis because it is faster and avoids the need for patient cooperation during sedation.
- MRI should only be ordered if the psychosis does not respond to antipsychotic treatment after 4 weeks.

**Rationale.** Imaging never changes treatment, CT over MRI, and Treat before image all defer a workup that is most valuable precisely at first-episode presentation. The page states first-episode psychosis 'warrants a medical workup because a treatable organic cause is more likely when there is no established psychiatric history' and specifically recommends MRI in the named scenarios.

**Pearl.** First-episode psychosis earns a full medical workup including MRI when there are focal signs, atypical onset, seizures, or concern for autoimmune encephalitis — organic yield is highest here.

**Stem.** A student argues that lurasidone should be prescribed for a first-episode patient because 'it is one of the newest second-generation agents and therefore most effective.' Which framing from CATIE is most relevant here?

- The student is correct — second-generation agents show consistently superior efficacy in first-episode trials compared to older agents.
- Choose the antipsychotic by the patient's specific side-effect risk profile — metabolic, motor, QTc, sedation, and prolactin risks — not by recency or generation. **← keyed correct**
- CATIE found perphenazine was superior to all second-generation agents for first-episode psychosis — first-generation agents should be chosen first.
- CATIE only applies to chronic schizophrenia — for first-episode psychosis, the newest generation agents should be preferred.

**Rationale.** Newer = better, Older = better, and CATIE exception for first episode all invert or misapply the CATIE lesson. The conclusion of CATIE: effectiveness is broadly comparable across FGAs and SGAs; the clinically actionable difference is in the side-effect profile matched to the patient's individual vulnerability.

**Pearl.** CATIE: antipsychotic choice should be driven by the patient's metabolic, EPS, QTc, and sedation risk — not by generation or recency.

**Stem.** A 24-year-old man with schizophrenia is brought to the inpatient unit by his parents. He insists there is nothing wrong with him and refuses to engage with the treatment plan. His parents ask: 'Why won't he just accept that he's sick?' Which response best reflects the clinical approach?

- Confronting the patient with the evidence that he is ill — the psychoeducation-first approach — produces the fastest improvement in insight.
- Anosognosia is a neurobiological deficit in self-awareness, not willful denial — use the LEAP approach (Listen, Empathize, Agree, Partner) to build engagement without requiring the patient to accept the diagnosis first. **← keyed correct**
- Lack of insight is a reliable indicator of poor prognosis — formal involuntary treatment is indicated for any patient who refuses to acknowledge their diagnosis.
- Accept the patient's denial and focus treatment on behavioral symptoms only — attempting to improve insight in schizophrenia is not evidence-based.

**Rationale.** The righting reflex and No insight = no hope displace LEAP-based engagement. The page explicitly states: 'many patients have anosognosia, so use Amador's LEAP approach (Listen, Empathize, Agree, Partner) rather than arguing about whether they are ill.' Anosognosia is a neurobiological symptom, not willful denial — debating it produces resistance, not insight.

**Pearl.** Anosognosia is not denial — use LEAP (Listen, Empathize, Agree, Partner) to build engagement without requiring the patient to accept the diagnosis first.

**Stem.** A 37-year-old man with schizophrenia has had an inadequate response to olanzapine (adequate dose and duration) and then risperidone (adequate dose and duration). Residual negative symptoms (flat affect, avolition, alogia) have persisted on both trials. The team is now choosing a next step. Which is the evidence-based recommendation?

- Try a third antipsychotic from a different chemical class — a third-line non-clozapine agent should be exhausted before clozapine.
- Add an antidepressant — the residual negative symptoms are likely a secondary depression co-occurring with schizophrenia.
- Increase the risperidone dose further — super-therapeutic doses overcome treatment resistance.
- Initiate clozapine with baseline ANC and recommended hematologic monitoring per the prescribing information — two failed adequate antipsychotic trials define treatment-resistant schizophrenia. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Skip the ladder, Negative symptoms = depression, and More = better all defer the evidence-based intervention. The page states: 'two failed adequate antipsychotic trials means consider clozapine, with recommended ANC monitoring per the prescribing information.' This threshold is defined and evidence-based.

**Pearl.** Two adequate antipsychotic trial failures = treatment-resistant schizophrenia = clozapine with ANC monitoring; a third non-clozapine trial has no supporting evidence.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Beyond agranulocytosis, which additional serious adverse effects of clozapine require active monitoring?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Retinal toxicity and tardive dyskinesia — these are the long-term side effects that require monitoring beyond the first year."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Nephrotoxicity and pulmonary fibrosis — clozapine accumulates in renal and pulmonary tissue with prolonged use."}, {"key": "C", "t": "Myocarditis (especially in the first weeks), severe constipation and ileus (potentially life-threatening), seizures, and orthostatic hypotension — these require active clinical monitoring beyond the ANC.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "Hepatic failure and peripheral neuropathy — the CYP1A2 metabolism of clozapine produces hepatotoxic intermediates with chronic use."}], "why": "The ANC is the best-known monitoring requirement because it drove the REMS program, but the page explicitly names four other serious risks: myocarditis (early, potentially fatal — watch for chest pain, dyspnea, and troponin elevation in the first weeks), severe constipation progressing to ileus (can be fatal and is underrecognized), seizures (dose-dependent, lowered threshold), and orthostasis. 'Beyond the ANC: clozapine can cause myocarditis (especially in the first weeks), severe constipation/ileus, seizures, and orthostasis — monitor for these, not just the count.'"}

**Stem.** A 32-year-old woman with schizophrenia started haloperidol 5 days ago. She is now febrile to 39.9°C, diaphoretic, and rigid. Nursing staff describe her muscle tone as 'like a pipe.' Her CK is 5,400. What is the most important immediate action?

- Stop the antipsychotic immediately, transfer to a medical unit for supportive care, and monitor vital signs and CK serially — this is neuroleptic malignant syndrome. **← keyed correct**
- Increase the antipsychotic dose — the psychotic agitation underlying the NMS-like symptoms requires more aggressive dopamine blockade.
- Start empiric antibiotics — fever and rigidity after medication initiation represent a superimposed infection in an immunocompromised patient.
- Administer a benzodiazepine for the agitation and recheck vitals in 4 hours before escalating.

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic, Fever = infection, and Treat and wait all delay the single most important intervention: stopping the antipsychotic. NMS is the can't-miss antipsychotic emergency — fever + lead-pipe rigidity + elevated CK after dopamine blocker initiation requires immediate drug cessation and medical-level care.

**Pearl.** NMS: fever + lead-pipe rigidity + elevated CK after antipsychotic → stop the antipsychotic immediately and transfer to medical care.

**Stem.** A 21-year-old woman develops subacute psychosis, behavioral changes, and seizures over 4 weeks. She also has involuntary mouth movements and facial grimacing, and her family reports fluctuating states of agitation and relative unresponsiveness. Which diagnosis should be actively considered in this presentation?

- Cannabis-induced psychosis — young adults with psychosis and behavioral changes most commonly have substance-induced illness.
- Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis — the combination of subacute psychosis with seizures, orofacial dyskinesias, and fluctuating consciousness characterizes this autoimmune syndrome and requires urgent CSF and antibody testing. **← keyed correct**
- Schizophrenia — the presence of psychosis, behavioral change, and movement abnormalities in a young woman is classic for schizophrenia spectrum illness.
- Bipolar disorder with psychotic features — the fluctuating states and behavioral change represent rapid cycling.

**Rationale.** Substance first, Psychosis = schizophrenia, and Bipolar by default all produce premature diagnostic closure on a treatable condition. The page specifically names anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis as a can't-miss mimic: 'subacute psychosis with seizures, dyskinesias, autonomic instability' — the triad here maps directly to that presentation.

**Pearl.** Subacute psychosis + seizures + orofacial dyskinesias + fluctuating consciousness = anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis until proven — urgent CSF and antibody testing.

**Stem.** A 20-year-old man is diagnosed with first-episode schizophrenia. His family asks, 'What kind of treatment should we expect — just medication?' Which response best reflects the evidence-based approach for first-episode psychosis?

- Yes — antipsychotic medication alone is the evidence-based standard for first-episode psychosis; psychosocial interventions are added only after medication stabilization.
- Clozapine should be initiated immediately for first-episode schizophrenia — early aggressive treatment reduces DUP most effectively.
- Coordinated specialty care, including antipsychotic medication, active family involvement, early psychosocial intervention, and integrated care — as supported by the RAISE project — is the evidence-based approach for first-episode psychosis. **← keyed correct**
- The focus for first-episode psychosis should be on medication titration alone; family involvement risks creating dependency and should be avoided in young adults.

**Rationale.** Medication alone for first episode, Skip the ladder, and Family involvement = dependency all fail to apply the RAISE evidence. The page explicitly states: 'For first-episode patients, RAISE supports coordinated specialty care with active family involvement and early psychosocial intervention, not medication alone.'

**Pearl.** First-episode psychosis: coordinated specialty care (RAISE model) — medication + family involvement + psychosocial intervention from the start, not medication alone.

**Stem.** A 78-year-old man with no prior psychiatric history is noted to be 'psychotic' — he is making nonsensical statements, appears to be responding to internal stimuli, and is alternating between agitation and somnolence. He was admitted 2 days ago for a hip fracture repair. Which diagnosis should be considered first?

- Late-onset schizophrenia — psychosis with no prior history in an older adult indicates a late-onset primary psychotic disorder.
- Bipolar disorder with late-onset psychotic mania — the agitation and apparent hallucinations indicate a mood disorder with psychotic features.
- Brief psychotic disorder — the onset after a medical stressor meets the criteria for brief psychotic disorder.
- Delirium — acute onset after surgery with fluctuating consciousness, inattention, disorganized thinking, and alternating agitation and somnolence is delirium until proven otherwise. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia, Bipolar by default, and Stress-reactive psychosis all assign a primary psychiatric diagnosis to what is clinically delirium. The page states: 'Think delirium first in any acute, fluctuating presentation with inattention or clouded consciousness.' The alternating agitation and somnolence, post-surgical timing, and older age converge on the delirium diagnosis.

**Pearl.** Delirium first in any acute, fluctuating presentation with clouded consciousness — new 'psychosis' after surgery in an older patient is delirium until proven otherwise.

**Stem.** A 41-year-old man with treatment-resistant schizophrenia on clozapine for 6 months presents with 3 days of fever, severe constipation, and abdominal distension. His ANC is within the monitored range. Which clozapine adverse effect must be immediately considered?

- Severe constipation progressing to ileus — an overlooked but potentially fatal clozapine complication; obstipation and abdominal distension require urgent evaluation. **← keyed correct**
- Agranulocytosis — fever in a clozapine-treated patient always indicates a dropping ANC regardless of the current count.
- Myocarditis — clozapine-associated myocarditis presents with fever and should be evaluated by cardiac workup first.
- Clozapine toxicity — fever and abdominal symptoms indicate supratherapeutic levels requiring a dose reduction.

**Rationale.** All fever = agranulocytosis, Fever = myocarditis, and Toxicity explains everything all focus on the better-known risks while missing the one the presentation actually points to. The page explicitly states: 'severe constipation/ileus' as a serious clozapine risk that must be monitored 'beyond the ANC.' Ileus from clozapine is underrecognized and can be fatal.

**Pearl.** Clozapine beyond the ANC: severe constipation → ileus is a potentially fatal complication; fever + abdominal distension in a clozapine-treated patient requires urgent GI evaluation.

**Stem.** A 28-year-old woman presents with 2 weeks of auditory hallucinations and paranoid delusions coinciding with a severe depressive episode. Between prior mood episodes she has no psychotic symptoms. Which diagnosis is most consistent with this pattern?

- Schizophrenia — persistent auditory hallucinations lasting 2 weeks meet duration criteria for schizophrenia.
- Mood disorder with psychotic features — psychosis confined to the mood episode and absent between episodes, distinguishing this from schizoaffective disorder or schizophrenia. **← keyed correct**
- Schizoaffective disorder — concurrent psychosis and a mood episode always indicate schizoaffective disorder.
- Brief psychotic disorder — the acute onset within a depressive episode qualifies for a brief psychotic disorder diagnosis.

**Rationale.** Psychosis = schizophrenia, Concurrent = schizoaffective, and Brief = distinct diagnosis all misidentify the key discriminating feature: does the psychosis persist independent of mood episodes? When psychosis is confined to mood episodes, the diagnosis is mood disorder with psychotic features, not schizophrenia or schizoaffective.

**Pearl.** Psychosis confined to mood episodes = mood disorder with psychotic features; schizoaffective disorder requires ≥2 weeks of psychosis independent of any mood episode.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "What is the minimum distinguishing criterion between schizoaffective disorder and a mood disorder with psychotic features?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "The number of mood episodes — schizoaffective disorder requires at least three mood episodes with psychosis."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Psychotic symptoms that persist for at least 2 weeks in the absence of a major mood episode — this independent psychosis distinguishes schizoaffective disorder from a mood disorder with psychotic features.", "c": true}, {"key": "C", "t": "The severity of hallucinations — complex auditory hallucinations with formed voices indicate schizoaffective disorder; simple noises indicate a mood disorder."}, {"key": "D", "t": "The presence of negative symptoms — negative symptoms are only found in schizoaffective disorder and schizophrenia, not in mood disorders with psychotic features."}], "why": "The DSM-5 criterion that distinguishes schizoaffective disorder from mood disorder with psychotic features is straightforward but clinically important: schizoaffective disorder requires a period of at least 2 weeks where delusions or hallucinations are present without any major mood episode (depressive, manic, or mixed). If psychosis is always and only present during mood episodes, the diagnosis is a mood disorder — not schizoaffective. This distinction matters because the treatment focus differs significantly."}

**Stem.** A 33-year-old man with schizophrenia has had a modest response to risperidone 4 mg/day for 6 weeks. His attending considers this a non-response and wants to proceed to clozapine. The student questions whether the treatment resistance threshold has been met. What must be true before clozapine is indicated for treatment-resistant schizophrenia?

- One failed antipsychotic trial at any dose is sufficient to establish treatment resistance and justify clozapine.
- Failure of any antipsychotic regardless of dose or duration qualifies — the current trial started 6 weeks ago and is adequate.
- Two adequate antipsychotic trials — at therapeutic doses, for adequate durations — must be documented before treatment-resistant schizophrenia is established and clozapine is considered. **← keyed correct**
- Trial of ECT followed by failure of one antipsychotic qualifies — somatic therapy plus pharmacotherapy failure establishes the threshold.

**Rationale.** One trial sufficient, Duration sufficient alone, and ECT in the ladder all misstate the threshold. The page defines treatment resistance as 'inadequate response to two adequate antipsychotic trials' — both trials must be at therapeutic doses for adequate durations. One partially-dosed trial does not qualify.

**Pearl.** Treatment-resistant schizophrenia = inadequate response to two adequate antipsychotic trials (therapeutic dose, adequate duration) — both criteria must be met before clozapine.

**Stem.** A 22-year-old has had delusions, hallucinations, and disorganized speech for the past 3 months with functional decline and no mood episode or substance cause. Which diagnosis best fits at this point?

- Schizophrenia
- Brief psychotic disorder
- Delusional disorder
- Schizophreniform disorder **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Psychotic symptoms lasting 1–6 months with functional decline, no mood/substance cause, is schizophreniform disorder. <1 month = brief psychotic disorder; ≥6 months = schizophrenia.

**Pearl.** Psychosis duration: <1 mo = brief psychotic; 1–6 mo = schizophreniform; ≥6 mo = schizophrenia.

**Stem.** A patient has had auditory hallucinations and delusions for two weeks with no significant mood symptoms during that period, but has also had major depressive episodes during most of the illness. Best diagnosis?

- Schizoaffective disorder **← keyed correct**
- Major depressive disorder with psychotic features
- Schizophrenia without prominent mood burden
- Bipolar I with psychotic features

**Rationale.** Schizoaffective disorder requires a period of at least 2 weeks of psychosis without prominent mood symptoms, plus mood episodes present for the majority of the illness. Psychosis confined to mood episodes is mood disorder with psychotic features; schizophrenia does not have mood episodes occupying most of the illness.

**Pearl.** Schizoaffective: ≥2 weeks of psychosis without mood symptoms + mood episodes for most of the illness.

**Stem.** A family meeting is held the day before planned discharge for a 52-year-old man admitted after a suicide attempt by overdose. When the agenda reaches safety and warning signs, his wife says: 'We'll keep a close eye on him. And you don't need to worry about the guns — he would never use them, and they're put away.' The attending invites your input. Which response is best?

- 'It makes sense that everyone is exhausted and scared. Before discharge, who can confirm that the firearms — and any stockpiled medications — are actually secured, and how will we know it's done?' **← keyed correct**
- 'Firearms are involved in about half of US suicides and are lethal in 85 to 90 percent of attempts — "put away" isn't good enough.'
- 'That's reassuring — we'll document that the family will keep him safe and that the guns are secured.'
- 'Discharge can't move forward unless you agree to remove every firearm from the home today.'

**Rationale.** The best response validates first, stays inside the student role, and converts 'put away' into a concrete, verifiable step — who secures the items and how the team will know. Quoting lethality statistics at the family is the righting reflex in evidence clothing; accepting reassurance leaves means safety unconfirmed at the exact moment risk peaks; and an ultimatum makes the family responsible for treatment while overstepping the student's role.

**Pearl.** Means safety is a lethality intervention, not a formality — 'who can confirm items are secured before discharge?' is a clinical act.

**Stem.** A family meeting is held on hospital day 3 for a 29-year-old man admitted for a first manic episode. His mother interrupts repeatedly: 'He does this every time he stops his medication — he's selfish and he makes us all suffer.' She is tearful and wringing her hands. The attending invites the student to observe what is happening. What is the student's best understanding of this dynamic?

- The mother's criticism is accurate and should be incorporated into the discharge plan as a documented adherence barrier
- The meeting should be suspended — the mother's distress is dysregulating the patient and the therapeutic goals cannot be met
- The mother should be gently corrected about the medication-stopping narrative to address the factual inaccuracy
- The mother is showing expressed emotion — criticism that likely masks fear and grief — and the student's task is to validate the feeling behind it before moving to problem-solving **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Criticism as fact accepts EE as objective reporting; Terminate for emotion cuts off the highest-yield phase of the meeting; Correct the narrative redirects to facts before the emotional climate has been heard. Expressed emotion — criticism, hostility, emotional over-involvement — is the measurable family dynamic that predicts relapse. De-shaming and validating the underlying fear or grief, without correcting in the moment, is what the playbook calls the phase that most changes outcomes.

**Pearl.** Expressed emotion — criticism, hostility, emotional over-involvement — often masks fear; validate it without correcting it in the moment, because this phase is where outcomes change.

**Stem.** A student is seeing a 38-year-old man who is admitted after an alcohol-related incident — his second alcohol-related admission this year. When the student mentions concerns about his drinking, the patient says, 'Look, I drink to handle stress — I don't have a problem. Everyone drinks.' The student's instinct is to explain why the drinking is dangerous and list the health consequences. Which response best reflects motivational interviewing?

- 'That label isn't the point — and it sounds like work stress has been really heavy lately. You've ended up here twice this year. What's your take on that?' [Rolling with resistance, reflecting back the fact without argument, then opening with a genuine question] **← keyed correct**
- 'Your liver enzymes are elevated and your blood pressure is high — the data clearly show that alcohol is harming you.' [Presenting the medical evidence directly to motivate change]
- 'I think you should stop drinking entirely — it's the only way to protect your health.' [A clear recommendation from the clinician]
- 'Would you be willing to try an abstinence contract for the next 30 days — just to see if you feel better?' [Proposing a behavior change target]

**Rationale.** The righting reflex, Advice without readiness, and Planning before evoking all push the patient into a defensive position. MI's core insight: 'People talk themselves into change. Your job is not to supply the arguments but to evoke the patient's own reasons.' Rolling with resistance and reflecting the ambiguity without arguing is the MI move.

**Pearl.** Resist the righting reflex — don't argue for change; roll with resistance, reflect change talk, and let the patient articulate their own reasons.

**Stem.** A family asks the treatment team what they can do during their loved one's hospitalization for schizophrenia to help with recovery. The team is considering whether to invest the time in a formal family meeting. Which argument best supports making structured family psychoeducation the default approach?

- Family involvement is primarily a courtesy — it improves patient satisfaction but has no measurable impact on relapse rates.
- Families frequently refuse structured family meetings, so the effort should be reserved for families who specifically request it.
- Family psychoeducation is the highest-evidence family intervention — it led 11 models for 12-month relapse prevention (Rodolico et al., Lancet Psychiatry 2022; 90 RCTs) with an NNT of 7 (Pharoah, Cochrane 2010; 53 RCTs); dose matters: ≤2 sessions is ineffective, with NICE recommending ≥10 sessions over ≥3 months. **← keyed correct**
- Family involvement should wait until the patient is stable — in the acute phase, it increases stimulation and worsens psychosis.

**Rationale.** Family work = courtesy, Patient/family refusal as barrier, and Stability first all undermine the evidence-based standard. Family psychoeducation has NNT 7 for 12-month relapse prevention — one of the strongest NNTs in inpatient psychiatry. The dose requirement (≥10 sessions over ≥3 months) means starting the work during the admission and securing the outpatient continuation.

**Pearl.** Family psychoeducation for schizophrenia: NNT 7 for 12-month relapse prevention; ≥10 sessions over ≥3 months; dose matters — start during admission.

**Stem.** A student is using the MI readiness ruler with a patient who has poorly controlled diabetes and drinks heavily. He rates the importance of cutting back alcohol as '4 out of 10.' Which follow-up question best uses the readiness ruler to elicit change talk?

- 'You said 4 — why not a 2? What keeps it that high?' [Evoking the patient's own reasons for change by asking why the number isn't even lower] **← keyed correct**
- 'Why isn't your number higher? You should care more about your health.' [Naming the mismatch between the rating and the clinical severity]
- 'That's a 4 — you're clearly not ready to change right now. Let's talk about something else.' [Accepting the score and moving on]
- 'A 4 means ambivalence — let's complete a full decisional balance worksheet together right now.' [Shifting to a structured CBT exercise]

**Rationale.** Confrontational reflect, Score = readiness fixed, and Decisional balance as MI all bypass the evocative purpose of the readiness ruler. The MI readiness ruler follow-up sequence: '...why not a 2?' forces change talk by asking the patient to articulate why they're not lower. 'What would move you from a 4 to a 6?' explores the next steps. Both are evocative, not confrontational.

**Pearl.** MI readiness ruler: after the patient rates importance (e.g., 4/10), ask 'why not a 2?' — this forces the patient to articulate their own change talk rather than pushing your reasons.

**Stem.** The family of a patient with schizophrenia is described in the chart as 'high expressed emotion' — high criticism and high emotional over-involvement. The resident says, 'We can't do much about the family climate — it's just how they are.' Which response is most evidence-based?

- Agree — expressed emotion is a fixed family trait that does not respond to clinical intervention.
- High EE only predicts relapse in schizophrenia, not in other psychiatric illnesses — it is not relevant beyond psychosis.
- High EE is a family problem but not a clinical problem — it is outside the scope of inpatient psychiatric intervention.
- Expressed emotion (EE) substantially raises relapse odds — roughly doubling to quintupling across studies (Butzlaff & Hooley 1998) — while family warmth is protective, but EE is modifiable through behavioral family therapy and family psychoeducation, making the family climate a tractable clinical target. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** EE is fixed, EE = schizophrenia only, and EE = out of scope all represent clinical fatalism about a modifiable variable. The page states EE 'roughly doubles to quintuples relapse odds depending on the study (Butzlaff & Hooley 1998 meta-analysis), while family warmth is protective — and family climate is modifiable, which is why meetings help lower it.'

**Pearl.** Expressed emotion (EE) substantially raises relapse odds (roughly doubling to quintupling across studies; Butzlaff & Hooley 1998) and is MODIFIABLE — behavioral family therapy and psychoeducation specifically target and reduce it; EE is a tractable clinical problem.

**Stem.** A 27-year-old woman is being discharged after a suicidal crisis. The resident asks her to 'sign a contract that she won't hurt herself.' The student wonders whether this is the evidence-based approach. Which response reflects the current standard?

- Yes — a no-suicide contract is the gold standard for discharge safety planning; it establishes a binding commitment to safety.
- Yes — contracts are more legally protective for the clinician than safety plans, which is why they are standard at discharge.
- No — a collaborative safety plan (Stanley-Brown model) is the evidence-based tool; no-suicide contracts have no supporting evidence and can create false reassurance that displaces genuine clinical assessment. **← keyed correct**
- Neither approach has evidence — rely on clinical judgment and family support for suicidal patients at discharge.

**Rationale.** Contract = gold standard, Contract = legal protection, and No evidence either way all misrepresent the evidence. The brief psychotherapy page explicitly states: 'Abandon no-suicide contracts — they don't work.' Safety planning (Stanley-Brown) with follow-up reduces suicidal behaviors by approximately 50%.

**Pearl.** Safety planning (Stanley-Brown) with follow-up ≈ 50% fewer suicidal behaviors — abandon no-suicide contracts, which have no evidence and create false reassurance.

**Stem.** A 52-year-old man with severe depression is withdrawn, stays in bed all day, and says 'nothing matters anymore.' The student asks how to perform a brief psychotherapy intervention given a 15-minute window. Which approach is most appropriate for this presentation?

- Behavioral activation — schedule one small, specific valued activity for today; this is the highest-yield, lowest-cognitive-demand inpatient move for behavioral shutdown. **← keyed correct**
- Psychodynamic interpretation — explore the unconscious drivers of the behavioral withdrawal during this session.
- Prolonged exposure — begin reliving any traumatic experiences that may underlie the depression.
- Wait for medication to take effect before beginning any psychotherapy — behavioral shutdown is too severe for psychotherapy engagement.

**Rationale.** Insight therapy for shutdown, PE for depression, and Medication before psychotherapy all mismatch mechanism to technique. The brief psychotherapy table: 'Withdrawn, inactive, anhedonic — behavioral shutdown → reach for behavioral activation (BA): schedule one small, valued activity. Superior to TAU for depression (SMD −0.78) and anxiety, on par with CBT; lowest cognitive demand → best inpatient fit.'

**Pearl.** Behavioral activation is the highest-yield, lowest-effort inpatient move for the shutdown depressed patient — schedule one small valued activity; it requires no cognitive engagement.

**Stem.** A patient with recurrent MDD completed three individual therapy sessions during an 8-day hospitalization. At discharge, the student writes in the note: 'CBT skills taught inpatient; patient discharged with psychotherapy complete.' The attending asks the student to reconsider the framing. Which is more accurate?

- The note is accurate — three CBT sessions is the evidence-based minimum for inpatient psychotherapy, and the skills have been transferred.
- Inpatient psychotherapy starts the work and secures the handoff — the discharge note should name the specific therapeutic thread started, identify the named outpatient clinician who will continue it, and make the handoff explicit. **← keyed correct**
- Psychotherapy is an outpatient intervention — inpatient therapy has no meaningful impact and should not be documented as a treatment component.
- Three CBT sessions is sufficient only if the patient was 'CBT-naive' — if she had prior CBT, the inpatient work is redundant.

**Rationale.** Example: 'BA for behavioral shutdown' is the specific thread; naming the clinician prevents the work from being dropped at discharge. Three sessions = complete, Inpatient therapy = ineffective, and Prior CBT = redundant all miss the purpose. The page: 'The skill is to read the mechanism driving this patient's crisis right now and match one brief technique to it — then hand the work off to outpatient care.' 'Write the handoff so the outpatient clinician can continue the thread — the point of inpatient therapy is to start it and secure the follow-up.'

**Pearl.** Inpatient psychotherapy starts the thread and secures the handoff — document the technique, name the outpatient clinician, and make the handoff explicit; it does not complete treatment.

**Stem.** The treatment team reviews its data and finds that structured family meetings occurred in only 8% of admissions over the past year, despite family members being available and willing in most cases. The team discusses why. Which explanation is most consistent with the literature?

- Families of inpatient psychiatric patients predominantly refuse to participate in structured family meetings when offered.
- Structured family work requires >90 minutes per family — the time investment is prohibitive in an acute inpatient setting.
- The main barrier to family psychoeducation being delivered is clinician avoidance — structured family work is available to the minority of families who receive it largely because of clinician behavior, not family behavior. **← keyed correct**
- Insurance authorization is the dominant barrier — family meetings require separate billing codes that are denied in most health systems.

**Rationale.** Family refusal, Time as primary barrier, and Billing as dominant barrier all attribute the gap to external factors. The literature finding is counterintuitive and important: 'Reaches a minority of families — structured family work is delivered in only ~0–53% of cases (historically <10%), and the main barrier is clinician avoidance, not patient refusal (Dixon 1999; Eassom 2014).' This is a clinician self-awareness finding.

**Pearl.** The main barrier to family psychoeducation in inpatient psychiatry is clinician avoidance, not patient or family refusal — the gap is a clinician behavior problem.

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman with BPD cuts herself on the unit. The charge nurse says, 'Document it and flag it. Don't talk to her about it — it just encourages the behavior.' The student wonders about the evidence-based approach. Which response is most accurate?

- The nurse is right — direct conversation about self-harm incidents reinforces the behavior by providing attention; the clinical standard is documentation without engagement.
- The appropriate response is immediate medication review — self-harm indicates the current regimen is inadequate.
- Place the patient on 1:1 observation and restrict milieu privileges pending a formal incident review — the safety breach requires an institutional response before therapeutic engagement resumes.
- A chain analysis — walking the sequence of antecedents, emotions, and behaviors with the patient non-punitively — turns the incident into a clinical formulation and a coping plan; this is the DBT standard and replaces documentation-only or punitive responses. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Engagement reinforces behavior, Self-harm = medication failure, and Punitive documentation all fail to apply the DBT-informed clinical standard. The page states: 'Chain analysis replaces a punitive response to self-harm — understand the behavior, don't just document it.' The chain analysis is a brief, structured, non-punitive conversation about what preceded the self-harm that turns the incident into a formulation.

**Pearl.** After self-harm: chain analysis (non-punitive, with the patient) replaces documentation-only or punitive responses — it transforms the incident into a formulation and a coping plan.
