**Stem.** A 32-year-old man with alcohol use disorder has refused treatment for 2 years. His wife is desperate to help and asks the team what she can do. The team is considering a traditional confrontational intervention approach. Which family therapy modality is specifically evidence-based for this scenario?

- CRAFT (Community Reinforcement and Family Training) — the family member changes her own behavior in specific ways that reduce the reinforcement of drinking and increase the appeal of treatment-seeking, without confrontation or blame. **← keyed correct**
- Structural family therapy (Minuchin) — the focus is on realigning the roles and boundaries in the marriage to reduce the enabling dynamic.
- A family confrontation (traditional intervention) — bringing family and friends together to confront the patient with consequences is the most evidence-based approach.
- Emotionally focused therapy (EFT) — addressing the attachment disruption in the marriage will resolve the motivational impasse for the patient.

**Rationale.** Structural for substance refusal, Confrontational intervention, and EFT for treatment refusal all apply less-specific frameworks. CRAFT is the specific modality: 'the family changes its own behavior to invite treatment — no blame.' It trains the wife to use behavioral strategies (reinforcing non-drinking behavior, withdrawing reinforcement of drinking, using teachable moments) that increase the patient's engagement with treatment.

**Pearl.** CRAFT: the family changes its OWN behavior to invite treatment — no blame, no confrontation; evidence-based for families of treatment-refusing people with SUD.

**Stem.** A quality-improvement team wants to identify the single inpatient psychological intervention with the strongest readmission-prevention evidence. Which intervention has the hardest numbers?

- Safety planning (Stanley-Brown) — approximately 50% fewer suicidal behaviors.
- Psychoeducation — the hardest inpatient numbers for readmission prevention: readmission NNT 5 (Xia, Cochrane 2011) and relapse NNT 9; and it costs nothing except time. **← keyed correct**
- Behavioral activation — the most evidence-based inpatient psychological intervention for any condition.
- Problem-solving therapy (PST) — NNT 12 for repeat self-harm re-presentation.

**Rationale.** Safety planning hardest numbers, BA = strongest overall, and PST hardest numbers each reference real evidence for different outcomes. For readmission prevention specifically, psychoeducation's NNT 5 is the standout: 'Psychoeducation has the hardest inpatient numbers (readmission NNT 5) — and it's free.' This is the quality-improvement answer.

**Pearl.** Psychoeducation has the hardest inpatient readmission numbers: NNT 5 (Xia, Cochrane 2011) — the highest-evidence, lowest-cost psychological intervention for preventing readmission.

**Stem.** A young man with schizophrenia is being discharged to his parents. Which family intervention has the best evidence for reducing his relapse risk?

- Keeping illness discussion out of the discharge plan
- Family psychoeducation (including multifamily groups) **← keyed correct**
- Limiting family contact as the relapse-prevention plan
- Individual insight-oriented therapy for the patient alone

**Rationale.** Family psychoeducation and multifamily group approaches reduce relapse in schizophrenia, largely by lowering expressed emotion and improving problem-solving and support.

**Pearl.** Family psychoeducation reduces schizophrenia relapse — the evidence-based family intervention.

**Stem.** A patient with alcohol use disorder says, 'I don't really think my drinking is a problem.' Which response best reflects motivational interviewing?

- 'You clearly have a serious problem and need to stop now.'
- 'If you don't quit, you'll end up back in the hospital.'
- 'What have you noticed about how drinking fits with the things that matter to you?' **← keyed correct**
- 'Fine, it's your choice — let me know if you ever want help.'

**Rationale.** Motivational interviewing rolls with resistance and evokes the patient's own reasons for change through open, values-linked questions — rather than confronting, warning, or disengaging (the 'righting reflex').

**Pearl.** MI evokes change talk with open, values-linked questions; avoid the confronting 'righting reflex.'

**Stem.** A patient's adult son calls the unit asking for details of his mother's diagnosis and medications. The patient has not authorized this. What is the most appropriate response?

- Share diagnosis and medications because he is family
- Refuse any conversation and hang up
- Verify identity, then disclose the full record
- No specific disclosure without consent; general support is okay **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Without the patient's consent, do not confirm or share specific diagnosis or medication information. You can still listen, offer general support and education, and coordinate a consented family meeting; safety exceptions are separate.

**Pearl.** No consent means no specific disclosure; general support and education are still allowed.

**Stem.** A 19-year-old man is admitted after a near-lethal suicide attempt. He has expressed persistent hopelessness and a stated wish to die for three days. On hospital day 4, nurses report he seems 'much better' — calm, well-rested, and telling the team he is ready to go home. His affect is bright and he is chatty with staff. No clinical change or social development explains the shift. Best next step?

- Begin discharge planning — improvement is the goal of admission and this patient has achieved it
- Reduce his observation level and move him to a lower-acuity room to reinforce the progress
- Escalate to the attending immediately — unexplained sudden improvement after severe suicidality is a warning sign requiring reassessment **← keyed correct**
- Update the safety plan and schedule discharge within 24 hours while the patient is engaged

**Rationale.** Sudden improvement = recovery is the most dangerous trap: the pocket card explicitly lists unexplained sudden improvement as an immediate escalation trigger. Quiet patient = stable patient reduces vigilance at exactly the wrong moment. Safety plan = discharge permission slip conflates a tool with a risk decision.

**Pearl.** Sudden unexplained improvement after severe suicidality is an escalation trigger, not evidence of recovery.

**Stem.** A patient is pacing the hallway, speaking loudly, and waving his hands. He has not threatened anyone and is not in immediate danger of harming himself or others. You are the student. Which response is best?

- Approach calmly, introduce yourself, acknowledge that he looks upset, and offer him a choice — 'Would it help to step somewhere quieter, or is there something I can get you?' **← keyed correct**
- Return to the nursing station and request a PRN intramuscular medication order before approaching
- Call for additional staff and prepare for restraint in case the patient escalates
- Instruct him firmly to return to his room or staff will have to intervene

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic, Restraint first, and Commands over choices are the three forms of the same error: defaulting to force or medication before de-escalation. The Project BETA framework begins with calm presence, acknowledgment of distress, and genuine choices — not commands or preemptive escalation.

**Pearl.** Verbal de-escalation — calm presence, acknowledgment, and genuine choices — is the default first response to agitation, not a courtesy step before medication.

### qb_saf_003

**Stem.** A 22-year-old man is admitted following a suicide attempt by acetaminophen overdose. When the student asks about means restriction, he asks only: 'Do you have access to anything dangerous at home?' The patient answers 'no.' The attending asks what a complete means safety assessment requires. Which response is best?

- Ask the patient if he feels safe returning home — a verbal assurance of safety constitutes adequate means restriction documentation.
- Ask specifically about firearms, stockpiled medications, sharps and ligatures, and who will remove or secure each item — then ask how it will be confirmed before discharge. **← keyed correct**
- Have the patient and family sign a no-harm contract — a signed document constitutes the means safety plan.
- Document 'means safety discussed' in the note — documenting the discussion fulfills the clinical obligation.

**Rationale.** Face-value reassurance and Document and continue are both documentation traps. Means safety requires asking by item — firearms, stockpiled medications, sharps, ligatures, and the patient-specific methods the presentation revealed — and confirming who will secure them and how the team will know it happened. A general safety question or a no-harm contract does not accomplish this.

**Pearl.** Means safety = ask by item (firearms, medications, sharps), name who will secure them, and confirm how the team will know.

**Stem.** A 45-year-old man with schizophrenia and two prior suicide attempts is admitted for worsening command hallucinations telling him to jump. He is calm on exam; he reports the voices are 'quieter today' and denies current intent. Which statement best reflects how to frame his suicide risk?

- His current denial of suicidal intent indicates low acute risk — patient self-report is the most reliable indicator of imminent safety.
- His prior attempts are the most important factor and define his overall risk level — chronic factors drive the primary assessment.
- His schizophrenia diagnosis independently elevates his risk to high — a psychiatric diagnosis drives the risk classification.
- Separate chronic risk (prior attempts, schizophrenia — the floor) from acute risk (active command hallucinations — the current target); the formulation must address both tiers and identify the modifiable acute drivers. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Denies SI = low risk in two forms and Diagnosis = high risk all produce single-tier formulations that miss the key structure. Risk formulation separates the chronic floor (prior attempts, schizophrenia) from the acute drivers (command hallucinations, current voices) that are the admission's targets. Command hallucinations are explicitly listed as an acute risk factor; denial of intent does not neutralize them.

**Pearl.** Separate chronic and acute risk — prior attempts and a psychiatric diagnosis set the floor; command hallucinations and current intent are the acute targets.

**Stem.** A 38-year-old woman is admitted after a near-lethal suicide attempt. She has three young children and is an ICU nurse. A student notes 'strong family ties and professional identity' as protective factors and proposes these substantially mitigate her acute risk. The attending asks the student to refine this reasoning. Which statement is most accurate?

- The number of protective factors is inversely proportional to acute risk — more factors reliably indicate lower risk.
- Protective factors matter only if they are credible and available during the risk window — a family connection that is geographically unavailable during a crisis does not reduce risk at that moment. **← keyed correct**
- Professional status significantly reduces suicide risk — healthcare workers have lower rates of completed suicide than the general population.
- Protective factors and acute risk factors can be numerically compared — strong protective factors offset recent preparatory behavior in the calculation.

**Rationale.** Face-value reassurance appears throughout. Protective factors are not protective if they are geographically distant, unavailable, or theoretically present but non-operative during the crisis. Healthcare worker status does not protect against suicide. The page states explicitly: 'Protective factors are not magic. They matter only if they are credible and available during the risk window.'

**Pearl.** Protective factors matter only if they are credible and available during the risk window — not as a list, but as real-time assets.

**Stem.** A student is asked to draft the safety note for a patient admitted with active suicidal ideation, a stated plan, and unsecured firearm access. Which of the following best illustrates strong safety documentation?

- "Patient endorses suicidal ideation — reviewed risks and benefits of hospitalization, patient agrees to continued treatment."
- "Denies SI/HI. No acute safety concerns."
- "Acute suicide risk elevated due to active plan, persistent insomnia, escalating alcohol use, and firearm access not yet confirmed secured; chronic risk elevated by prior attempt and family history; modifiable targets include means restriction and substance stabilization; continued inpatient care indicated." **← keyed correct**
- "Patient stated: 'I want to die and I have a gun at home.' Verbatim suicidal statement documented per policy."

**Rationale.** Document and continue, Denies SI = low risk, and Quotes = documentation all replace formulation with something easier to write. Strong safety documentation names chronic factors, acute drivers, protective factors with limits, modifiable targets, and the current plan. 'Denies SI/HI. No acute safety concerns.' is the example the page uses for the weakest possible documentation.

**Pearl.** Strong safety documentation = chronic factors + acute drivers + protective factors with limits + modifiable targets + current plan — not 'denies SI/HI.'

### qb_saf_007

- id=`qb_saf_007` · status=`attested` · type=`sba` · difficulty=`2` · competency=`['safety', 'next-step']`
- Linked pages: `pg_suicide.md`
- Evidence: `p`, `g`, `_`, `s`, `u`, `i`, `c`, `i`, `d`, `e`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `'`, `W`, `h`, `a`, `t`, ` `, `T`, `o`, ` `, `E`, `s`, `c`, `a`, `l`, `a`, `t`, `e`, ` `, `I`, `m`, `m`, `e`, `d`, `i`, `a`, `t`, `e`, `l`, `y`, `'`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `I`, `n`, `t`, `o`, `x`, `i`, `c`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `o`, `r`, ` `, `w`, `i`, `t`, `h`, `d`, `r`, `a`, `w`, `a`, `l`, `.`

**Stem.** A 19-year-old woman is brought to the inpatient unit after a self-reported suicide attempt. On arrival she is visibly intoxicated with alcohol. The student is attempting to complete an initial risk assessment. Which of the following best describes the correct approach?

- Escalate to the attending immediately — intoxication is an explicit escalation trigger; a reliable risk assessment cannot be completed while she is intoxicated, and medical monitoring is required. **← keyed correct**
- Complete the risk assessment now — gathering history while the patient can still speak is more important than waiting for sobriety.
- Document 'unable to assess due to intoxication' and defer further evaluation to the morning team.
- Reassure the patient that alcohol often exaggerates suicidal statements and that a full assessment in the morning will clarify her actual risk level.

**Rationale.** Assess before escalate and Face-value reassurance both produce the same error: treating an escalation trigger as a reason to delay action. Intoxication is explicitly listed on the card as 'what to escalate immediately.' Document and continue creates an unmonitored gap. The correct approach is immediate attending notification, with the formal risk assessment deferred to sobriety.

**Pearl.** Intoxication during active suicidal ideation is an escalation trigger — notify the attending now; complete the formal risk assessment after sobriety.

- Evidence: `p`, `g`, `_`, `s`, `u`, `i`, `c`, `i`, `d`, `e`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `'`, `S`, `t`, `u`, `d`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `R`, `o`, `l`, `e`, `'`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `Y`, `o`, `u`, ` `, `c`, `a`, `n`, `n`, `o`, `t`, `:`, ` `, `C`, `l`, `e`, `a`, `r`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `p`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `f`, `o`, `r`, ` `, `d`, `i`, `s`, `c`, `h`, `a`, `r`, `g`, `e`, ` `, `i`, `n`, `d`, `e`, `p`, `e`, `n`, `d`, `e`, `n`, `t`, `l`, `y`, `.`

**Stem.** A medical student is alone with a patient who says: 'I feel so much better — can you put in a good word with the team about going home today?' The student has not been briefed on this patient's risk. Which response is most appropriate?

- Affirm to the patient that she appears improved and will relay this to the team, then complete a brief suicide screen to provide the team with an update.
- Explain that discharge is the team's decision, then complete and document a formal suicide risk assessment in the medical record based on this conversation.
- Reassure the patient that the team is working toward discharge and provide the hospital's crisis line number.
- Tell the patient that discharge is the team's decision and that you will let the attending know she wants to discuss it — then inform the team promptly. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Student clears patient in two forms — direct affirmation and independent documentation — both cross the role boundary. The student's role is to ask, observe, and report to the team — not to clear a patient for discharge or to produce an independent safety assessment. Reassure and defer leaves the student in a limbo of implied endorsement without any supervisory loop.

**Pearl.** Students can ask, observe, and report to the team — they cannot independently clear a patient for discharge or conduct a solo risk assessment.

- Linked pages: `agitation.md`
- Evidence: `a`, `g`, `i`, `t`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `'`, `R`, `e`, `s`, `t`, `r`, `a`, `i`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `s`, `e`, `c`, `l`, `u`, `s`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `'`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `T`, `h`, `e`, `y`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `q`, `u`, `i`, `r`, `e`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `t`, `i`, `m`, `e`, `-`, `l`, `i`, `m`, `i`, `t`, `e`, `d`, ` `, `p`, `h`, `y`, `s`, `i`, `c`, `i`, `a`, `n`, ` `, `o`, `r`, `d`, `e`, `r`, `,`, ` `, `c`, `l`, `o`, `s`, `e`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `n`, `u`, `o`, `u`, `s`, ` `, `m`, `o`, `n`, `i`, `t`, `o`, `r`, `i`, `n`, `g`, `,`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `d`, `i`, `s`, `c`, `o`, `n`, `t`, `i`, `n`, `u`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `m`, `o`, `m`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `p`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `e`, `n`, `t`, ` `, `i`, `s`, ` `, `s`, `a`, `f`, `e`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `n`, `o`, `t`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `e`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `o`, `f`, ` `, `a`, ` `, `s`, `h`, `i`, `f`, `t`, `.`

**Stem.** A patient placed in physical restraints 4 hours ago following an agitation episode is now calm, cooperative, and asking to be released. His nurse pages the on-call student. Which of the following best reflects the correct management principle?

- Continue the restraints through the end of the shift to ensure safety is maintained across the nursing team handoff.
- Notify the attending immediately — the patient's calm state and request to be released require prompt reassessment; restraints must be discontinued as soon as the patient is safe, not held to shift end. **← keyed correct**
- Ask the patient to demonstrate he can remain calm for 30 minutes, then release him and document the trial.
- Since the original physician order is still active, no action is needed until the order expires.

**Rationale.** Restraint as stability in two forms and Order = permission both treat the order as a static permission. Restraint is a time-limited safety measure requiring a physician order, continuous monitoring, and discontinuation the moment the patient is safe — the page explicitly states 'not the end of a shift.' The student's correct action is immediate attending notification so the reassessment can happen.

**Pearl.** Restraints require time-limited orders, continuous monitoring, and discontinuation as soon as the patient is safe — not the end of a shift.

**Stem.** A team is debriefing after a physical restraint episode involving a Black patient on the inpatient unit. A student asks what the published evidence shows about racial disparities in restraint use in inpatient psychiatry. Which statement correctly reflects the data?

- Black patients are restrained at similar rates to White patients after adjusting for clinical acuity — the apparent disparity is explained by severity of presentation.
- Black patients are more likely to receive verbal de-escalation before physical restraint — the data show greater resource deployment, not disparity.
- Black patients face roughly 1.85× the adjusted odds of physical restraint on inpatient units — this disparity is a quality-of-care issue, not an acuity artifact. **← keyed correct**
- Joint Commission non-discrimination standards adopted in 2015 have eliminated racial disparities in restraint use.

**Rationale.** No disparity exists in three forms. The page explicitly states: 'Black patients face roughly 1.85× the adjusted odds of physical restraint on inpatient units (and ~2.84× in the ED awaiting admission) — that disparity is a quality-of-care issue, not a footnote.' This is the only data point cited on the page for this topic and is explicitly framed as belonging in a post-restraint debrief.

**Pearl.** Black patients face ~1.85× the adjusted odds of physical restraint on inpatient units — a quality-of-care disparity that belongs in every post-restraint debrief.

- Linked pages: `agitation.md`
- Evidence: `a`, `g`, `i`, `t`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `'`, `V`, `e`, `r`, `b`, `a`, `l`, ` `, `d`, `e`, `-`, `e`, `s`, `c`, `a`, `l`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, `,`, ` `, `f`, `i`, `r`, `s`, `t`, `'`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `T`, `h`, `e`, ` `, `P`, `r`, `o`, `j`, `e`, `c`, `t`, ` `, `B`, `E`, `T`, `A`, ` `, `d`, `e`, `-`, `e`, `s`, `c`, `a`, `l`, `a`, `t`, `i`, `o`, `n`, ` `, `f`, `r`, `a`, `m`, `e`, `w`, `o`, `r`, `k`, ` `, `(`, `R`, `i`, `c`, `h`, `m`, `o`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `e`, `t`, ` `, `a`, `l`, `.`, ` `, `2`, `0`, `1`, `2`, `)`, ` `, `c`, `e`, `n`, `t`, `e`, `r`, `s`, ` `, `r`, `e`, `s`, `p`, `e`, `c`, `t`, `,`, ` `, `s`, `i`, `m`, `p`, `l`, `e`, ` `, `b`, `o`, `u`, `n`, `d`, `a`, `r`, `i`, `e`, `s`, `,`, ` `, `l`, `i`, `s`, `t`, `e`, `n`, `i`, `n`, `g`, `,`, ` `, `a`, `n`, `d`, ` `, `o`, `f`, `f`, `e`, `r`, `i`, `n`, `g`, ` `, `c`, `h`, `o`, `i`, `c`, `e`, `s`, ` `, `r`, `a`, `t`, `h`, `e`, `r`, ` `, `t`, `h`, `a`, `n`, ` `, `i`, `s`, `s`, `u`, `i`, `n`, `g`, ` `, `c`, `o`, `m`, `m`, `a`, `n`, `d`, `s`, `.`

**Stem.** A patient with mania is pacing loudly in the hallway, slamming cabinet doors, and making hostile comments toward staff. He has not made a direct threat or moved toward anyone. You are the student and the first person to encounter him. Which response is best?

- Signal a nearby staff member that you are approaching, then step calmly into the hallway — not blocking his exit — introduce yourself and acknowledge his frustration: 'I can see you're really upset right now. What's going on?' Then offer a choice of where to speak. **← keyed correct**
- Return to the nursing station and request an involuntary IM medication order before approaching, so the team is ready to intervene.
- Stand in the doorway and firmly instruct him to return to his room or further action will be taken.
- Alert the restraint team and position staff at the hallway exits in case the patient escalates.

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic, Commands over choices, and Restraint first are three forms of reaching for escalation before de-escalation. The Project BETA framework starts with calm presence, acknowledgment, genuine choices, and listening. The student also needs to stay near the exit and never block the patient's — personal safety and de-escalation are not competing goals. A student encountering an agitated patient should signal a staff member before engaging — personal safety and de-escalation are complementary, not competing goals.

**Pearl.** Calm presence + acknowledging distress + offering choices = the first move in de-escalation; stay near your exit and never block the patient's.

### qb_saf_012

**Stem.** A 67-year-old man with no psychiatric history, admitted for pneumonia, begins shouting at night and pulling at his oxygen tubing. He is combative with staff. The on-call resident asks the student how to approach this. What is the most appropriate first frame?

- Start an antipsychotic immediately and then search for the cause — treating the agitation reduces the risk of self-harm while the workup proceeds.
- Initiate a CIWA-Ar protocol — nighttime agitation in an older admitted patient is most likely alcohol withdrawal.
- Apply soft restraints and then assess — the agitation puts both the patient and staff at immediate risk.
- Name the question: 'What is driving this agitation?' — systematic evaluation for delirium, substance withdrawal, medication effects (akathisia, steroids), pain, hypoxia, urinary retention, and fear must precede any pharmacologic decision. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic, It's just withdrawal, and Restraint first all skip cause-finding. Agitation is a symptom. The standard is to name the driver — delirium, withdrawal, akathisia, pain, hypoxia, retention, fear — before selecting an intervention. Pharmacotherapy, if needed, then matches the cause.

**Pearl.** Agitation is always a symptom — name the driver before selecting the intervention; the treatment should match the cause.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does matching the treatment to the agitation driver matter so much in an older medical patient?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Older patients cannot metabolize antipsychotics — any dose will cause over-sedation regardless of indication."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Benzodiazepines are preferred over antipsychotics in older adults when a calming agent is needed — their rapid onset and lower risk of EPS make them the safer pharmacologic choice after non-pharmacologic measures fail.", "trap": {"name": "Benzodiazepines preferred in older adults", "note": "Benzodiazepines are appropriate for alcohol or sedative withdrawal, but deepen most other causes of delirium in older adults — using them when the driver is unknown or medical risks worsening the underlying cause, not treating it."}}, {"key": "C", "t": "The wrong treatment can worsen the driver: benzodiazepines deepen most delirium; antipsychotics can precipitate NMS in unexcluded catatonia; treating withdrawal incorrectly can cause seizures — mismatching agent to cause produces iatrogenic harm.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "Pharmacotherapy is inappropriate in all older patients — verbal de-escalation is the only evidence-based approach regardless of driver."}], "why": "It is a drug–driver matching principle. Benzodiazepines are correct for alcohol/sedative withdrawal and dangerous in most other delirium. Antipsychotics are appropriate for dangerous agitation in non-catatonic, non-LBD states and dangerous if catatonia or LBD is present. Analgesics resolve pain-driven agitation that no psychotropic would touch. The wrong drug in the wrong patient makes the agitation worse — the paradigm case being benzodiazepines deepening delirium in a delirious elder."}

**Stem.** A patient with depression and passive suicidal ideation is being discharged. Which single intervention has the strongest evidence for reducing suicide death?

- Means restriction — counseling to remove or secure firearms and lethal medications **← keyed correct**
- Having the patient sign a no-suicide contract
- Scheduling routine follow-up in three months
- Prescribing a large supply of a sedative for sleep

**Rationale.** Means restriction — especially securing or removing firearms and limiting access to lethal medication — is the most evidence-based single intervention to reduce suicide death. No-suicide contracts are not protective.

**Pearl.** Means restriction (firearms above all) is the highest-yield suicide-prevention step; contracts are not protective.

**Stem.** During which period is a psychiatric patient's suicide risk generally highest?

- The middle of a long stable outpatient course
- The immediate post-discharge period after a psychiatric hospitalization **← keyed correct**
- During the acute inpatient stay
- Suicide risk is constant and does not vary over time

**Rationale.** The peri-discharge and especially the immediate post-discharge period is among the highest-risk windows. Discharge planning — follow-up, safety planning, means restriction — is a safety intervention, not paperwork.

**Pearl.** Highest-risk window is right after discharge — treat the transition as the intervention.

**Stem.** A patient on an SSRI is started on tramadol and develops agitation, diaphoresis, tremor, and lower-extremity clonus with hyperreflexia. What is the diagnosis, and the key bedside discriminator from NMS?

- Neuroleptic malignant syndrome — lead-pipe rigidity
- Anticholinergic toxicity — dry flushed skin
- Serotonin syndrome — clonus and hyperreflexia **← keyed correct**
- Malignant hyperthermia — anesthetic trigger

**Rationale.** A serotonergic combination (SSRI plus tramadol) with agitation, diaphoresis, tremor, lower-extremity clonus, and hyperreflexia is serotonin syndrome. The bedside discriminator from NMS is the neuromuscular exam: clonus/hyperreflexia in serotonin syndrome vs lead-pipe rigidity with lower reflexes in NMS.

**Pearl.** Clonus and hyperreflexia after a serotonergic change = serotonin syndrome; NMS is lead-pipe rigidity after dopamine blockade.

**Stem.** You are assessing a patient's risk of violence on the unit. Which is the single strongest predictor of future violence?

- A specific psychiatric diagnosis alone
- The patient's stated remorse
- Male sex by itself
- A history of past violence **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Past violence is the strongest single predictor of future violence. Distinguish static factors (history — fixed) from dynamic factors (acute psychosis, intoxication, agitation — modifiable and the treatment target).

**Pearl.** Past violence is the best predictor; separate static (history) from dynamic (treatable) risk.

**Stem.** A 34-year-old man admitted last night for opioid use disorder asks to start buprenorphine right away — 'I don't want to get sick.' His last heroin use was about 6 hours ago. He is anxious but has no yawning, rhinorrhea, mydriasis, or piloerection; his COWS score is 4. Best next step?

- Start buprenorphine now, before the withdrawal becomes severe
- Continue serial COWS scoring and start buprenorphine once objective withdrawal is present (COWS roughly 8 to 12) **← keyed correct**
- Give a benzodiazepine to control the withdrawal symptoms
- Reassure him that opioid withdrawal is not dangerous and defer any medication

**Rationale.** Comfort-first induction is the trap the request invites — buprenorphine started before objective withdrawal precipitates the withdrawal it was meant to prevent. Serial COWS scores make the timing objective: induction begins at roughly 8 to 12, not at the patient's anxiety. The benzodiazepine belongs to alcohol and sedative withdrawal, and 'not dangerous' is not a plan when MOUD linkage is the discharge intervention.

**Pearl.** Do not start buprenorphine until objective withdrawal is on board (COWS roughly ≥ 8 to 12) or you may precipitate withdrawal.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why must buprenorphine wait for objective withdrawal?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Buprenorphine is ineffective until the urine drug screen has cleared"}, {"key": "B", "t": "Early dosing risks oversedation and respiratory arrest from stacking agonists"}, {"key": "C", "t": "As a high-affinity partial agonist, buprenorphine displaces residual full agonist from the receptor — started too early, it precipitates withdrawal", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "Waiting builds the patient's motivation to engage with treatment"}], "why": "It is a receptor-displacement problem, not a clearance, sedation, or motivation problem — the partial agonist evicts the remaining full agonist and the net signal drops abruptly. Making the patient 'earn' the dose has no place in a non-judgmental, brain-disease framing of addiction."}

**Stem.** A 52-year-old man with a history of heavy daily alcohol use is admitted in early withdrawal — tremulous and tachycardic. A nurse is about to start an IV dextrose infusion for a fingerstick glucose in the low-normal range. The most important step before administering the glucose is which of the following?

- Obtain a CIWA-Ar score to guide benzodiazepine dosing before starting any IV fluids
- Confirm his blood alcohol level has cleared to zero before giving glucose-containing fluids
- Administer thiamine before or with the glucose to prevent Wernicke encephalopathy **← keyed correct**
- Reassure the team that Wernicke encephalopathy risk applies only to patients with a documented prior episode

**Rationale.** Sequence error and It's just withdrawal are the traps. Administering carbohydrate to a thiamine-depleted patient precipitates Wernicke encephalopathy — a preventable neurologic emergency. The rule is simple and categorical: thiamine before glucose, every time, without requiring a prior history of encephalopathy.

**Pearl.** Thiamine before glucose, every time — carbohydrate loading in a thiamine-depleted patient can precipitate Wernicke encephalopathy.

**Stem.** A 57-year-old man with severe alcohol use disorder is admitted through the ED with altered mental status. The nurse establishes IV access and is preparing to infuse D5W. He appears malnourished and confused. Which step must occur before the glucose infusion is started?

- Start a CIWA-Ar protocol — the altered mental status indicates active alcohol withdrawal requiring immediate benzodiazepine dosing.
- Obtain a CT head to exclude intracranial pathology before administering any fluids.
- Administer IV thiamine before any glucose-containing fluids — carbohydrate loading in a thiamine-depleted patient can precipitate Wernicke encephalopathy. **← keyed correct**
- Confirm blood alcohol level — management depends on whether the AMS is from intoxication or withdrawal.

**Rationale.** It's just withdrawal and Imaging before treatment both bypass the irreversible intervention: thiamine before glucose. Wernicke encephalopathy — confusion, ophthalmoplegia, ataxia — is precipitated when carbohydrate is infused into a thiamine-depleted nervous system. The intervention costs almost nothing and the omission is catastrophic. Labs before treatment similarly delays a maneuver that should be reflexive.

**Pearl.** Thiamine before glucose — every time, before any IV dextrose in a malnourished or alcohol-dependent patient.

**Stem.** A 46-year-old woman is admitted for alcohol detoxification. Her CIWA-Ar score is 16 on admission. A nurse asks how benzodiazepines should be dosed for alcohol withdrawal management on this unit. Which approach is correct?

- Symptom-triggered dosing guided by serial CIWA-Ar scores — administer benzodiazepines when the score crosses a threshold and escalate monitoring for seizure or delirium tremens risk. **← keyed correct**
- A fixed-schedule taper starting at maximum dose for 5 days regardless of symptoms — scheduled dosing prevents breakthrough withdrawal.
- Administer benzodiazepines only if a seizure occurs — prophylactic dosing is not supported by evidence.
- Use a non-benzodiazepine anticonvulsant such as phenobarbital as first-line — benzodiazepines are contraindicated in alcohol withdrawal due to cross-tolerance.

**Rationale.** Scheduled over symptom-triggered, Underestimate the emergency, and Contraindication confusion are all active. Symptom-triggered CIWA-Ar–guided benzodiazepine dosing is the standard for alcohol withdrawal — it provides treatment when needed, avoids over-sedation, and maintains vigilance for the lethal end of the spectrum (seizures, delirium tremens).

**Pearl.** Alcohol withdrawal: CIWA-Ar–driven, symptom-triggered benzodiazepines — escalate monitoring for seizure and DT risk.

**Stem.** A 28-year-old man with opioid use disorder requests buprenorphine. He last used heroin 5 hours ago and reports mild cramping but looks comfortable. His COWS score is 5. He says, 'I'm ready to start — please give it now.' What is the most appropriate response?

- Start buprenorphine now — early induction prevents relapse and meeting the patient's readiness is clinically more important than withdrawal scoring.
- Wait until objective withdrawal is established (COWS roughly ≥8–12) before initiating buprenorphine induction — this is required to avoid precipitating withdrawal. **← keyed correct**
- Administer a test dose of buprenorphine 0.5 mg and observe for 30 minutes — a small test dose avoids precipitated withdrawal.
- Refuse buprenorphine and offer clonidine instead — clonidine is safer in the early withdrawal window.

**Rationale.** Comfort-first induction in two forms is the central trap. Buprenorphine is a partial agonist with high receptor affinity — it displaces full agonists. If taken before sufficient withdrawal, it precipitates sudden, severe withdrawal. The COWS threshold (≥8–12) ensures residual full agonist has cleared enough receptors that induction is safe.

**Pearl.** COWS ≥8–12 before buprenorphine induction — the scoring threshold prevents precipitated withdrawal, not patient readiness.

**Stem.** A 34-year-old woman with cocaine use disorder presents with elevated mood, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, and grandiosity beginning 2 days after she used cocaine heavily. The team wants to document a diagnosis of bipolar I disorder. Which statement best characterizes the diagnostic approach?

- Bipolar I disorder should be diagnosed — the clinical picture is classic and the cocaine use is a separate comorbidity.
- Stimulant-induced mood disorder is the only possible diagnosis — patients with SUD cannot be diagnosed with primary bipolar disorder.
- The decreased sleep alone confirms a manic episode distinct from stimulant effects.
- A primary psychiatric diagnosis cannot be reliably established while the patient is within the acute intoxication or withdrawal window — stabilize medically, allow clearance, then reassess. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Diagnose through intoxication and SUD excludes comorbidity are the two opposing errors. The standard is clear: co-occurring psychiatric illness is the rule in SUD, but you cannot reliably diagnose a primary mood or psychotic disorder while the patient is acutely intoxicated or withdrawing — the symptoms are indistinguishable. Stabilize first, then reassess.

**Pearl.** No primary psychiatric diagnosis while acutely intoxicated or withdrawing — stabilize first, then reassess.

**Stem.** A 61-year-old man with severe alcohol use disorder is admitted for detoxification. On day 2, a nurse pages: he is increasingly confused, stumbling when ambulated, and his eyes appear to be 'moving strangely.' He was ambulatory on admission. Which diagnosis must be excluded immediately?

- Delirium tremens — fluctuating confusion and ataxia are expected in severe alcohol withdrawal and represent the feared end of the spectrum.
- Wernicke encephalopathy — the triad of confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia constitutes Wernicke, which occurs in incomplete forms and requires immediate IV thiamine regardless of completeness. **← keyed correct**
- Alcohol-related cerebellar degeneration — chronic ataxia is expected in severe alcohol use and does not require acute intervention.
- Hepatic encephalopathy — asterixis and confusion in a patient with alcohol use disorder indicate liver failure.

**Rationale.** It's just withdrawal in two forms. The Wernicke triad — confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia — is classically taught as a complete triad but occurs frequently in incomplete forms. The threshold for treatment is low because thiamine is cheap and Wernicke untreated progresses to Korsakoff syndrome, which is irreversible.

**Pearl.** Wernicke encephalopathy: confusion + ataxia + ophthalmoplegia — often incomplete; treat with IV thiamine immediately without waiting for the complete triad.

**Stem.** A 31-year-old man with opioid use disorder is being discharged after a brief admission for opioid intoxication. He declines to start buprenorphine during the admission. Which discharge intervention has the strongest direct evidence for preventing death in the weeks following discharge?

- Refer to a residential rehabilitation program — structured environments eliminate the post-discharge overdose window.
- Prescribe clonidine for autonomic withdrawal symptoms — addressing physiological discomfort reduces relapse.
- Provide naloxone with overdose-prevention education and arrange linkage to medication for opioid use disorder (MOUD) before discharge. **← keyed correct**
- Schedule a routine outpatient psychiatric follow-up in 4–6 weeks — continuity of care addresses the root causes of relapse.

**Rationale.** Referral without bridge, Non-MOUD for OUD, and Routine follow-up adequate all fail to address the acute post-discharge window. Tolerance drops during admission; returning to a pre-admission dose now carries high overdose risk. Naloxone plus MOUD linkage before discharge is the concrete, evidence-based life-saving intervention named on the page.

**Pearl.** Naloxone + MOUD linkage at discharge for every patient with opioid use disorder — the post-discharge window is the highest-risk period.

**Stem.** A 52-year-old woman completes medically supervised alcohol detoxification and wants pharmacotherapy to support sobriety. She has normal renal and hepatic function and no contraindications. Which agents are FDA-approved, evidence-based first-line options for alcohol use disorder maintenance?

- Naltrexone or acamprosate — both are FDA-approved pharmacotherapies with evidence for reducing relapse and supporting sobriety in alcohol use disorder. **← keyed correct**
- A long-term benzodiazepine — GABAergic agents reduce alcohol craving through the same receptor mechanism as alcohol.
- An SSRI — the anxiety and dysphoria underlying alcohol use are the primary drivers, and antidepressants address them.
- Valproate — mood stabilization reduces the emotional dysregulation and impulsivity that drive relapse.

**Rationale.** The reflexive benzodiazepine, Treat the symptom not the disorder, and Off-label equals evidence-based all miss the two agents the page explicitly names. Naltrexone (opioid antagonist — reduces reward) and acamprosate (GABA/glutamate modulation — reduces craving) are the established pharmacotherapies for maintaining sobriety in alcohol use disorder.

**Pearl.** AUD maintenance pharmacotherapy: naltrexone or acamprosate — not benzodiazepines, not SSRIs alone.

**Stem.** A patient with opioid use disorder says: 'I'm not an addict. I can stop whenever I want — it hasn't caused me any real problems.' You are the student conducting the initial interview using a motivational interviewing approach. Which response is best?

- "That's not accurate — research shows that opioid use changes your brain's reward system in ways that make 'just stopping' very difficult. Let me explain what the evidence says."
- "I hear you saying it hasn't caused problems, but the data is clear — untreated opioid use disorder has a high mortality rate, and I want you to understand that risk."
- "I understand you feel that way. Before we go further, I need you to complete this substance use disorder questionnaire for our documentation."
- "It sounds like you feel like you have it handled. I'm curious — has there been any time when using has gotten in the way of something that mattered to you?" **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** The righting reflex and Quoting statistics at patients are the two forms of pushing for change that motivational interviewing specifically avoids — they produce resistance. Process over person abandons the conversation entirely. The correct response reflects the patient's frame, rolls with resistance, and opens a door with curiosity — the Volkow brain-disease model frames addiction as a brain condition requiring engagement rather than moral correction.

**Pearl.** MI meets ambivalence with curiosity, not confrontation — reflect, don't argue; the righting reflex produces sustain talk.

**Stem.** A 19-year-old man is admitted for heroin withdrawal. He is visibly distressed, diaphoretic, tachycardic, and requesting immediate medication. A colleague asks, 'Is this patient in danger?' Which response most accurately conveys the clinical urgency?

- Yes, severely — opioid withdrawal can progress to respiratory depression and circulatory collapse.
- Minimal — opioid withdrawal is uncomfortable but medically benign; supportive care and reassurance are sufficient without formal pharmacotherapy.
- Opioid withdrawal is rarely fatal, but leaving it untreated risks AMA discharge into the highest overdose-risk window — the treatment imperative is engaging the patient in MOUD, not the withdrawal severity alone. **← keyed correct**
- Yes — untreated opioid withdrawal routinely progresses to seizures and delirium, creating the same risk as alcohol withdrawal.

**Rationale.** Not lethal = not treatable in two forms frame this item. Opioid withdrawal itself is rarely lethal — but dismissing it as benign is a clinical error because untreated withdrawal drives AMA discharge at the moment when tolerance is lowest and overdose risk is highest. The urgency is engagement in MOUD, not hemodynamic management.

**Pearl.** Opioid withdrawal: rarely fatal but drives AMA discharge into the highest-overdose-risk window — the treatment priority is MOUD engagement, not the withdrawal itself.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does the post-discharge period carry elevated overdose risk after an opioid withdrawal episode?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Opioid withdrawal produces a lasting sensitization that amplifies the effect of future doses for weeks."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Withdrawal depletes endogenous endorphins, causing rebound pain that requires higher subsequent doses."}, {"key": "C", "t": "Tolerance decreases substantially during withdrawal — the dose used before admission now exceeds the lethal threshold for a less-tolerant brain.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "Post-discharge overdose risk is from co-ingestant adulterants (e.g., fentanyl), not from tolerance changes."}], "why": "Tolerance is not a fixed property — it decreases during a period of abstinence or withdrawal. **Stem.** A 56-year-old man with alcohol use disorder is brought in by EMS after being found unresponsive. Blood alcohol level is 0.22. He remains obtunded 45 minutes after arrival despite being stable. A colleague says, 'He's just drunk — let's monitor and wait.' Which response is most accurate?

- Agree — a BAC of 0.22 fully accounts for the clinical picture in a patient with alcohol use disorder.
- Disagree — obtundation in a patient who uses alcohol requires workup for Wernicke encephalopathy, subdural hematoma, infection, hepatic encephalopathy, and co-ingestants before attributing to alcohol alone. **← keyed correct**
- Agree — wait for the alcohol to metabolize, but plan to obtain a CT head if not improved in 4 hours.
- Disagree — the next step is naloxone administration to reverse any opioid co-ingestion before other workup is done.

**Rationale.** It's just intoxication in two forms: accepting alcohol as the only explanation and deferring to a 4-hour reassessment both delay a workup that could catch a treatable emergency. The page explicitly states that altered mental status in a patient who uses substances is never automatically 'just withdrawal' — the same principle applies to intoxication. The differential includes Wernicke, subdural, infection, hepatic encephalopathy, and polysubstance use.

**Pearl.** AMS + alcohol use disorder = real differential; BAC does not close the workup — Wernicke, subdural, infection, and hepatic encephalopathy must be actively excluded.

`J`, ` `, `M`, `e`, `d`, ` `, `1`, `9`, `9`, `8`, `)`, `.`

**Stem.** A 64-year-old man with severe alcohol use disorder and documented Child-Pugh B cirrhosis is admitted for alcohol withdrawal. CIWA-Ar is 14. The team considers using chlordiazepoxide for symptom-triggered dosing. A student identifies a safety concern. Which response is most accurate?

- In significant hepatic impairment, long-acting benzodiazepines (chlordiazepoxide, diazepam) accumulate due to impaired oxidative metabolism — lorazepam or oxazepam are preferred because they undergo direct glucuronidation, which is preserved in hepatic disease. **← keyed correct**
- Benzodiazepines are contraindicated in alcohol withdrawal for patients with cirrhosis — use phenobarbital or an antipsychotic instead.
- Chlordiazepoxide is preferred in liver disease — its long half-life provides smoother coverage than short-acting agents and prevents rebound.
- All benzodiazepines are equally affected by hepatic dysfunction — no single agent is preferred over another in cirrhosis.

**Rationale.** Contraindication confusion and Long half-life equals smooth coverage are the two active errors. The clinical principle: lorazepam and oxazepam are preferred in hepatic impairment precisely because their glucuronidation pathway is preserved while oxidative metabolism (which processes chlordiazepoxide and diazepam) is impaired in cirrhosis. All drugs equal ignores a clinically important pharmacokinetic distinction.

**Pearl.** Hepatic impairment + alcohol withdrawal: use lorazepam or oxazepam (glucuronidation preserved); avoid long-acting benzodiazepines that accumulate.

**Stem.** A 49-year-old man with severe alcohol use disorder is admitted after a tonic-clonic seizure at home. He has a documented prior alcohol withdrawal seizure from two years ago. CIWA-Ar on arrival is 12. Which statement best characterizes his risk and clinical priority?

- The seizure occurred before admission; since he is now calmer, observation without immediate pharmacologic intervention is appropriate.
- The seizure risk has passed — withdrawal seizures occur in the first 24–48 hours; further seizures are not expected after a first event.
- Start a fixed-schedule antipsychotic — antipsychotics lower seizure threshold and provide targeted coverage for alcohol withdrawal psychosis.
- Prior withdrawal seizure is a strong predictor of future seizures; this patient requires aggressive CIWA-Ar-guided benzodiazepine titration with close monitoring for progression to delirium tremens. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Underestimate the emergency in two forms, and Agitation = antipsychotic. Prior withdrawal seizure is explicitly the most robust predictor of future seizure risk. Antipsychotics lower seizure threshold and are contraindicated for seizure prevention in alcohol withdrawal. CIWA-Ar-guided benzodiazepines are the treatment, and DT vigilance is the priority.

**Pearl.** Prior withdrawal seizure = highest predictor of future seizures; aggressive CIWA-Ar-guided benzodiazepines + DT monitoring is the standard.

**Tier-2 / stretch.** {"q": "Why does abrupt alcohol cessation cause seizures, whereas opioid withdrawal does not?", "options": [{"key": "A", "t": "Alcohol directly damages neurons, and the damaged cells become epileptogenic on cessation."}, {"key": "B", "t": "Opioid withdrawal does cause seizures, but at a lower incidence than alcohol withdrawal."}, {"key": "C", "t": "Alcohol downregulates GABA-A receptors with chronic use — abrupt cessation uncovers an unopposed glutamatergic hyperexcitable state, removing tonic GABAergic inhibition and generating the kindling-based seizure risk.", "c": true}, {"key": "D", "t": "The seizure risk is from concurrent electrolyte disturbances (hypomagnesemia, hypocalcemia), not from the alcohol withdrawal itself."}], "why": "Chronic alcohol use potentiates GABA-A activity. In response, the brain compensatory downregulates GABA receptors and upregulates glutamate receptors. When alcohol is abruptly removed, the GABAergic brake is gone and the glutamatergic accelerator is overactive — the resulting hyperexcitability produces seizures and, at its worst, delirium tremens. Opioid withdrawal does not affect the seizure threshold through this mechanism — it causes autonomic distress but not neuronal hyperexcitability."}

### qb_sud_015

- id=`qb_sud_015` · status=`draft` · type=`sba` · difficulty=`2` · competency=`['dx', 'safety']`
- Linked pages: `t_sud.md`

**Stem.** A 55-year-old man with heavy alcohol use is admitted with confusion, gaze-evoked nystagmus with impaired eye movements, and a wide-based unsteady gait. What is the immediate priority?

- Give parenteral thiamine before any glucose **← keyed correct**
- Give IV dextrose immediately to correct presumed hypoglycemia
- Start a benzodiazepine taper and defer thiamine
- Obtain an MRI before any treatment

**Rationale.** The triad of encephalopathy, ophthalmoplegia, and ataxia is Wernicke encephalopathy. Give thiamine before glucose, because a glucose load in a thiamine-deficient patient can precipitate or worsen it.

**Pearl.** Wernicke = confusion + ophthalmoplegia + ataxia; give thiamine BEFORE glucose.

### qb_sud_016

- id=`qb_sud_016` · status=`draft` · type=`sba` · difficulty=`2` · competency=`['pharm', 'management']`
- Linked pages: `t_sud.md`
- Evidence: `t`, `_`, `s`, `u`, `d`, `.`, `m`, `d`, ` `, `—`, ` `, `A`, `l`, `c`, `o`, `h`, `o`, `l`, ` `, `u`, `s`, `e`, ` `, `d`, `i`, `s`, `o`, `r`, `d`, `e`, `r`, ` `, `p`, `h`, `a`, `r`, `m`, `a`, `c`, `o`, `t`, `h`, `e`, `r`, `a`, `p`, `y`, `.`

**Stem.** A patient with alcohol use disorder is medically stable and motivated to cut down, with compensated cirrhosis. Which maintenance pharmacotherapy is a reasonable first-line choice?

- Naltrexone (if no contraindication) or acamprosate — both are first-line for alcohol use disorder **← keyed correct**
- A scheduled long-term benzodiazepine
- Disulfiram as the automatic first choice for everyone
- No pharmacotherapy is effective for alcohol use disorder

**Rationale.** Naltrexone (reduces heavy drinking; avoid in acute hepatitis/opioid use) and acamprosate (supports abstinence; usable in liver disease, adjust in renal impairment) are first-line for AUD. Disulfiram is second-line and adherence-dependent.

**Pearl.** AUD maintenance: naltrexone or acamprosate first-line; disulfiram is adherence-dependent second-line.

## Pre-test pool (12 items — the diagnostic learners see first)

### qb_mood_004  · category `mood`

**Stem.** A 38-year-old man presents for admission with a current depressive episode. He reports no prior manic episodes. His wife, who accompanied him, states that 3 years ago he 'went weeks without sleeping, gave away his car, and was fired for erratic behavior.' He does not recall this period as abnormal. Which diagnostic move has the highest yield in clarifying the bipolar question?

- Patient self-report is the most reliable — the wife's account may be distorted by relationship conflict.
- Obtain collateral history from the wife and prior clinicians — collateral is the single highest-yield diagnostic move for distinguishing bipolar from unipolar disorder. **← keyed correct**
- Administer a structured screening tool (Mood Disorder Questionnaire) — it replaces the need for collateral history.
- Only documented hospitalizations for mania count as evidence — behavioral reports from family members do not meet the diagnostic threshold.

**Rationale.** Self-report over collateral and Protocol over clinical judgment both bypass the highest-yield step. The mood page explicitly calls collateral 'the single highest-yield diagnostic move for distinguishing bipolar from unipolar.' This patient's lack of insight into a probable manic episode is classic — the wife's account is the clinical key.

**Pearl.** Collateral history is the single highest-yield diagnostic move for bipolar vs unipolar — patients with past mania often lack insight into the episode.

### qb_psy_008  · category `psychosis`

**Stem.** A 32-year-old woman with schizophrenia started haloperidol 5 days ago. She is now febrile to 39.9°C, diaphoretic, and rigid. Nursing staff describe her muscle tone as 'like a pipe.' Her CK is 5,400. What is the most important immediate action?

- Stop the antipsychotic immediately, transfer to a medical unit for supportive care, and monitor vital signs and CK serially — this is neuroleptic malignant syndrome. **← keyed correct**
- Increase the antipsychotic dose — the psychotic agitation underlying the NMS-like symptoms requires more aggressive dopamine blockade.
- Start empiric antibiotics — fever and rigidity after medication initiation represent a superimposed infection in an immunocompromised patient.
- Administer a benzodiazepine for the agitation and recheck vitals in 4 hours before escalating.

**Rationale.** Agitation = antipsychotic, Fever = infection, and Treat and wait all delay the single most important intervention: stopping the antipsychotic. NMS is the can't-miss antipsychotic emergency — fever + lead-pipe rigidity + elevated CK after dopamine blocker initiation requires immediate drug cessation and medical-level care.

**Pearl.** NMS: fever + lead-pipe rigidity + elevated CK after antipsychotic → stop the antipsychotic immediately and transfer to medical care.

### qb_anx_004  · category `anxiety`

**Stem.** A 52-year-old man is admitted to the inpatient psychiatry unit. He is anxious, diaphoretic, and tremulous. His heart rate is 102. He says he has always been 'an anxious person.' The team prepares to increase his home sertraline. Which step is most important before attributing this presentation to his anxiety disorder?

- Obtain a psychiatric consultation — his elevated heart rate may indicate a panic attack requiring specialist evaluation.
- Screen for medical and withdrawal causes first — diaphoresis, tachycardia, and tremor require workup for hyperthyroidism, withdrawal states, arrhythmia, and hypoglycemia before attributing symptoms to primary anxiety. **← keyed correct**
- Increase the sertraline — the anxiety disorder history provides the most parsimonious explanation.
- Obtain an ECG and troponin immediately — the anxiety symptoms likely represent cardiac ischemia.

**Rationale.** Specialist before differential, Anchoring to prior diagnosis, and Single diagnosis before differential all bypass the foundational step. The page's first pearl: 'Anxiety is a symptom before it is a diagnosis — clear the medical and withdrawal mimics first.' In a newly admitted patient with diaphoresis, tremor, and tachycardia, alcohol or sedative withdrawal must top the differential.

**Pearl.** Anxiety is a symptom before it is a diagnosis — clear medical and withdrawal mimics (thyroid, withdrawal, arrhythmia, hypoglycemia) before treating the disorder.

### qb_sud_001  · category `substance`

**Stem.** A 34-year-old man admitted last night for opioid use disorder asks to start buprenorphine right away — 'I don't want to get sick.' His last heroin use was about 6 hours ago. He is anxious but has no yawning, rhinorrhea, mydriasis, or piloerection; his COWS score is 4. Best next step?

- Start buprenorphine now, before the withdrawal becomes severe
- Continue serial COWS scoring and start buprenorphine once objective withdrawal is present (COWS roughly 8 to 12) **← keyed correct**
- Give a benzodiazepine to control the withdrawal symptoms
- Reassure him that opioid withdrawal is not dangerous and defer any medication

**Rationale.** Comfort-first induction is the trap the request invites — buprenorphine started before objective withdrawal precipitates the withdrawal it was meant to prevent. Serial COWS scores make the timing objective: induction begins at roughly 8 to 12, not at the patient's anxiety. The benzodiazepine belongs to alcohol and sedative withdrawal, and 'not dangerous' is not a plan when MOUD linkage is the discharge intervention.

**Pearl.** Do not start buprenorphine until objective withdrawal is on board (COWS roughly ≥ 8 to 12) or you may precipitate withdrawal.

### qb_cog_011  · category `neurocog`

**Stem.** A 58-year-old man is brought to the emergency department by his wife, who reports an 18-month history of increasingly disinhibited behavior — making inappropriate sexual comments to strangers, hoarding food, and neglecting hygiene. He has no memory complaints, names and recognizes objects normally, and denies mood symptoms. Neuropsychological testing shows prominent executive dysfunction with intact episodic memory. Most likely diagnosis?

- Bipolar disorder — new-onset mania presents with disinhibition, impulsivity, and executive dysfunction in midlife.
- Behavioral-variant frontotemporal dementia — early disinhibition, socially inappropriate behavior, and executive dysfunction with relative sparing of episodic memory in a younger adult is the classic bvFTD presentation. **← keyed correct**
- Early-onset Alzheimer disease — insidious behavioral change in a 58-year-old is most commonly early-onset AD.
- Personality disorder — disinhibition, impulsivity, and boundary violations reflect a longstanding personality pattern rather than a new neurological process.

**Rationale.** Bipolar by default, Most common = correct, and Personality disorder by default are three forms of the same error: assigning a functional psychiatric diagnosis before considering neurodegeneration. FTD is the dementia of younger adults, presenting with behavior and personality change or progressive language, while sparing episodic memory early — the exact opposite of Alzheimer.

**Pearl.** Frontotemporal dementia: younger onset, behavioral disinhibition or progressive language first, episodic memory spared early — the signature that separates it from Alzheimer.

### qb_pha_001  · category `pharm`

**Stem.** A 23-year-old man with a history of depression on high-dose sertraline is brought to the emergency department 3 hours after a second serotonergic medication was added to his regimen. Examination shows agitation, diarrhea, diaphoresis, and clonus; reflexes are brisk and symmetrically hyperactive throughout. Temperature is 38.3°C. Which of the following best explains this presentation?

- Neuroleptic malignant syndrome — high-dose SSRIs can block dopamine receptors
- Anticholinergic toxidrome — sertraline has strong anticholinergic activity
- Alcohol withdrawal — autonomic instability and agitation are hallmarks
- Serotonin syndrome — rapid onset after a second serotonergic agent was added, with hyperreflexia, clonus, and autonomic instability **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Wrong toxidrome is the common trap in two directions: NMS (requires a dopamine blocker, slower, lead-pipe rigid) and anticholinergic (dry skin, urinary retention, no clonus). It's just withdrawal misreads autonomic instability without the serotonin-specific signs. Serotonin syndrome is the answer: rapid onset after serotonergic augmentation, clonus, hyperreflexia, and autonomic instability are its distinguishing features.

**Pearl.** Serotonin syndrome: rapid onset, clonus, hyperreflexia after a serotonergic agent was added. NMS: lead-pipe rigidity, elevated CK, on a dopamine blocker — slower and stiffer.

### qb_saf_001  · category `safety`

**Stem.** A 19-year-old man is admitted after a near-lethal suicide attempt. He has expressed persistent hopelessness and a stated wish to die for three days. On hospital day 4, nurses report he seems 'much better' — calm, well-rested, and telling the team he is ready to go home. His affect is bright and he is chatty with staff. No clinical change or social development explains the shift. Best next step?

- Begin discharge planning — improvement is the goal of admission and this patient has achieved it
- Reduce his observation level and move him to a lower-acuity room to reinforce the progress
- Escalate to the attending immediately — unexplained sudden improvement after severe suicidality is a warning sign requiring reassessment **← keyed correct**
- Update the safety plan and schedule discharge within 24 hours while the patient is engaged

**Rationale.** Sudden improvement = recovery is the most dangerous trap: the pocket card explicitly lists unexplained sudden improvement as an immediate escalation trigger. Quiet patient = stable patient reduces vigilance at exactly the wrong moment. Safety plan = discharge permission slip conflates a tool with a risk decision.

**Pearl.** Sudden unexplained improvement after severe suicidality is an escalation trigger, not evidence of recovery.

### qb_per_003  · category `personality`

**Stem.** A 24-year-old woman with borderline personality disorder has had 6 psychiatric admissions in the past 2 years, each following an episode of suicidal ideation and non-suicidal self-injury after an interpersonal rupture. She describes ongoing passive suicidal ideation at baseline. Tonight she is calm, the relationship dispute that triggered this admission has somewhat resolved, and her self-harm behaviors have decreased. How should the team conceptualize the discharge planning?

- She should be admitted as long as suicidal ideation is present — admission ends only when suicidal ideation resolves completely.
- Discharge should be planned now that the acute crisis has resolved — her chronic suicidality is managed primarily through collaborative safety planning and outpatient therapy, not serial acute admissions. **← keyed correct**
- Transfer to a long-term residential program is the appropriate step — repeated admissions indicate that outpatient treatment has failed.
- Increase the frequency of psychiatric medications — the recurrent admissions indicate inadequate pharmacotherapy.

**Rationale.** Admission until SI resolves, Repeated admissions = step up, and Polypharmacy for BPD all fail to apply the chronic-vs-acute distinction. The page is explicit: 'For chronic suicidality, collaborative safety planning is often preferable to repeated or prolonged admission, which can reinforce the hospital as the primary coping strategy.' Discharge planning after acute-on-chronic resolution is the correct frame.

**Pearl.** Chronic BPD suicidality ≠ acute crisis — distinguish the baseline from escalation; safety planning + outpatient DBT, not serial admissions, manage the chronic pattern.

### qb_cdev_002  · category `childdev`

**Stem.** An 18-year-old with autism spectrum disorder is admitted for aggressive behavior. His parents ask if medication can 'help with his autism.' The team considers starting risperidone. Which statement about the pharmacological target of risperidone in ASD is most accurate?

- Risperidone is FDA-approved to improve the core social-communication deficits of autism, including eye contact, reciprocal conversation, and restricted interests.
- Risperidone is effective for all ASD-related behavioral symptoms when used at sufficient doses — including social withdrawal, repetitive behaviors, and communication deficits.
- Risperidone and aripiprazole are FDA-approved for irritability and aggression associated with autism — they do not treat the core social-communication features; target symptoms must be explicitly defined, and metabolic monitoring is required. **← keyed correct**
- Risperidone is a first-line agent for all behaviors in any patient with autism regardless of severity — it should be started prophylactically to prevent aggression.

**Rationale.** Antipsychotic treats core ASD features, Sufficient dose treats all features, and Prophylactic antipsychotic all misstate the indication. The page is specific: 'Risperidone and aripiprazole are FDA-approved for irritability/aggression associated with autism (they do NOT treat the core social-communication features), so target symptoms explicitly, monitor metabolically, and avoid reflexive standing antipsychotics.'

**Pearl.** Risperidone/aripiprazole treat autism-associated irritability/aggression (FDA-approved) — they do NOT treat core social-communication features; always document the target symptom.

### qb_oth_001  · category `otherdx`

**Stem.** A 16-year-old with severe anorexia nervosa has been restrictive for 4 months and is medically admitted with critically low weight. Before nutritional rehabilitation begins, which approach most directly reduces the risk of refeeding syndrome?

- Start full nutritional rehabilitation immediately at a standard adult caloric target to restore weight as rapidly as possible.
- Give prophylactic anxiolytics and monitor for 24 hours before introducing nutrition — anxiety increases metabolic demand in malnourished patients.
- Correct all electrolyte abnormalities to normal before introducing any nutrition — electrolyte imbalances worsen if nutrition begins before they are stable.
- Start caloric refeeding at a conservative initial target and advance slowly; administer thiamine before or with carbohydrate and check phosphate, potassium, and magnesium at least daily early in refeeding. **← keyed correct**

**Rationale.** Faster is safer, Sedation before nutrition, and Normalize first all create delay or metabolic risk. The source's 'start low, go slow' principle is the safety framework: conservative start, daily electrolyte checks, and thiamine before carbohydrate to prevent Wernicke encephalopathy.

**Pearl.** Refeeding syndrome prevention: start low, go slow, give thiamine before carbohydrate, check phosphate daily.

### qb_eth_001  · category `ethics`

**Stem.** A 45-year-old man with schizophrenia is voluntarily admitted and refuses his prescribed antipsychotic. When asked, he explains what the medication is for, articulates specific concerns about its side effects, understands the risks of not taking it, and asks whether alternatives exist. Best next step?

- Conduct a formal capacity assessment; if he has capacity, his refusal must be respected, documented, and an alternative offered **← keyed correct**
- Override the refusal — voluntarily admitted patients accept the prescribed treatment plan as a condition of admission
- Seek an emergency court order — a schizophrenia diagnosis with medication refusal requires judicial review
- Discharge him against medical advice — he is voluntary and refusing treatment

**Rationale.** Refusal = no capacity in two forms — assumed from admission type and assumed from diagnosis — is the central error. Capacity is decision-specific and clinical: the four abilities are assessed, not inferred from the admission voluntary/involuntary status or from a psychiatric diagnosis. Discharge for refusal conflates refusal with grounds for discharge. This patient's demonstrated abilities (communicating, understanding, appreciating, reasoning) point toward capacity — in which case his refusal must be honored.

**Pearl.** Capacity is decision-specific and clinical — a psychiatric diagnosis and even a refusal do not equal incapacity; assess the four abilities.

### qb_rel_001  · category `relational`

**Stem.** A family meeting is held the day before planned discharge for a 52-year-old man admitted after a suicide attempt by overdose. When the agenda reaches safety and warning signs, his wife says: 'We'll keep a close eye on him. And you don't need to worry about the guns — he would never use them, and they're put away.' The attending invites your input. Which response is best?

- 'It makes sense that everyone is exhausted and scared. Before discharge, who can confirm that the firearms — and any stockpiled medications — are actually secured, and how will we know it's done?' **← keyed correct**
- 'Firearms are involved in about half of US suicides and are lethal in 85 to 90 percent of attempts — "put away" isn't good enough.'
- 'That's reassuring — we'll document that the family will keep him safe and that the guns are secured.'
- 'Discharge can't move forward unless you agree to remove every firearm from the home today.'

**Rationale.** The best response validates first, stays inside the student role, and converts 'put away' into a concrete, verifiable step — who secures the items and how the team will know. Quoting lethality statistics at the family is the righting reflex in evidence clothing; accepting reassurance leaves means safety unconfirmed at the exact moment risk peaks; and an ultimatum makes the family responsible for treatment while overstepping the student's role.

**Pearl.** Means safety is a lethality intervention, not a formality — 'who can confirm items are secured before discharge?' is a clinical act.
